One Server, Three Languages#

Learn C, Go and Rust from scratch by building the same Redis-compatible server three times, and understand the power and the weakness of each.

Compiled bykodebot

October 2026 edition

C17 with clang 17 (notes on C23 and gcc) Go 1.27 Rust 1.99, edition 2024, tokio 1 redis-cli 8.10
Start with the preface Why these three languages Start building

15 chapters and 39 exercises. Reading time about 4 hours 25 minutes, measured by the build. Building time is yours. This copy was built on 12 October 2026.

NextPreface3 min

Preface#

You learn by building. So this book gives you one thing to build, and asks you to build it three times.

The thing is kv, a small server that speaks the Redis protocol. From chapter 4 on, the real redis-cli can talk to it. By the end it keeps keys and values in a hash table you wrote and serves many clients at once. It removes a key when its time limit runs out, survives a crash by replaying a log, and answers redis-benchmark at a respectable speed.

You build kv in C first, then in Go, then in Rust. The order is the order of history. C was made in 1972 to write an operating system. Go and Rust were both made, about forty years later, to fix the problems that forty years of C had shown. Go fixed them by making the language smaller and adding a garbage collector. Rust fixed them by making the compiler stricter. Building the same server in all three is the clearest way to feel the difference.

Who this book is for#

You wrote some C at university and have forgotten most of it. You used Go for a while and have forgotten that too. You have heard that Rust is good for systems programming and have never written any. You want to understand what each language is good at, what each one is bad at, and what the tools around each one are like. You do not want a long book.

No chapter assumes that you remember the chapter before. Each one restates the few facts it needs. Many readers read English as a second language, so every sentence holds one fact and no idiom.

How to read it#

Every teaching chapter has the same shape. A short lead says what the chapter is about. A table compares the concept across the three languages. Then you build the feature in C, in Go, and in Rust. Each build has an exercise. The exercise shows a design and partial code. You write the rest. If you are stuck, open the folded solution under the exercise. It holds the complete listing. The chapter closes with a verdict, a small scorecard of which language was strong, which was weak, and why.

Four kinds of box appear in the text. A history box says why a language is the way it is. A rough edge box warns about a mistake that many people make. A practice box names the convention professionals follow. A platforms box explains where macOS, Linux and Windows differ.

Code appears side by side on a wide screen and in tabs on a narrow one. Press 1, 2 or 3 to follow one language through the whole book, or use the Follow buttons in the sidebar. Press ⌘K to search.

The code#

Every listing in this book is taken from a file in the code/ folder of the book's repository. There is one complete copy of kv for each chapter and each language, so code/rust/ch08 is exactly what chapter 8 ends with. The same test script drives all three versions through redis-cli and compares the replies. The build of the book reads the listings from those files, so the text and the code cannot disagree.

The book targets C17 with clang 17 (notes on C23 and gcc), Go 1.27 and Rust 1.99, edition 2024, tokio 1, with redis-cli 8.10 as the client. Version-sensitive facts were verified from 12 October 2026 onwards. The edition is October 2026.

This copy was built on 12 October 2026. Reading time is about 4 hours 25 minutes, measured by the build. The time you spend building is yours, and it is the point.

Next · Origins01Fifty years, three answers12 min

Fifty years, three answers#

Origins · Chapter 01· 12 min read

C was made in 1972 to write an operating system. Go and Rust were made about forty years later to fix what forty years of C had shown. Go made the language smaller and added a garbage collector. Rust made the compiler stricter. This chapter tells the three stories, because every design choice in this book has a reason in them.

One problem, three answers#

Every program in this book does the same job. It takes bytes from a network socket, keeps some of them in memory, and sends bytes back. That is systems programming. The hardware has not changed its nature since 1972. Memory is still a flat run of bytes. A socket is still a stream. What changed is how much a mistake costs.

The timeline below is the shape of the whole chapter. Read it once now and again at the end.

1969 to 1973: why C#

In 1969, Ken Thompson wrote the first Unix on a DEC PDP-7 at Bell Labs. The machine had little memory and almost no software. To write programs for it, Thompson made a small language called B. B was a cut-down version of BCPL, a language that Martin Richards had designed in the mid-1960s. Dennis Ritchie later wrote that "B can be thought of as C without types".

B had one type. Every value was a machine word. On the PDP-7 that was fine, because the machine addressed memory in words. In 1970 the group got a PDP-11. The PDP-11 addressed memory in bytes. It also had no floating-point hardware at first. A language with one word-sized type did not fit a machine that worked in bytes. B was also slow, because its compiler produced code that an interpreter ran, not machine code.

So in 1971 Ritchie added a character type to B and made it produce PDP-11 machine code. During 1971 to 1973 he added more types and then structures. The result was C. In the summer of 1973 the Unix kernel was rewritten in C. An attempt in 1972 had failed. The attempt in 1973 succeeded because C now had structures, which the kernel needed to describe its own tables.

📜Why C has pointers and arrays the way it does

Ritchie called the rule that turns an array into a pointer "the crucial jump in the evolutionary chain between typeless BCPL and typed C". An array name in C is the address of its first element. That rule is why buf[3] and *(buf + 3) mean the same thing. It is also why C cannot check an array index at run time. The length is not part of the value.

Unix spread, and C spread with it. Brian Kernighan and Ritchie published The C Programming Language in 1978. In the same years the language was made portable. A compiler that could produce code for more than one machine was described in 1978, and Unix was moved to the Interdata 8/32. From then on, C was the language that operating systems were written in. In 1983 an ANSI committee began to write a standard. It finished at the end of 1989, and ISO adopted it in 1990. Newer standards followed in 1999, 2011, 2018 and 2024. The 2018 standard is called C17 and is the one this book uses. The 2024 standard is called C23.

In 1995, when it began the next revision, the committee wrote down what it wanted to keep. It called this the spirit of C, and listed it as five short rules. The first was "Trust the programmer". The second was "Don't prevent the programmer from doing what needs to be done". The fifth was "Make it fast, even if it is not guaranteed to be portable". Those rules explain almost everything about C that surprises a reader coming from another language. C does not check an array index. C does not know when to free memory. C lets you add one to a pointer and read whatever is there. The programmer is trusted to be right.

Ritchie's own summary of the language was "quirky, flawed, and an enormous success". Every word of that is true.

What forty years of C cost#

Trusting the programmer worked when one careful person wrote a kernel. It worked less well when thousands of people wrote browsers, and strangers on the internet sent those browsers input.

In 2019 Microsoft reported that about 70 percent of the vulnerabilities it reported each year were memory safety bugs. The Chromium project reported the same figure for its own code. Around 70 percent of its serious security bugs, in a sample of 912 high and critical bugs since 2015, were memory safety problems. A memory safety bug is a read or write that the program should not have made. An index past the end of an array is one. Using memory after it was freed is another. C allows both, because of the rules above.

Memory was one cost. Build time was another. By 2007, Rob Pike wrote, a large C++ binary at Google took 45 minutes to build. The C++ language inherited the C way of sharing declarations, which is to copy a header file into every file that includes it. In one measurement, 4.2 megabytes of source expanded to more than 8 gigabytes of compiler input. That is about 2,000 bytes of work for every byte a programmer wrote. The problem was old. In 1984 a Unix build was found to include the same header file 37 times for one program.

The third cost was concurrency. By 2007 a computer had several processors, and a server had to use all of them. C gives you threads and locks and nothing else. A mistake with a lock is a data race, and a data race in C is undefined behaviour. The program may do anything.

The C committee has said this itself. The charter for the 2024 standard states that "Trust the programmer, as a goal, is outdated in respect to the security and safety programming communities". The committee kept the goal. A sixth rule, added for the 2011 standard, says "Make support for safety and security demonstrable". Two other groups of people did not wait for the committee. They made new languages.

2007 to 2012: why Go#

On 21 September 2007, Robert Griesemer, Rob Pike and Ken Thompson started to sketch a new language on a whiteboard at Google. Thompson had made B in 1969. Now he was in the room again, 38 years later, to replace what B had become. The Go FAQ says the language "was born out of frustration with existing languages and environments". It names the frustrations. Large programs were too complex to build. Multiprocessor computers were "becoming universal but most languages offered little help to program them". And a programmer had to "choose either efficient compilation, efficient execution, or ease of programming". Pike's own account says the idea came during one of the 45 minute builds.

The answers in Go are all answers to those frustrations. Each one is a thing C does not do.

  • A garbage collector. The FAQ says the lifetime of objects is "one of the biggest sources of bookkeeping in systems programs". Go frees memory for you. The collector pauses the program, but by now the pauses are "often to the sub-millisecond range".
  • Goroutines and channels. The concurrency comes from the idea of communicating sequential processes, which Tony Hoare described in 1978. Go took it through two earlier languages, Newsqueak and Limbo. A goroutine is cheap, and a channel sends a value from one to another.
  • Fast builds. A Go file names only the packages it imports, and never copies in a header. An import that is not used is a compile error. So the compiler reads each dependency once, and the build of a large program takes seconds.
  • No exceptions. The FAQ says the try-catch-finally idiom "results in convoluted code". A Go function returns its error as a second value, and the caller checks it. Go keeps panic for the cases that are truly exceptional.
  • One format. The tool gofmt decides how code looks. Pike wrote that this removed "an entire class of argument between programmers".

The syntax came from C. The packages and declarations came from the Pascal, Modula and Oberon family, which Griesemer knew from Niklaus Wirth. Go became an open source project on 10 November 2009. Go 1 was released on 28 March 2012 with a promise. A program written for Go 1 "will continue to compile and run correctly, unchanged, over the lifetime of that specification". The promise still holds. The version in this book is Go 1.27, from August 2026.

📜Why Go looks plain

Go was designed for a company with a very large code base and a very large number of programmers. A language that is small is a language that everyone on the team reads the same way. That is why Go has no classes, no generics until 2022, and one loop keyword. The plainness is the point. When Go feels limited in this book, remember who it was built for.

2006 to 2015: why Rust#

Rust began in 2006 as a personal project of Graydon Hoare, an engineer at Mozilla. He worked on it alone for three years. From late 2009 Mozilla paid him to work on it. In 2010 he presented it to the company. He hoped it would be "slightly less annoying" than the languages Mozilla used. The two aims he gave were better memory safety and more concurrency. Mozilla wrote a web browser in C++, and a browser is the program that the 70 percent figure above describes. Every memory bug in it is a hole that a web page can reach.

Go answered the memory problem with a garbage collector. Mozilla did not want its engine to pause for a collector, or to pay for one in memory. So Rust needed a different answer. It found one in the compiler. Every value in Rust has exactly one owner. When the owner goes out of scope, the value is freed. A value can be lent to another part of the program, but the compiler checks every loan. A loan may not outlive its owner. While one part of the program can change a value, no other part can read it. These rules are checked before the program runs, so they cost nothing at run time. The checker is called the borrow checker, and it is the part of Rust that new programmers argue with.

The same rules solve the concurrency problem. A data race needs two threads with access to one value, and at least one of them writing. The ownership rules do not allow that. A Rust program that compiles, and uses no unsafe, has no data races. The compiler proves it before the program runs. Go has a race detector, but the detector finds a race only when a test meets one.

Rust 1.0 was released on 15 May 2015. The announcement called it "the official beginning of our commitment to stability". It described the aim as "low-level control over performance with high-level convenience and safety guarantees", "without requiring a garbage collector or runtime". A new version has come out about every six weeks since then. The versions this book uses are 1.97 from July 2026, 1.98 from August and 1.99 from October. Larger changes to the language arrive as an edition, and a program chooses its edition. This book uses the 2024 edition, which shipped with Rust 1.85 in February 2025.

In February 2021 the Rust Foundation was created to own the language, with AWS, Google, Huawei, Microsoft and Mozilla as founding members. Mozilla, "the original home of the Rust project", gave it the trademarks and the package registry. The language no longer belongs to one company.

📜Why Rust is hard at first

C trusts you. Go checks a little, and a garbage collector handles the rest. Rust refuses to compile a program until it can prove that the memory rules hold. The proof is yours to supply, in the types you write. That is why a Rust program takes longer to write the first time and why the compiler's messages are so long. The compiler is doing the work that in C you do in your head, and in Go you leave to the collector.

Two answers to one question#

The question was what to do about the costs of trusting the programmer. Go and Rust gave different answers, and the table shows where they differ. The rest of the book is the long form of this table.

The costCGoRust
Memory that is freed too earlya crash or a security holeimpossible, the collector frees itdoes not compile, the owner frees it
Memory that is never freeda leakunreachable objects are always freed, but a kept reference is a leakfreed at the end of scope, but a reference cycle or mem::forget can leak
Two threads writing one valueundefined behavioura race the detector may finddoes not compile
A forgotten error checkthe error is lostthe error is lost, but a linter can warnthe compiler warns on an unused Result
A slow buildthe headers are copied into every filepackages are read once, builds take secondscrates are compiled once, but the checks take time
The cost you payyour attention, all the timememory and pauses for the collectorthe time to satisfy the compiler

No answer is free. Go pays with a collector and with some things it refuses to let you express. Rust pays with the time it takes to learn, and with code that spells out more than C or Go would ask for. C pays nothing in advance, and everything later. In each chapter of this book you will feel one of those costs directly, because you will build the same thing three times.

What this means for kv#

The server you will build, kv, is a small version of Redis. Redis itself is written in C, and is a well written C program. It was started by Salvatore Sanfilippo in 2009, the year Go was announced. It keeps every key in memory, serves many clients from one thread, and writes an append-only log to survive a crash. Each of those three ideas is a chapter in this book. In each one you see the C answer first, then what Go and Rust change.

Chapter 2 installs the three toolchains and compiles one program in each language. Chapter 3 opens a socket.
