Toolchains and hello, three times#

Origins · Chapter 02· 14 min read· 85 lines of code

Install a C compiler, Go and Rust. Compile one small program in each. Then look at what the compiler made, because a binary is the thing all three languages exist to produce. The chapter also installs redis-cli, which the server will talk to from chapter 4.

kv does not exist yet. By the end of this chapter you have three programs that print a line. You also know what a compiler, a linker and a build tool each do. Every later chapter assumes those three tools are on your path.

The toolchainCGoRust
Compilercc (clang or gcc)gorustc, driven by cargo
Build toolMake, or CMake for large projectsgo build, with no build filecargo build, with Cargo.toml
Project filenone, a Makefile by conventiongo.modCargo.toml
Formatterclang-format, optionalgofmt, universalrustfmt, universal
Hello binary on macOS8 KB, links the C library2.5 MB, the runtime is inside415 KB, the standard library is inside
Where the standard library isa shared file the OS providescompiled into every binarycompiled into every binary

Install the three toolchains#

Each language has one official way to install it. For Go and Rust use that way, and not a package from a distribution, so that the version matches the baseline of this book. The commands are below for each platform.

🖥A C compiler on macOS, Linux and Windows

On macOS, run xcode-select --install. It installs Apple's clang, which answers to the name cc. On Debian and Ubuntu, run sudo apt install build-essential, which installs gcc, make and the C library headers. On Windows there are two choices. The Microsoft way is to download the Build Tools for Visual Studio and select the "Desktop development with C++" workload. The compiler is then cl.exe, and you run it from a Developer Command Prompt, because the tools need many environment variables set. The other way is to install WSL and use the Ubuntu line above. The C code in this book is written for POSIX, the Portable Operating System Interface that macOS and Linux share. So WSL is the simpler choice on Windows.

Go is installed from a package. On macOS the package installs into /usr/local/go and adds it to your path. On Linux, download the archive from go.dev/dl and extract it:

$ rm -rf /usr/local/go && tar -C /usr/local -xzf go1.27.0.linux-amd64.tar.gz
$ export PATH=$PATH:/usr/local/go/bin
$ go version
go version go1.27.0 linux/amd64

On Windows, run the MSI installer and open a new command prompt. The check is the same, go version.

Rust is installed by rustup, which also manages versions later. On macOS, Linux and WSL the command is one line:

$ curl --proto '=https' --tlsv1.2 -sSf https://sh.rustup.rs | sh
$ rustc --version
rustc 1.99.0 (b940084d7 2026-09-28)

On Windows, download and run rustup-init.exe. It may ask you to install the Visual Studio C++ Build Tools, because Rust uses the Microsoft linker there. Finally, install the Redis client. On macOS run brew install redis. On Debian and Ubuntu run sudo apt install redis-tools. Check with redis-cli --version.

⚠️Go downloads a newer Go when a module asks for one

The Go you run may not be the Go you installed. A go.mod file names a Go version. Since Go 1.21, when that version is newer than the installed one, the go command downloads the newer toolchain and uses it. The author's machine had Go 1.23 installed, and go version printed 1.27 inside this book's code. That is convenient, and it is also surprising. The environment variable GOTOOLCHAIN=local turns it off.

C: from source to binary in four steps#

The C program is six lines:

#include <stdio.h>

int main(void) {
    printf("hello from C\n");
    return 0;
}
hello/c/hello.c · region hello

Compile and run it. The flags after -std=c17 turn on every useful warning and make each warning an error. Use them on every C file you ever compile:

$ cc -std=c17 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -o hello hello.c
$ ./hello
hello from C

The one command ran four tools, and it helps to see each one. The preprocessor copies stdio.h into the file, and expands macros. Those six lines become 571. The compiler turns C into assembly. The assembler turns assembly into an object file, which is machine code with holes where the addresses of other functions go. The linker fills the holes and joins the object file with the C library. You can stop after any step:

$ cc -std=c17 -E hello.c | wc -l        # preprocess only
     571
$ cc -std=c17 -S -O2 -o - hello.c       # compile to assembly, print it
_main:
        pushq   %rbp
        movq    %rsp, %rbp
        leaq    L_str(%rip), %rdi
        callq   _puts
        xorl    %eax, %eax
        popq    %rbp
        retq
$ cc -std=c17 -c -o hello.o hello.c     # assemble to an object file
$ nm hello.o                            # the names it defines (T) and needs (U)
0000000000000000 T _main
                 U _printf

Two details in that output matter for the rest of the book. The assembly calls _puts, not _printf. The compiler saw a format string with no arguments and chose the cheaper function. The compiler is allowed to do that, and much more, whenever the result is the same. The object file was built without -O2, so it lists _printf as undefined, marked U. The linker resolves it to the C library, which on macOS is the shared file libSystem.B.dylib. Your binary does not contain printf. It contains a note that says where to find it.

📜Why C has header files

A C compiler reads one file at a time and knows nothing about the others. For a call to printf to compile, the file must have seen a declaration of printf first. Before header files, a declaration that several files needed was written again in each of them. A header file is that declaration written once and copied in by the preprocessor. The scheme was cheap on a PDP-11 and expensive. By 1984 one Unix program was found to include the same header 37 times. At Google in 2007 the same scheme turned 4.2 megabytes of source into 8 gigabytes of compiler input. Go and Rust both replaced headers with modules, which chapter 7 covers.

Exercise 02.1CWhat size is a word?

Add two lines to main. The first prints the size of an int, a long and a pointer. The second prints the compiler version. The operator sizeof gives a size in bytes, and the format %zu prints one. The macro __VERSION__ is a string that gcc and clang define. It is not part of the C standard, and cl.exe on Windows defines _MSC_VER instead. On the author's machine the output is int 4, long 8, pointer 8 bytes.

int main(void) {
    printf("hello from C\n");
    /* your two lines here */
    return 0;
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void) {
    printf("hello from C\n");
    /* sizeof gives a size in bytes. The %zu format prints a size_t. */
    printf("int %zu, long %zu, pointer %zu bytes\n",
           sizeof(int), sizeof(long), sizeof(void *));
    printf("compiler " __VERSION__ "\n");
    return 0;
}
hello/c/hello.c
✅Compile with -Wall -Wextra -Werror from the first day C

The C compiler accepts a great many programs that are wrong. The warnings catch a large share of them, and -Werror stops you from ignoring one. Every C file in this book is compiled with those three flags, and with -std=c17 so that the compiler applies one known standard.

Go: one command, one binary#

A Go program is a package. The file below is the whole of package main, and the function main is where the program starts:

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"runtime"
	"unsafe"
)

func main() {
	fmt.Println("hello from Go")
}
hello/go/main.go · region hello

Go needs one more file, go.mod, which names the module and the Go version it is written for:

module hello

go 1.27
hello/go/go.mod

Then one command compiles and links. There is no Makefile, because the go tool finds every file in the folder itself:

$ go build -o hello .
$ ./hello
hello from Go
$ go run .                  # compile and run in one step, for development
hello from Go

The binary is 2.5 megabytes, three hundred times the C one. It contains the Go runtime, which is the garbage collector, the scheduler for goroutines, and the code that talks to the operating system. On Linux it has no dependency on the C library at all. On macOS it calls libSystem for system calls and nothing else. That is why a Go binary can be copied to another machine of the same kind and run there with nothing installed. Chapter 13 uses that fact.

Exercise 02.2GoWhat does the runtime know?

Add two lines to main. The first prints the size of an int and of a pointer, with unsafe.Sizeof, which works like sizeof in C. The second prints runtime.Version(), runtime.GOOS and runtime.GOARCH. Import the packages runtime and unsafe. An import you do not use is an error in Go, so add each import together with the line that uses it.

func main() {
	fmt.Println("hello from Go")
	// your two lines here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"runtime"
	"unsafe"
)

func main() {
	fmt.Println("hello from Go")
	// unsafe.Sizeof is a compile-time constant, like sizeof in C.
	var i int
	var p *int
	fmt.Printf("int %d, pointer %d bytes\n", unsafe.Sizeof(i), unsafe.Sizeof(p))
	fmt.Println("compiler", runtime.Version(), runtime.GOOS, runtime.GOARCH)
}
hello/go/main.go
✅Run gofmt and go vet before you commit Go

gofmt -l . lists the files whose layout is not the standard one, and gofmt -w . fixes them. go vet ./... finds mistakes the compiler accepts, such as a wrong format verb. Both tools come with Go, and every Go project runs them. There is no setting for the style, which is the point.

Rust: cargo owns the project#

In Rust you rarely run the compiler, rustc, yourself. The tool cargo creates the project, builds it, runs it, tests it and fetches its dependencies. cargo new hello makes a folder with two files. The first is the project file:

[package]
name = "hello"
version = "0.1.0"
edition = "2024"

[dependencies]
hello/rust/Cargo.toml

The edition line names the version of the language rules. The 2024 edition is the current one. The second file is src/main.rs:

fn main() {
    println!("hello from Rust");
}
hello/rust/src/main.rs · region hello

The exclamation mark means that println! is a macro, not a function. A macro can check the format string at compile time, and this one does. A build has two modes. The default is a debug build, which compiles quickly. The release build is slower to compile and runs faster:

$ cargo build --release
   Compiling hello v0.1.0
    Finished `release` profile [optimized] target(s)
$ ./target/release/hello
hello from Rust
$ cargo run --release       # build if needed, then run

Everything cargo makes goes under target/. cargo new already lists it in the project's .gitignore. The binary is 415 kilobytes. Like the Go one it contains its standard library. Like the C one it calls the C library of the operating system for a call such as write. Rust sits between C and Go.

Exercise 02.3RustWhat size is a usize?

Add two lines to main. The first prints the size of four types, with size_of::<T>(), which the compiler evaluates at compile time. The four types are i32, i64, usize and a reference. The second prints std::env::consts::OS and std::env::consts::ARCH. Then run cargo clippy and cargo fmt, and see whether either tool reports something.

fn main() {
    println!("hello from Rust");
    // your two lines here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
fn main() {
    println!("hello from Rust");
    // size_of is evaluated at compile time, like sizeof in C.
    println!(
        "i32 {}, i64 {}, usize {}, pointer {} bytes",
        size_of::<i32>(),
        size_of::<i64>(),
        size_of::<usize>(),
        size_of::<&i32>()
    );
    println!("target {} {}", std::env::consts::OS, std::env::consts::ARCH);
}
hello/rust/src/main.rs
✅Run rustfmt and clippy before you commit Rust

cargo fmt formats the project. cargo clippy is a linter with more than 800 checks, from a needless clone to a loop that should be an iterator. Every Rust snapshot in this book passes cargo clippy -- -D warnings, which makes each warning an error, and cargo fmt --check.

What a binary is#

All three programs are files the operating system can load and run. On macOS the format is called Mach-O. On Linux it is called ELF, the Executable and Linkable Format. On Windows it is called PE, the Portable Executable. The file command names the format:

$ file hello
hello: Mach-O 64-bit executable x86_64

A binary holds machine code, the constant data such as the string to print, and a list of the shared libraries it needs. On macOS the list is printed by otool -L, on Linux by ldd, and on Windows by dumpbin /dependents. The three hello programs name the same library:

$ otool -L hello
hello:
        /usr/lib/libSystem.B.dylib (compatibility version 1.0.0, current version 1356.0.0)

So the three binaries differ in what they carry, not in what they are. The C binary carries almost nothing and depends on the system for everything. The Go binary carries its own runtime. The Rust binary carries its standard library and depends on the system for the rest. The sizes in the table at the top of the chapter follow from that. The programs print the same word sizes too. An int is 4 bytes in C and 8 in Go. A pointer is 8 bytes in all three. A Rust usize is the size of a pointer. The pointer size comes from the machine. The size of int is a choice each language made, and C and Go chose differently.

🖥The same program on Linux and Windows

On Linux the C binary is an ELF file and links libc.so.6, the GNU C library. The Go binary links nothing at all, because Go makes its system calls directly on Linux. On Windows the C program links the Windows C runtime, and Rust links the system libraries it needs. Go loads kernel32.dll and a few other system libraries at start, because Windows has no stable system call interface. The chapter on shipping explains what that means for copying a binary from one machine to another.

Build tools, and the lack of one#

C has no build tool of its own. The convention is make, which Stuart Feldman wrote at Bell Labs in 1976. It reads a file called Makefile. The file lists each output, the inputs it depends on, and the command that makes it. The Makefile for the hello program is short:

CC     ?= cc
CFLAGS ?= -std=c17 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -O2

hello: hello.c
	$(CC) $(CFLAGS) -o $@ hello.c

clean:
	rm -rf hello hello.dSYM

.PHONY: clean
hello/c/Makefile

The line hello: hello.c says that hello is made from hello.c, and the indented line below says how. make runs the command only when the input is newer than the output. The ?= sets a variable only when the environment has not set it, which is how the book's test script adds sanitizer flags later. Large C projects use CMake, which writes Makefiles for you from a description that works on every platform. The Redis source, by comparison, is plain Make.

⚠️A Makefile recipe begins with a tab C

Eight spaces are not a tab. The command line under a rule must start with a tab character, and an editor that converts tabs to spaces produces the error missing separator. Feldman has said the tab was a workaround in the first version, kept so as not to break the first users. Every C programmer since has met it.

Go and Rust each come with a build tool, and each tool is the only one anyone uses. For Go the description of the project is go.mod, and the build is go build. For Rust it is Cargo.toml and cargo build. Both tools also run tests, fetch dependencies, and format code. The difference from C is not that these tools are cleverer than Make. It is that there is one of them, so every project looks the same.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
InstallingOKStrongStrongA C compiler is one system command away on macOS and Linux. Go and Rust are one download each, on every platform.
BuildingWeakStrongStrongC has no build tool, only a convention. Go and Rust each have one tool that every project uses.
Seeing what you madeStrongOKOKC shows every step from preprocessor to linker. Go and Rust hide the steps, which is usually what you want.
Binary sizeStrongWeakOK8 KB for C, 2.5 MB for Go because the runtime is inside, 415 KB for Rust.
Running elsewhereWeakStrongOKThe C binary needs the right C library on the target, the Go binary needs nothing, and Rust is in between.
Next · Building kv03Echo server14 min

Echo server#

Building kv · Chapter 03· 14 min read· 138 lines of code

A server that sends back what it receives. It is the smallest program that opens a port, accepts a client and moves bytes in both directions. Every later chapter adds to this one. The chapter teaches the socket calls that all three languages share, and how each language wraps them.

kv has to be reachable over the network. A client such as redis-cli connects to a port, sends bytes, and reads bytes back. Before kv can understand a command it must be able to hold that conversation at all. So the first version of kv is an echo server. Whatever the client sends, the server sends back, one client at a time.

The socket callsCGoRust
Open a portsocket, setsockopt, bind, listennet.Listen("tcp", ":6380")TcpListener::bind("0.0.0.0:6380")
Wait for a clientaccept, returns an intln.Accept(), returns a net.Connlistener.incoming(), yields a TcpStream
Read bytesread(fd, buf, n), returns a count or -1conn.Read(buf), returns a count and an errorstream.read(&mut buf), returns a Result
Copy in to outa loop you write, with a short-write loop insideio.Copy(conn, conn)io::copy(&mut reader, &mut writer)
Closeclose(fd), which you must rememberdefer conn.Close()dropped at the end of scope
An error-1 and a number in errnoa second return valuean Err you must handle or pass on

The four calls in the first row are all there is. A socket is a file descriptor that the operating system connects to the network instead of to a disk. To bind is to give the socket an address and a port. To listen is to tell the operating system that this socket accepts connections, and how many may wait. To accept is to block until a client connects, and to get a second socket that talks to that one client. The first socket keeps listening. The second one is read and written like a file, and closed like one.

📜Why every language has the same four calls

The calls come from 4.2BSD, the Berkeley version of Unix released in 1983, and are called the Berkeley sockets API. The operating system standard POSIX, the Portable Operating System Interface, adopted them. Windows copied them in 1992 under the name Winsock, with a few changes. Go and Rust do not replace these calls. They call them. Underneath, net.Listen and TcpListener::bind are the same four calls. So the C version of this chapter is also an explanation of the other two.

C: four calls, and a way to clean up#

The port comes from an environment variable, so the tests can pick one that is free. The C library function getenv returns a pointer to the value, or NULL when the variable is not set. The pointer belongs to the environment, not to you, so you do not free it:

/* The port comes from the environment, so the tests can pick a free one. */
static int kv_port(void) {
    const char *s = getenv("KV_PORT");
    if (s == NULL || *s == '\0') {
        return 6380;
    }
    return atoi(s);
}
c/ch03/kv.c · region port

Opening the port takes the four calls, and each of them can fail. The pattern below is the most common shape in C that does more than one thing that can fail. Each failure prints its reason and jumps to cleanup, which releases what was acquired so far:

/* Open a listening socket on every interface. Returns the socket, or -1 after
 * printing the reason. The caller owns the socket and must close() it. */
static int kv_listen(int port) {
    int fd = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0);
    if (fd < 0) {
        perror("socket");
        return -1;
    }
    /* Without this, a restart within a minute fails with "address in use". */
    int yes = 1;
    if (setsockopt(fd, SOL_SOCKET, SO_REUSEADDR, &yes, sizeof yes) < 0) {
        perror("setsockopt");
        goto cleanup;
    }
    struct sockaddr_in addr;
    memset(&addr, 0, sizeof addr);
    addr.sin_family = AF_INET;
    addr.sin_addr.s_addr = htonl(INADDR_ANY);
    addr.sin_port = htons((uint16_t)port);
    if (bind(fd, (struct sockaddr *)&addr, sizeof addr) < 0) {
        perror("bind");
        goto cleanup;
    }
    if (listen(fd, 16) < 0) {
        perror("listen");
        goto cleanup;
    }
    return fd;

cleanup:
    close(fd);
    return -1;
}
c/ch03/kv.c · region listen

Read the function from the bottom. There is one place where the socket is closed, and every failure after the socket exists reaches it. The goto is not a sign of bad code here. It is how C expresses "undo what I did" without exceptions and without destructors. perror prints the name you give it, a colon, and a message. The message is the text for the current value of errno. That is the global number which every failed system call sets.

✅One exit, through cleanup C

A function that acquires more than one resource has one label, cleanup, at the end. Each acquisition that fails jumps there. The cleanup code releases resources in the reverse order of acquisition. Each release must be safe to run when the resource was never acquired. For example, set a descriptor to -1 first, and skip close when it is still -1. The Linux kernel is written this way, and so is Redis.

⚠️Address already in use C

A server that you restart soon after it stopped fails to bind. When a connection over TCP, the Transmission Control Protocol, closes, the operating system keeps its address in a waiting state for a while. The wait stops late packets from reaching a new program. The option SO_REUSEADDR says that your server may bind anyway. Every server sets it. Forget it and the second start of kv prints bind: Address already in use.

Serving a client is a loop. read returns the number of bytes it put in the buffer, zero when the client closed the connection, or -1 on an error. Writing those bytes back needs a loop of its own, because write may send fewer bytes than you asked when the network is busy. Both calls can also be interrupted by a signal, in which case errno is EINTR and the right response is to try again.

Exercise 03.1CServe one client

Write kv_serve. Read into a 4 KB buffer until read returns zero or an error. After each read, write everything back, and keep writing until every byte is out. Treat EINTR as "try again" in both calls. The caller closes the client socket.

static void kv_serve(int client) {
    char buf[4096];
    for (;;) {
        ssize_t n = read(client, buf, sizeof buf);
        /* your code: handle n < 0 and n == 0, then write all n bytes back */
    }
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
/* Copy everything the client sends back to it, until the client closes. */
static void kv_serve(int client) {
    char buf[4096];
    for (;;) {
        ssize_t n = read(client, buf, sizeof buf);
        if (n < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) {
                continue;
            }
            perror("read");
            return;
        }
        if (n == 0) {
            return; /* the client closed the connection */
        }
        /* write() may send fewer bytes than asked, so loop until all are out */
        ssize_t off = 0;
        while (off < n) {
            ssize_t w = write(client, buf + off, (size_t)(n - off));
            if (w < 0) {
                if (errno == EINTR) {
                    continue;
                }
                perror("write");
                return;
            }
            off += w;
        }
    }
}
c/ch03/kv.c · region serve

The main function ties the pieces together. It accepts one client, serves it to the end, closes it, and accepts the next. A second client waits in the operating system's queue until the first one is done. Chapter 8 removes that limit:

int main(void) {
    int port = kv_port();
    int fd = kv_listen(port);
    if (fd < 0) {
        return 1;
    }
    fprintf(stderr, "kv listening on port %d\n", port);
    for (;;) {
        int client = accept(fd, NULL, NULL);
        if (client < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) {
                continue;
            }
            perror("accept");
            break;
        }
        kv_serve(client);
        close(client);
    }
    close(fd);
    return 1;
}
c/ch03/kv.c · region main

Build and run it with the Makefile from chapter 2, which has grown a SANITIZE variable for the tests:

CC      ?= cc
CFLAGS  ?= -std=c17 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -O2 -g
SANITIZE ?=

kv: kv.c
	$(CC) $(CFLAGS) $(SANITIZE) -o $@ kv.c

clean:
	rm -rf kv kv.dSYM

.PHONY: clean
c/ch03/Makefile · region all

Then talk to it with nc, the netcat tool that comes with macOS and most Linux systems. Type a line and the server sends it back:

$ make
$ ./kv &
kv listening on port 6380
$ nc localhost 6380
hello
hello

Press Ctrl-C to stop nc, and kill %1 to stop the server.

Go: the loop you did not have to write#

The Go version has the same three parts. The port function reads the environment with os.Getenv, which returns the empty string when the variable is unset. There is no NULL to check, and nothing to free:

// The port comes from the environment, so the tests can pick a free one.
func port() string {
	if p := os.Getenv("KV_PORT"); p != "" {
		return p
	}
	return "6380"
}
go/ch03/main.go · region port

Serving a client is one call. io.Copy reads from its second argument and writes to its first until the reader returns end of file. A net.Conn is both a reader and a writer, so copying a connection to itself is an echo. The short-write loop and the retry on interruption are inside io.Copy and the runtime:

Exercise 03.2GoServe one client with io.Copy

Write serve. It takes a net.Conn, closes it when the function returns, copies the connection to itself, and returns the error from the copy. Use defer for the close.

func serve(conn net.Conn) error {
	// your code here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
// serve copies everything the client sends back to it, until the client closes.
func serve(conn net.Conn) error {
	defer conn.Close()
	_, err := io.Copy(conn, conn)
	return err
}
go/ch03/main.go · region serve
✅defer the close on the line after the open Go

defer conn.Close() runs when the function returns, by whichever path. Write it on the line after the connection is created, so that no later return can forget it. This is the Go form of the C cleanup label, and it needs no label.

The main function listens and loops. net.Listen makes the four calls of the C version for you. It also sets SO_REUSEADDR, which you can see in the file net/sockopt_bsd.go of the Go source. An error from Accept is printed and the loop continues, because one bad connection must not stop the server:

func main() {
	ln, err := net.Listen("tcp", ":"+port())
	if err != nil {
		fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, err)
		os.Exit(1)
	}
	fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, "kv listening on port", port())
	for {
		conn, err := ln.Accept()
		if err != nil {
			fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, "accept:", err)
			continue
		}
		if err := serve(conn); err != nil {
			fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, "client:", err)
		}
	}
}
go/ch03/main.go · region main
$ go run .
kv listening on port 6380
⚠️A missing error check is legal Go Go

Go lets you drop an error on the floor. serve(conn) on a line of its own compiles, and the error is lost. The compiler checks that a value you assign is used, but not that a value you never assign is looked at. go vet does not catch it either. A separate linter called errcheck does. This book does not add it, and writes every check out instead. In this chapter the check is written out, as if err := serve(conn); err != nil.

Rust: a Result at every step#

In Rust the environment variable is a Result. std::env::var returns Ok(value) or an error when the variable is unset or not valid text. unwrap_or_else turns the error case into the default:

/// The port comes from the environment, so the tests can pick a free one.
fn port() -> String {
    std::env::var("KV_PORT").unwrap_or_else(|_| "6380".to_string())
}
rust/ch03/src/main.rs · region port

Serving a client uses io::copy, which does the same as io.Copy in Go, with the arguments in the other order. There is one new idea. A TcpStream is one value, and the ownership rules allow one mutable use of it at a time. The simplest way to get two handles on it is to clone the stream. try_clone asks the operating system for a second descriptor to the same connection, and that can fail, so it returns a Result too:

Exercise 03.3RustServe one client with io::copy

Write serve. Clone the stream with try_clone, copy from the clone to the original with io::copy, and return Ok(()). Use ? after each call that returns a Result, so that an error returns from the function at once.

fn serve(stream: TcpStream) -> io::Result<()> {
    // your code here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
/// Copy everything the client sends back to it, until the client closes.
fn serve(stream: TcpStream) -> io::Result<()> {
    let mut reader = stream.try_clone()?;
    let mut writer = stream;
    io::copy(&mut reader, &mut writer)?;
    Ok(())
}
rust/ch03/src/main.rs · region serve

The main function returns io::Result<()>, so ? works inside it and a failure to bind prints the error and exits with a non-zero status. The loop uses incoming(), which yields one Result per connection attempt. A match handles both arms, and the compiler would refuse the program if one arm were missing:

fn main() -> io::Result<()> {
    let port = port();
    let listener = TcpListener::bind(format!("0.0.0.0:{port}"))?;
    eprintln!("kv listening on port {port}");
    for stream in listener.incoming() {
        match stream {
            Ok(stream) => {
                if let Err(e) = serve(stream) {
                    eprintln!("client: {e}");
                }
            }
            Err(e) => eprintln!("accept: {e}"),
        }
    }
    Ok(())
}
rust/ch03/src/main.rs · region main
$ cargo run --release
kv listening on port 6380

Nothing closes the stream, and nothing needs to. When serve returns, the TcpStream it owns goes out of scope, and Rust runs its destructor, which calls close. The clone is closed the same way. This is the third way to do what cleanup did in C and defer did in Go. It is the only one of the three that cannot be forgotten.

⚠️A Result you ignore is a warning, not an error Rust

The compiler warns about an unused Result and then compiles the program. serve(stream); on a line of its own produces warning: unused Result that must be used. The book's build turns warnings into errors with cargo clippy -- -D warnings, so in this book the line does not compile. Without that flag it does, and the error is lost as in Go. The difference from Go is that Rust told you.

Errors, three ways#

The three versions of serve differ most in how they say that something went wrong, and the difference runs through the whole book. In C a function returns -1, and the reason is a number in the global errno. The caller must know to check, and must check before any other call overwrites the number. In Go a function returns the error as a second value, and nil means success. The caller must still choose to look. In Rust a function returns Result, which is either Ok(value) or Err(reason), and the value is inside. To use the value at all, the caller must take it out. The ways to take it out are match, ?, and unwrap. The last one crashes on an error and is for examples only. Chapter 4 builds a parser in all three styles, where the difference is larger.

🖥Sockets on Windows

Winsock has the same calls with the same names, with three differences that matter. A program must call WSAStartup before any of them. A socket is a handle of its own type, not an int, and it is closed with closesocket. And the option SO_REUSEADDR means something stronger on Windows, where it allows two programs to bind the same port. The C version of kv is written for POSIX and does not build on Windows without changes. The Go and Rust versions build there unchanged, because their standard libraries contain the differences.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
Opening a portWeakStrongStrongC needs four calls, a struct and an option. Go and Rust need one call that sets the option for you.
Closing it againWeakOKStrongC needs a cleanup label and Go needs a defer you must write, but Rust closes at the end of scope by itself.
The echo loopWeakStrongStrongC needs the short-write loop and the EINTR retry. Both standard libraries contain them.
A lost errorWeakWeakOKC and Go let a return value drop. Rust warns, and with the book's flags refuses.
Seeing what happensStrongOKOKThe C version shows every system call. The other two hide them, which is what you want on most days.
WindowsWeakStrongStrongThe C code needs Winsock changes. Go and Rust build unchanged.
What you must learn firstStrongOKWeakC is the calls themselves. Rust asks for Result, ?, ownership and try_clone before an echo works.
Next · Building kv04Speaking RESP25 min

Speaking RESP#

Building kv · Chapter 04· 25 min read· 479 lines of code

Redis clients talk in a small wire format called RESP, the Redis serialization protocol. This chapter teaches kv to read it and write it. After this chapter the real redis-cli can say PING to your server and get PONG back. The chapter also compares how the three languages say "something went wrong", because a parser is where that question is asked most often.

The echo server sends bytes back. redis-cli does not want its bytes back. It wants an answer in the format it expects. When you type ECHO hi into redis-cli, it sends these 22 bytes to the server:

*2\r\n$4\r\nECHO\r\n$2\r\nhi\r\n

Written with one element per line, the request reads as follows. An array of 2 bulk strings. The first has 4 bytes, ECHO. The second has 2 bytes, hi. The server replies with a bulk string of its own, $2\r\nhi\r\n. Every message in RESP is built from five types, each marked by its first byte:

+OK\r\n                   a simple string, for short replies such as OK and PONG
-ERR unknown command\r\n  an error, which the client shows as an error
:42\r\n                   an integer
$5\r\nhello\r\n           a bulk string: the length, then exactly that many bytes
*2\r\n...                 an array: the count, then that many messages
$-1\r\n                   the null bulk string, which GET returns for a missing key

The parts are separated by \r\n, a carriage return and a line feed, which this chapter calls a line ending. A bulk string carries its length in front, so the bytes inside can be anything, including line endings. That one rule is what makes the format both readable and fast to parse. There is no quoting and no escaping.

📜Why RESP looks like this

The Redis protocol specification names its three aims as "Simple to implement. Fast to parse. Human readable." The length in front of a bulk string means the parser never scans the payload for special characters, as a JSON parser must. The second version of the protocol, RESP2, became the standard in Redis 2.0. A third version, RESP3, adds types such as maps and a null of its own. A client chooses it with the HELLO command. kv speaks RESP2, which every client still speaks. The specification also allows an inline command. That is a plain line of words, so that a person with only telnet can talk to a server. Those work in kv too, which is why nc still works after this chapter.

Reading a requestCGoRust
Where the bytes waita buffer you grow with realloca bufio.Readera BufReader
Reading a linememchr for the line feedr.ReadBytes('\n')r.read_until(b'\n', &mut line)
Reading n bytescheck the length, then take a pointerio.ReadFull(r, buf)r.read_exact(&mut buf)
The argumentspointers into the buffer, no copy[][]byte, one copyVec<Vec<u8>>, one copy
Not enough bytes yetthe status KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETEthe reader blocks until they arrivethe reader blocks until they arrive
Bytes that are not RESPthe status KV_PARSE_BADthe error value errProtocolan io::Error of kind InvalidData
The client went awayread returns 0io.EOFOk(None)

The table shows the first large difference between C and the other two. Go and Rust read from a buffered reader that blocks until the bytes arrive, so the parser can ask for a line and wait. C has stdio, a buffered reader built for text files, and kv does not use it. The event loop of chapter 8 needs to see the bytes that have arrived so far. The C parser works on whatever bytes have arrived so far, and says "incomplete" when the request is not all there. That design is more work now. In chapter 8 it is the design the C event loop needs.

C: a buffer, a parser and three answers#

C needs a place to keep bytes between reads, because one read may return half a request, or two and a half. The structure below is a growable byte buffer. The comment on data says who owns it. The book writes such a comment on every pointer in a structure, because nothing else in C will tell you:

/* A growable byte buffer. It owns data, and kv_buf_free releases it. */
typedef struct {
    char *data; /* owned; NULL until the first append */
    size_t len;
    size_t cap;
} kv_buf;

static bool kv_buf_reserve(kv_buf *b, size_t extra) {
    if (b->len + extra <= b->cap) {
        return true;
    }
    size_t cap = b->cap ? b->cap * 2 : 1024;
    while (cap < b->len + extra) {
        cap *= 2;
    }
    char *p = realloc(b->data, cap);
    if (p == NULL) {
        return false; /* the old block is still valid and still ours */
    }
    b->data = p;
    b->cap = cap;
    return true;
}

static bool kv_buf_append(kv_buf *b, const void *src, size_t n) {
    if (!kv_buf_reserve(b, n)) {
        return false;
    }
    memcpy(b->data + b->len, src, n);
    b->len += n;
    return true;
}

/* Drop the first n bytes. memmove allows the two ranges to overlap. */
static void kv_buf_consume(kv_buf *b, size_t n) {
    memmove(b->data, b->data + n, b->len - n);
    b->len -= n;
}

static void kv_buf_free(kv_buf *b) {
    free(b->data);
    b->data = NULL;
    b->len = b->cap = 0;
}
c/ch04/kv.c · region buf

Look at kv_buf_reserve. When realloc fails it returns NULL and leaves the old block alone, so the code must not write the result into b->data before checking it. Writing b->data = realloc(b->data, cap) is a common mistake that loses the buffer on failure. kv_buf_consume uses memmove, not memcpy, because the source and the destination overlap. memcpy on overlapping memory is undefined behaviour.

A parsed command is a small structure of pointers into the buffer. No bytes are copied. The cost of that choice is a rule. The pointers are valid only until the buffer changes, and the comment says so. The parser returns one of three statuses, and the names of the statuses are the whole design:

/* One parsed request. The arguments point into the request buffer, so they are valid
 * only until that buffer is consumed. A kv_cmd owns nothing. */
typedef struct {
    size_t argc;
    const char *argv[KV_MAX_ARGS];
    size_t arglen[KV_MAX_ARGS];
} kv_cmd;

typedef enum {
    KV_PARSE_OK,         /* cmd is filled in, and *used bytes were taken */
    KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE, /* read more bytes and try again */
    KV_PARSE_BAD         /* the bytes are not RESP: close the connection */
} kv_parse_status;
c/ch04/kv.c · region cmd

Two helpers find a line ending and read a number. The number reader refuses more than 18 digits, so that the multiplication cannot overflow a 64-bit long. Signed overflow is undefined behaviour in C, and a length field is exactly where an attacker would put a large number. A third helper splits an inline line into words:

/* The '\n' that ends the line starting at p, or NULL when it has not arrived yet. */
static const char *kv_find_eol(const char *p, const char *end) {
    return memchr(p, '\n', (size_t)(end - p));
}

/* A decimal number between p and the line end. False when the text is not a number. */
static bool kv_parse_number(const char *p, const char *eol, long *out) {
    const char *stop = eol;
    if (stop > p && stop[-1] == '\r') {
        stop--;
    }
    if (p == stop || stop - p > 18) {
        return false;
    }
    long v = 0;
    for (; p < stop; p++) {
        if (*p < '0' || *p > '9') {
            return false;
        }
        v = v * 10 + (*p - '0');
    }
    *out = v;
    return true;
}

/* An inline command: words separated by spaces, as typed into nc or telnet. */
static kv_parse_status kv_parse_inline(const char *data, const char *eol, kv_cmd *cmd,
                                       size_t *used) {
    const char *p = data;
    const char *stop = eol;
    if (stop > p && stop[-1] == '\r') {
        stop--;
    }
    while (p < stop) {
        while (p < stop && *p == ' ') {
            p++;
        }
        if (p == stop) {
            break;
        }
        const char *word = p;
        while (p < stop && *p != ' ') {
            p++;
        }
        if (cmd->argc == KV_MAX_ARGS) {
            return KV_PARSE_BAD;
        }
        cmd->argv[cmd->argc] = word;
        cmd->arglen[cmd->argc] = (size_t)(p - word);
        cmd->argc++;
    }
    *used = (size_t)(eol + 1 - data);
    return KV_PARSE_OK;
}
c/ch04/kv.c · region parse-helpers

kv.c sets three limits. KV_MAX_ARGS is 64 arguments and KV_MAX_BULK is 1 MB for one bulk string. KV_MAX_REQUEST is twice that. The Go and Rust files set the same two limits under the names maxBulk and MAX_BULK.

Exercise 04.1CParse the bulk strings

Write the loop in kv_parse that reads the n bulk strings after the array header. For each one, check that the next byte is $ and find its line ending. Read the length. Then check that the whole string and its line ending have arrived. Return KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE when bytes are missing and KV_PARSE_BAD when a byte is wrong. Store a pointer and a length in cmd, and set *used to the number of bytes the request took. Treat a length above KV_MAX_BULK as bad.

static kv_parse_status kv_parse(const char *data, size_t len, kv_cmd *cmd, size_t *used) {
    const char *end = data + len;
    const char *p = data;
    cmd->argc = 0;
    if (len == 0) return KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE;
    const char *eol = kv_find_eol(p, end);
    if (eol == NULL) return KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE;
    if (*p != '*') return kv_parse_inline(data, eol, cmd, used);
    long n;
    if (!kv_parse_number(p + 1, eol, &n) || n > KV_MAX_ARGS) return KV_PARSE_BAD;
    p = eol + 1;
    for (long i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        /* your code here */
    }
    cmd->argc = (size_t)n;
    *used = (size_t)(p - data);
    return KV_PARSE_OK;
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
/* Parse one request from data[0..len). The request is an array of bulk strings, such as
 * "*2\r\n$4\r\nECHO\r\n$2\r\nhi\r\n", or one inline line. */
static kv_parse_status kv_parse(const char *data, size_t len, kv_cmd *cmd, size_t *used) {
    const char *end = data + len;
    const char *p = data;
    cmd->argc = 0;
    if (len == 0) {
        return KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE;
    }
    const char *eol = kv_find_eol(p, end);
    if (eol == NULL) {
        return KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE;
    }
    if (*p != '*') {
        return kv_parse_inline(data, eol, cmd, used);
    }
    long n;
    if (!kv_parse_number(p + 1, eol, &n) || n > KV_MAX_ARGS) {
        return KV_PARSE_BAD;
    }
    p = eol + 1;
    for (long i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (p >= end) {
            return KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE;
        }
        if (*p != '$') {
            return KV_PARSE_BAD;
        }
        eol = kv_find_eol(p, end);
        if (eol == NULL) {
            return KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE;
        }
        long blen;
        if (!kv_parse_number(p + 1, eol, &blen) || blen > KV_MAX_BULK) {
            return KV_PARSE_BAD;
        }
        p = eol + 1;
        if (end - p < blen + 2) {
            return KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE;
        }
        if (p[blen] != '\r' || p[blen + 1] != '\n') {
            return KV_PARSE_BAD;
        }
        cmd->argv[i] = p;
        cmd->arglen[i] = (size_t)blen;
        p += blen + 2;
    }
    cmd->argc = (size_t)n;
    *used = (size_t)(p - data);
    return KV_PARSE_OK;
}
c/ch04/kv.c · region parse
⚠️Pointers into a buffer that moves C

A pointer into a buffer dies when the buffer grows. realloc may move the block, and every pointer into the old block then points at freed memory. The kv_cmd holds such pointers. kv is safe because it runs the command and consumes the buffer before the next read appends to it. Move one line and it is not safe, and the compiler will not say a word. Chapter 11 shows AddressSanitizer catching exactly this.

Replies are appended to a second buffer. Each reply function returns false when memory runs out, and the callers pass that on:

static bool kv_reply_line(kv_buf *out, char first, const char *s) {
    return kv_buf_append(out, &first, 1) && kv_buf_append(out, s, strlen(s)) &&
           kv_buf_append(out, "\r\n", 2);
}

static bool kv_reply_simple(kv_buf *out, const char *s) { return kv_reply_line(out, '+', s); }
static bool kv_reply_error(kv_buf *out, const char *s) { return kv_reply_line(out, '-', s); }

static bool kv_reply_bulk(kv_buf *out, const char *s, size_t n) {
    char head[32];
    int h = snprintf(head, sizeof head, "$%zu\r\n", n);
    return kv_buf_append(out, head, (size_t)h) && kv_buf_append(out, s, n) &&
           kv_buf_append(out, "\r\n", 2);
}

static bool kv_reply_array(kv_buf *out, size_t n) {
    char head[32];
    int h = snprintf(head, sizeof head, "*%zu\r\n", n);
    return kv_buf_append(out, head, (size_t)h);
}
c/ch04/kv.c · region reply

Running a command is a chain of comparisons on the first argument. Command names in Redis are case-insensitive, so the comparison uses strncasecmp from strings.h, with the length, because the argument is not a C string. It has no terminating zero byte. That is a theme of the next chapter:

/* True when argument i of the command is name, ignoring case. */
static bool kv_arg_is(const kv_cmd *c, size_t i, const char *name) {
    size_t n = strlen(name);
    return c->arglen[i] == n && strncasecmp(c->argv[i], name, n) == 0;
}

static bool kv_reply_arity(kv_buf *out, const char *name) {
    char msg[96];
    snprintf(msg, sizeof msg, "ERR wrong number of arguments for '%s' command", name);
    return kv_reply_error(out, msg);
}

/* Run one command and append its reply to out. */
static bool kv_exec(const kv_cmd *c, kv_buf *out) {
    if (c->argc == 0) {
        return kv_reply_error(out, "ERR empty command");
    }
    if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "PING")) {
        if (c->argc == 1) {
            return kv_reply_simple(out, "PONG");
        }
        if (c->argc == 2) {
            return kv_reply_bulk(out, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1]);
        }
        return kv_reply_arity(out, "ping");
    }
    if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "ECHO")) {
        if (c->argc != 2) {
            return kv_reply_arity(out, "echo");
        }
        return kv_reply_bulk(out, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1]);
    }
    if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "COMMAND")) {
        return kv_reply_array(out, 0); /* redis-cli asks on connect; an empty list is fine */
    }
    char msg[96];
    int n = c->arglen[0] < 40 ? (int)c->arglen[0] : 40;
    snprintf(msg, sizeof msg, "ERR unknown command '%.*s'", n, c->argv[0]);
    return kv_reply_error(out, msg);
}
c/ch04/kv.c · region exec

The serving loop ties it together. After each read it runs every complete request in the buffer, because a client may send several at once, which Redis calls a pipeline. Then it writes all the replies in one call. A request that grows past KV_MAX_REQUEST without ending is treated as bad, so that a client cannot make the server grow its buffer without limit. The write loop from chapter 3 becomes a function first:

/* write() may send fewer bytes than asked, so loop until all are out. */
static bool kv_write_all(int fd, const char *p, size_t n) {
    size_t off = 0;
    while (off < n) {
        ssize_t w = write(fd, p + off, n - off);
        if (w < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) {
                continue;
            }
            perror("write");
            return false;
        }
        off += (size_t)w;
    }
    return true;
}
c/ch04/kv.c · region write-all
/* Read requests, run them, send the replies, until the client closes. */
static void kv_serve(int client) {
    kv_buf in = {0};
    kv_buf out = {0};
    char tmp[4096];
    for (;;) {
        ssize_t n = read(client, tmp, sizeof tmp);
        if (n < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) {
                continue;
            }
            perror("read");
            break;
        }
        if (n == 0) {
            break; /* the client closed the connection */
        }
        if (!kv_buf_append(&in, tmp, (size_t)n)) {
            break;
        }
        /* A pipeline holds several requests in one read, so loop until one is incomplete. */
        bool bad = false;
        for (;;) {
            kv_cmd cmd;
            size_t used = 0;
            kv_parse_status st = kv_parse(in.data, in.len, &cmd, &used);
            if (st == KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE) {
                bad = in.len > KV_MAX_REQUEST; /* a request that never ends is not a request */
                break;
            }
            if (st == KV_PARSE_BAD) {
                bad = true;
                break;
            }
            if (!kv_exec(&cmd, &out)) {
                break;
            }
            kv_buf_consume(&in, used);
        }
        if (bad) {
            kv_reply_error(&out, "ERR Protocol error");
        }
        if (out.len > 0 && !kv_write_all(client, out.data, out.len)) {
            break;
        }
        out.len = 0;
        if (bad) {
            break;
        }
    }
    kv_buf_free(&in);
    kv_buf_free(&out);
}
c/ch04/kv.c · region serve

Build it, run it, and talk to it with the real client:

$ make && ./kv &
$ redis-cli -p 6380 PING
PONG
$ redis-cli -p 6380 ECHO "hello world"
"hello world"
$ redis-cli -p 6380 FOO
(error) ERR unknown command 'FOO'
✅Say who owns every pointer C

A pointer in C carries no information about who frees it or how long it stays valid. So the comment must. kv_buf says "owned". kv_cmd says "valid only until the buffer is consumed". A function that returns a pointer says whether the caller must free it. Redis does this, the Linux kernel does this, and so does every C codebase that has survived long enough to be read by strangers.

Go: a reader that waits, and an error that names itself#

Go has bufio.Reader, which reads ahead into its own buffer and gives you lines or exact counts of bytes. The parser becomes a set of calls that block until the bytes are there. The "incomplete" status disappears, because the reader waits instead. The first helper reads a line and removes its ending:

// readLine returns one line without its line ending. A bare LF is accepted as well as
// CRLF, so that a command typed into nc works. io.EOF means the client closed the
// connection cleanly between commands.
func readLine(r *bufio.Reader) ([]byte, error) {
	line, err := r.ReadBytes('\n')
	if err != nil {
		if err == io.EOF && len(line) > 0 {
			return nil, io.ErrUnexpectedEOF
		}
		return nil, err
	}
	line = bytes.TrimSuffix(line, []byte("\n"))
	return bytes.TrimSuffix(line, []byte("\r")), nil
}
go/ch04/resp.go · region read-line

Two errors from the standard library carry meaning here. io.EOF means the connection closed, and readLine returns it unchanged when it happens between requests, which is the normal way for a client to leave. io.ErrUnexpectedEOF means the connection closed in the middle of something, which is not normal. A third error, errProtocol, is this file's own. It is a package-level variable, and a caller tests for it with errors.Is. Such a value is called a sentinel error.

Exercise 04.2GoRead the bulk strings

Write the loop in readCommand that reads n bulk strings. For each one, read a line and check that it starts with $. Convert the rest to a size with strconv.Atoi. Then read exactly size+2 bytes with io.ReadFull. Check that the last two bytes are the line ending. Return errProtocol for a wrong byte, and turn an io.EOF inside a request into io.ErrUnexpectedEOF. Treat a size above maxBulk as bad. for range n runs the body n times, and is new in Go 1.22.

func readCommand(r *bufio.Reader) ([][]byte, error) {
	line, err := readLine(r)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}
	if len(line) == 0 || line[0] != '*' {
		return bytes.Fields(line), nil
	}
	n, err := strconv.Atoi(string(line[1:]))
	if err != nil || n < 0 || n > maxArgs {
		return nil, errProtocol
	}
	args := make([][]byte, 0, n)
	for range n {
		// your code here
	}
	return args, nil
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
// readCommand reads one request: an array of bulk strings such as
// "*2\r\n$4\r\nECHO\r\n$2\r\nhi\r\n", or one inline line of words.
func readCommand(r *bufio.Reader) ([][]byte, error) {
	line, err := readLine(r)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}
	if len(line) == 0 || line[0] != '*' {
		return bytes.Fields(line), nil
	}
	n, err := strconv.Atoi(string(line[1:]))
	if err != nil || n < 0 || n > maxArgs {
		return nil, errProtocol
	}
	args := make([][]byte, 0, n)
	for range n {
		line, err := readLine(r)
		if err != nil {
			return nil, unexpected(err)
		}
		if len(line) == 0 || line[0] != '$' {
			return nil, errProtocol
		}
		size, err := strconv.Atoi(string(line[1:]))
		if err != nil || size < 0 || size > maxBulk {
			return nil, errProtocol
		}
		buf := make([]byte, size+2)
		if _, err := io.ReadFull(r, buf); err != nil {
			return nil, unexpected(err)
		}
		if buf[size] != '\r' || buf[size+1] != '\n' {
			return nil, errProtocol
		}
		args = append(args, buf[:size])
	}
	return args, nil
}

// unexpected turns a clean EOF in the middle of a request into an error that says so.
func unexpected(err error) error {
	if err == io.EOF {
		return io.ErrUnexpectedEOF
	}
	return err
}
go/ch04/resp.go · region read-command

Writing replies uses a bufio.Writer, which collects small writes and sends them in one system call when it is flushed:

func writeSimple(w *bufio.Writer, s string) error {
	_, err := w.WriteString("+" + s + "\r\n")
	return err
}

func writeError(w *bufio.Writer, s string) error {
	_, err := w.WriteString("-" + s + "\r\n")
	return err
}

func writeBulk(w *bufio.Writer, b []byte) error {
	if _, err := w.WriteString("$" + strconv.Itoa(len(b)) + "\r\n"); err != nil {
		return err
	}
	if _, err := w.Write(b); err != nil {
		return err
	}
	_, err := w.WriteString("\r\n")
	return err
}

func writeArrayLen(w *bufio.Writer, n int) error {
	_, err := w.WriteString("*" + strconv.Itoa(n) + "\r\n")
	return err
}
go/ch04/resp.go · region write

The command switch is the C chain of comparisons, written as a switch on an upper-cased string. Go strings are immutable and can hold any bytes, so converting an argument to a string costs one copy and nothing else:

// execute runs one command and writes its reply.
func execute(args [][]byte, w *bufio.Writer) error {
	if len(args) == 0 {
		return writeError(w, "ERR empty command")
	}
	name := strings.ToUpper(string(args[0]))
	switch name {
	case "PING":
		switch len(args) {
		case 1:
			return writeSimple(w, "PONG")
		case 2:
			return writeBulk(w, args[1])
		}
		return writeArity(w, "ping")
	case "ECHO":
		if len(args) != 2 {
			return writeArity(w, "echo")
		}
		return writeBulk(w, args[1])
	case "COMMAND":
		return writeArrayLen(w, 0) // redis-cli asks on connect; an empty list is fine
	}
	shown := string(args[0])
	if len(shown) > 40 {
		shown = shown[:40]
	}
	return writeError(w, "ERR unknown command '"+shown+"'")
}

func writeArity(w *bufio.Writer, name string) error {
	return writeError(w, "ERR wrong number of arguments for '"+name+"' command")
}
go/ch04/exec.go · region exec

The serving loop reads a request, runs it, and flushes the writer when the reader has no bytes waiting. That last check is the Go form of the C pipeline loop. While a pipeline is still being read, replies collect in the writer, and go out together:

// serve reads requests, runs them and sends the replies, until the client closes.
func serve(conn net.Conn) error {
	defer conn.Close()
	r := bufio.NewReader(conn)
	w := bufio.NewWriter(conn)
	for {
		args, err := readCommand(r)
		if err == io.EOF {
			return nil
		}
		if errors.Is(err, errProtocol) {
			writeError(w, "ERR Protocol error")
			w.Flush()
			return err
		}
		if err != nil {
			return err
		}
		if err := execute(args, w); err != nil {
			return err
		}
		// A pipeline holds several requests; flush once the reader has nothing waiting.
		if r.Buffered() == 0 {
			if err := w.Flush(); err != nil {
				return err
			}
		}
	}
}
go/ch04/main.go · region serve

The function returns nil on a clean close, and the error otherwise. On a protocol error it first tells the client why. The three tests on err are the Go way of handling three different failures, and the order matters. The specific checks come before the general err != nil.

✅One sentinel per failure a caller must tell apart Go

A caller that needs to know what went wrong compares with errors.Is. A caller that only needs to know that something went wrong tests err != nil. Define a sentinel only for the first kind of caller. errProtocol exists because the serving loop sends a different reply for it. The read error has no sentinel because the loop treats every read error the same way.

Rust: an Option for the end, a Result for the rest#

The Rust reader is the BufRead trait, and read_until does the same as ReadBytes in Go. The line function returns io::Result<Option<Vec<u8>>>, which is a mouthful and exactly right. The outer Result is "did the read fail". The inner Option is "was there a line at all". A clean close is Ok(None), and nobody has to remember that a particular error value means "not really an error":

/// One line without its line ending. A bare LF is accepted as well as CRLF, so that a
/// command typed into nc works. `Ok(None)` means the client closed the connection
/// cleanly between commands.
fn read_line(r: &mut impl BufRead) -> io::Result<Option<Vec<u8>>> {
    let mut line = Vec::new();
    if r.read_until(b'\n', &mut line)? == 0 {
        return Ok(None);
    }
    if line.pop() != Some(b'\n') {
        return Err(io::ErrorKind::UnexpectedEof.into());
    }
    if line.last() == Some(&b'\r') {
        line.pop();
    }
    Ok(Some(line))
}

fn parse_len(digits: &[u8]) -> io::Result<usize> {
    std::str::from_utf8(digits)
        .ok()
        .and_then(|s| s.parse().ok())
        .ok_or_else(protocol_error)
}
rust/ch04/src/resp.rs · region read-line

The ? after read_until returns any read error at once. The error for bad bytes is an io::Error with the kind InvalidData. One small function makes it, so that the serving loop can test the kind. parse_len turns the digits into a number in two steps. Each step returns an Option, and and_then chains them. The final ok_or_else turns a missing number into the protocol error.

Exercise 04.3RustRead the bulk strings

Write the loop in read_command that reads n bulk strings. Read a line with read_line, and treat None as an unexpected end of file. Check the first byte, parse the size, read exactly size + 2 bytes with read_exact, check the last two, then truncate and push. The let ... else at the top shows the shape for a missing line. Treat a size above MAX_BULK as bad.

pub fn read_command(r: &mut impl BufRead) -> io::Result<Option<Command>> {
    let Some(line) = read_line(r)? else {
        return Ok(None);
    };
    if line.first() != Some(&b'*') {
        let words = line.split(|b| *b == b' ').filter(|w| !w.is_empty());
        return Ok(Some(words.map(<[u8]>::to_vec).collect()));
    }
    let n = parse_len(&line[1..])?;
    if n > MAX_ARGS {
        return Err(protocol_error());
    }
    let mut args = Vec::with_capacity(n);
    for _ in 0..n {
        // your code here
    }
    Ok(Some(args))
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
/// One request: an array of bulk strings such as `*2\r\n$4\r\nECHO\r\n$2\r\nhi\r\n`,
/// or one inline line of words. `Ok(None)` means the client has gone.
pub fn read_command(r: &mut impl BufRead) -> io::Result<Option<Command>> {
    let Some(line) = read_line(r)? else {
        return Ok(None);
    };
    if line.first() != Some(&b'*') {
        let words = line.split(|b| *b == b' ').filter(|w| !w.is_empty());
        return Ok(Some(words.map(<[u8]>::to_vec).collect()));
    }
    let n = parse_len(&line[1..])?;
    if n > MAX_ARGS {
        return Err(protocol_error());
    }
    let mut args = Vec::with_capacity(n);
    for _ in 0..n {
        let line = read_line(r)?.ok_or(io::ErrorKind::UnexpectedEof)?;
        if line.first() != Some(&b'$') {
            return Err(protocol_error());
        }
        let size = parse_len(&line[1..])?;
        if size > MAX_BULK {
            return Err(protocol_error());
        }
        let mut buf = vec![0u8; size + 2];
        r.read_exact(&mut buf)?;
        if &buf[size..] != b"\r\n" {
            return Err(protocol_error());
        }
        buf.truncate(size);
        args.push(buf);
    }
    Ok(Some(args))
}
rust/ch04/src/resp.rs · region read-command

The reply functions take impl Write, which means any writer, and the write! macro formats straight into it:

pub fn write_simple(w: &mut impl Write, s: &str) -> io::Result<()> {
    write!(w, "+{s}\r\n")
}

pub fn write_error(w: &mut impl Write, s: &str) -> io::Result<()> {
    write!(w, "-{s}\r\n")
}

pub fn write_bulk(w: &mut impl Write, b: &[u8]) -> io::Result<()> {
    write!(w, "${}\r\n", b.len())?;
    w.write_all(b)?;
    w.write_all(b"\r\n")
}

pub fn write_array_len(w: &mut impl Write, n: usize) -> io::Result<()> {
    write!(w, "*{n}\r\n")
}
rust/ch04/src/resp.rs · region write

Running a command is a match on the upper-cased name as bytes. The let ... else handles an empty request. A bulk string is Vec<u8> and not String, because a Rust String must be valid UTF-8 and a Redis value need not be. When the name must be shown in an error message, from_utf8_lossy replaces any invalid bytes with a marker:

/// Run one command and write its reply.
pub fn execute(args: &[Vec<u8>], w: &mut impl Write) -> io::Result<()> {
    let Some(name) = args.first() else {
        return resp::write_error(w, "ERR empty command");
    };
    match name.to_ascii_uppercase().as_slice() {
        b"PING" => match args.len() {
            1 => resp::write_simple(w, "PONG"),
            2 => resp::write_bulk(w, &args[1]),
            _ => write_arity(w, "ping"),
        },
        b"ECHO" => match args.len() {
            2 => resp::write_bulk(w, &args[1]),
            _ => write_arity(w, "echo"),
        },
        // redis-cli asks on connect; an empty list is fine
        b"COMMAND" => resp::write_array_len(w, 0),
        _ => {
            let shown = String::from_utf8_lossy(&name[..name.len().min(40)]);
            resp::write_error(w, &format!("ERR unknown command '{shown}'"))
        }
    }
}

fn write_arity(w: &mut impl Write, name: &str) -> io::Result<()> {
    resp::write_error(
        w,
        &format!("ERR wrong number of arguments for '{name}' command"),
    )
}
rust/ch04/src/cmd.rs · region exec

The serving loop matches on the three outcomes of read_command. The arm with if e.kind() == ... is called a match guard, and it is the Rust form of errors.Is. The compiler checks that the four arms cover every case:

/// Read requests, run them and send the replies, until the client closes.
fn serve(stream: TcpStream) -> io::Result<()> {
    let mut reader = BufReader::new(stream.try_clone()?);
    let mut writer = BufWriter::new(stream);
    loop {
        match resp::read_command(&mut reader) {
            Ok(Some(args)) => cmd::execute(&args, &mut writer)?,
            Ok(None) => return Ok(()),
            Err(e) if e.kind() == io::ErrorKind::InvalidData => {
                resp::write_error(&mut writer, "ERR Protocol error")?;
                writer.flush()?;
                return Err(e);
            }
            Err(e) => return Err(e),
        }
        // A pipeline holds several requests; flush once the reader has nothing waiting.
        if reader.buffer().is_empty() {
            writer.flush()?;
        }
    }
}
rust/ch04/src/main.rs · region serve
⚠️A String is not a byte string Rust

String::from_utf8(bytes) fails on a value that is not text. Redis keys and values are bytes, and a client may store an image under a key. A parser that converts every argument to String rejects such a client with an error that looks like a bug in the client. Keep arguments as Vec<u8> and convert only for display, with from_utf8_lossy, or for commands that need text, such as a number.

Three ways to say what went wrong#

Each version of the parser had to express four different outcomes. The request is complete. The bytes are not RESP. The client closed the connection cleanly. The client closed it in the middle of a request. The table shows the three vocabularies.

The outcomeCGoRust
A value, or a failurea return code and an out-parametertwo return values, (v, err)one value, Result<T, E>
The reason for a failurethe global errno, or an enum of your ownan error value, compared with errors.Isthe Err payload, matched on its kind or type
Passing a failure upif (x < 0) return -1; on every callif err != nil { return err } on every callone character, ?
Ignoring a failurewrite nothing, and nothing warnswrite nothing, and nothing warnsthe compiler warns about an unused Result
"Not an error, but nothing"a special return value, such as 0 from reada special error value, io.EOFOption, which is not an error at all
Adding a reasona format string into a fixed arrayfmt.Errorf("...: %w", err)io::Error::new(kind, msg), or an enum of your own

C has one mechanism, the return value, and uses it for everything. The design works, and the whole of Unix is built on it, but it has two costs that the book will keep meeting. The first is that the check is optional and silent when missing. The second is that the value and the error share one channel. A function that returns a pointer uses NULL for failure. A function that returns a count uses -1. The caller must know which.

Go separates the value from the error, which fixes the second cost, and leaves the first in place by choice. The Go FAQ says the designers found try-catch-finally produced "convoluted code". The price is the three lines of if err != nil after every call, and a Go file of any size has many such checks. Rust puts the value inside the Result, so that the caller cannot reach the value without facing the failure. The ? operator makes the common case one character. The price is a type system that the programmer must learn before any of it makes sense. There is also a long error message from the compiler when a ? is used where the error types do not match.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
Reading bytesWeakStrongStrongC has stdio, which blocks, so kv grows its own buffer. Both standard libraries have one.
Holding the argumentsStrongOKOKThe C parser copies nothing. Go and Rust copy each argument once, which is simpler and almost never matters.
Saying "not RESP"OKOKStrongC and Go use a value by convention. Rust uses a kind the compiler knows about and a match the compiler checks.
Saying "the client left"OKWeakStrongGo reuses an error value for a non-error. Rust has Option for exactly this.
Passing an error upWeakWeakStrongC and Go repeat the check at every call. Rust writes one character.
Binary valuesStrongStrongOKC and Go treat all bytes alike. Rust makes you choose between String and Vec<u8>, and the wrong choice rejects real data.
Next · Building kv05GET and SET20 min

GET and SET#

Building kv · Chapter 05· 20 min read· 390 lines of code

kv starts to remember. SET stores a value under a key and GET returns it. Two commands take several keys, DEL and EXISTS. INCR treats a value as a number. The chapter is about strings and bytes, because a key and a value are bytes, and the three languages disagree about what a string is.

A Redis value is a sequence of bytes. It may be the text hello, the digits 42, or the first kilobyte of a JPEG. A Redis key is the same. The server must keep a copy of both and find a key again later. It must compare two keys for equality. For INCR it must read a value as an integer and write one back. Each of those steps asks the question "what is a string?", and each language answers it differently.

A stringCGoRust
What it isa pointer to bytes that end with a zero bytean immutable sequence of bytesa sequence of bytes that must be valid UTF-8
Bytes with a lengtha pointer and a size_t, by convention[]byteVec<u8>, or a borrowed &[u8]
Lengthstrlen walks to the zero, or you carry the lengthlen(s), stored with the valuev.len(), stored with the value
Equalitymemcmp with the lengths== on strings, bytes.Equal on slices== on both
As a map keya hash you write, in chapter 6string, because a slice cannot be a keyVec<u8>, because it is hashable
To an integeryou parse the digits, watching for overflowstrconv.ParseIntstr::parse::<i64>() after a UTF-8 check
Who owns the byteswhoever the comment saysthe garbage collectorexactly one binding, checked by the compiler
📜Why a C string ends with a zero byte

In BCPL, the language before B, a string began with its length in one byte. B dropped the count and ended a string with a marker character instead, and C kept that rule with the zero byte as the marker. Ritchie gave two reasons. The one-byte count limited a string to 255 characters, and "maintaining the count seemed, in our experience, less convenient than using a terminator". Fifty years later, that marker is why a C string cannot hold a zero byte. It is also why every C string function walks the whole string to find its end. Go and Rust store the length with the bytes, as BCPL did.

C: bytes, a length and an owner#

The arguments that the parser produces are not C strings. They are pointers into the request buffer with a length beside them, and no zero byte ends them. Every function in kv that touches a key or a value takes a pointer and a length. The C string library is never called on them. The first helpers copy, compare and parse such bytes:

/* A copy of n bytes that the caller owns and must free. Never NULL on success, even
 * for n == 0, because malloc(0) may return NULL and that would look like failure. */
static char *kv_bytes_dup(const char *src, size_t n) {
    char *p = malloc(n ? n : 1);
    if (p == NULL) {
        return NULL;
    }
    memcpy(p, src, n);
    return p;
}

static bool kv_bytes_eq(const char *a, size_t alen, const char *b, size_t blen) {
    return alen == blen && memcmp(a, b, alen) == 0;
}

/* Parse a signed decimal integer from bytes that have no terminating zero. Strict, as
 * Redis is: no spaces, no plus sign, no leading zeros, and no overflow. */
static bool kv_parse_int(const char *s, size_t n, long long *out) {
    size_t i = 0;
    bool neg = false;
    if (n == 0) {
        return false;
    }
    if (s[0] == '-') {
        neg = true;
        i = 1;
        if (n == 1) {
            return false;
        }
    }
    if (s[i] == '0' && n - i > 1) {
        return false;
    }
    unsigned long long v = 0;
    for (; i < n; i++) {
        if (s[i] < '0' || s[i] > '9') {
            return false;
        }
        unsigned d = (unsigned)(s[i] - '0');
        if (v > (ULLONG_MAX - d) / 10) {
            return false;
        }
        v = v * 10 + d;
    }
    if (!neg && v > (unsigned long long)LLONG_MAX) {
        return false;
    }
    if (neg && v > (unsigned long long)LLONG_MAX + 1) {
        return false;
    }
    *out = neg ? -(long long)(v - 1) - 1 : (long long)v;
    return true;
}
c/ch05/kv.c · region bytes

Three details deserve a look. kv_bytes_dup asks for at least one byte. The reason is that malloc(0) is allowed to return NULL, which would look like an out-of-memory failure. The integer parser works in unsigned arithmetic and checks before every multiplication, because overflow of a signed integer is undefined behaviour in C. The last line turns the unsigned result into a negative number. It avoids computing -v when v is one more than the largest positive value. That value is exactly the smallest negative one, and the obvious expression would overflow on the way there.

⚠️strlen on bytes that are not a C string C

Calling a string function on a key reads past the key. The key hello in a request is followed by \r\n and the rest of the buffer, not by a zero byte. strlen walks on until it finds a zero somewhere, and strcmp compares bytes that belong to another argument. Most of the time there is no crash. Both are undefined behaviour, and AddressSanitizer, which chapter 11 introduces, reports such a read. The rule in kv is simple. A key or a value is always a pointer and a length, and string.h is for messages the program writes itself.

The store in this chapter is a list of entries. A lookup walks the list and compares each key, which is slow once there are thousands of keys. Chapter 6 replaces the list with a hash table. The entry owns copies of its key and value, and the comments say so:

/* One key and its value. Both are owned copies, freed with the entry. */
typedef struct {
    char *key;
    size_t klen;
    char *val;
    size_t vlen;
} kv_entry;

/* Every entry, in a list. A lookup walks the whole list, which chapter 6 fixes. */
typedef struct {
    kv_entry *items; /* owned */
    size_t len;
    size_t cap;
} kv_store;

static kv_entry *kv_store_find(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen) {
    for (size_t i = 0; i < s->len; i++) {
        if (kv_bytes_eq(s->items[i].key, s->items[i].klen, key, klen)) {
            return &s->items[i];
        }
    }
    return NULL;
}
c/ch05/kv.c · region store
Exercise 05.1CStore a copy

Write kv_store_set. Copy the value first, because that can fail. If the key exists, free its old value and keep the new one. If not, grow the list when it is full, copy the key, and append an entry. On any failure, free what you copied and return false, leaving the store as it was.

static bool kv_store_set(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen, const char *val,
                         size_t vlen) {
    char *v = kv_bytes_dup(val, vlen);
    if (v == NULL) return false;
    /* your code here */
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
/* Store a copy of val under a copy of key, replacing any old value. */
static bool kv_store_set(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen, const char *val,
                         size_t vlen) {
    char *v = kv_bytes_dup(val, vlen);
    if (v == NULL) {
        return false;
    }
    kv_entry *e = kv_store_find(s, key, klen);
    if (e != NULL) {
        free(e->val); /* the old value, which nothing else points to */
        e->val = v;
        e->vlen = vlen;
        return true;
    }
    if (s->len == s->cap) {
        size_t cap = s->cap ? s->cap * 2 : 16;
        kv_entry *items = realloc(s->items, cap * sizeof *items);
        if (items == NULL) {
            free(v);
            return false;
        }
        s->items = items;
        s->cap = cap;
    }
    char *k = kv_bytes_dup(key, klen);
    if (k == NULL) {
        free(v);
        return false;
    }
    s->items[s->len++] = (kv_entry){k, klen, v, vlen};
    return true;
}

/* Remove a key. The last entry moves into the hole, so the order is not kept. */
static bool kv_store_del(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen) {
    kv_entry *e = kv_store_find(s, key, klen);
    if (e == NULL) {
        return false;
    }
    free(e->key);
    free(e->val);
    *e = s->items[--s->len];
    return true;
}

static void kv_store_free(kv_store *s) {
    for (size_t i = 0; i < s->len; i++) {
        free(s->items[i].key);
        free(s->items[i].val);
    }
    free(s->items);
    s->items = NULL;
    s->len = s->cap = 0;
}
c/ch05/kv.c · region store-ops

kv_store_del moves the last entry into the hole that the deleted one leaves. That keeps the list packed without moving everything after it, at the cost of order, which a store does not promise. A value is freed in three places. kv_store_set frees the old one on replacement, kv_store_del frees it, and at exit kv_store_free walks the list once. Every malloc in this file has one free that matches it, and a reader can find the pair.

INCR is where bytes and numbers meet. The stored value is bytes. The command parses them, adds one, and stores bytes again. The error messages are the ones Redis uses, so that a client written for Redis understands kv:

/* True when argument i of the command is name, ignoring case. */
static bool kv_arg_is(const kv_cmd *c, size_t i, const char *name) {
    size_t n = strlen(name);
    return c->arglen[i] == n && strncasecmp(c->argv[i], name, n) == 0;
}

static bool kv_reply_arity(kv_buf *out, const char *name) {
    char msg[96];
    snprintf(msg, sizeof msg, "ERR wrong number of arguments for '%s' command", name);
    return kv_reply_error(out, msg);
}

/* INCR: the value is bytes that must parse as an integer, and the new value is bytes too. */
static bool kv_exec_incr(const kv_cmd *c, kv_store *s, kv_buf *out) {
    long long n = 0;
    kv_entry *e = kv_store_find(s, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1]);
    if (e != NULL && !kv_parse_int(e->val, e->vlen, &n)) {
        return kv_reply_error(out, "ERR value is not an integer or out of range");
    }
    if (n == LLONG_MAX) {
        return kv_reply_error(out, "ERR increment or decrement would overflow");
    }
    n++;
    char text[32];
    int len = snprintf(text, sizeof text, "%lld", n);
    if (!kv_store_set(s, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1], text, (size_t)len)) {
        return false;
    }
    return kv_reply_int(out, n);
}

/* Run one command against the store and append its reply to out. */
static bool kv_exec(const kv_cmd *c, kv_store *s, kv_buf *out) {
    if (c->argc == 0) {
        return kv_reply_error(out, "ERR empty command");
    }
    if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "PING")) {
        if (c->argc == 1) {
            return kv_reply_simple(out, "PONG");
        }
        if (c->argc == 2) {
            return kv_reply_bulk(out, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1]);
        }
        return kv_reply_arity(out, "ping");
    }
    if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "ECHO")) {
        if (c->argc != 2) {
            return kv_reply_arity(out, "echo");
        }
        return kv_reply_bulk(out, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1]);
    }
    if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "SET")) {
        if (c->argc != 3) {
            return kv_reply_arity(out, "set");
        }
        if (!kv_store_set(s, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1], c->argv[2], c->arglen[2])) {
            return false;
        }
        return kv_reply_simple(out, "OK");
    }
    if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "GET")) {
        if (c->argc != 2) {
            return kv_reply_arity(out, "get");
        }
        kv_entry *e = kv_store_find(s, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1]);
        return e ? kv_reply_bulk(out, e->val, e->vlen) : kv_reply_nil(out);
    }
    if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "DEL") || kv_arg_is(c, 0, "EXISTS")) {
        if (c->argc < 2) {
            return kv_reply_arity(out, kv_arg_is(c, 0, "DEL") ? "del" : "exists");
        }
        long long n = 0;
        for (size_t i = 1; i < c->argc; i++) {
            if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "DEL")) {
                n += kv_store_del(s, c->argv[i], c->arglen[i]);
            } else {
                n += kv_store_find(s, c->argv[i], c->arglen[i]) != NULL;
            }
        }
        return kv_reply_int(out, n);
    }
    if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "INCR")) {
        if (c->argc != 2) {
            return kv_reply_arity(out, "incr");
        }
        return kv_exec_incr(c, s, out);
    }
    if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "COMMAND")) {
        return kv_reply_array(out, 0);
    }
    char msg[96];
    int n = c->arglen[0] < 40 ? (int)c->arglen[0] : 40;
    snprintf(msg, sizeof msg, "ERR unknown command '%.*s'", n, c->argv[0]);
    return kv_reply_error(out, msg);
}
