$ make && ./kv &
$ redis-cli -p 6380
127.0.0.1:6380> SET name kv

127.0.0.1:6380> GET name
"kv"
127.0.0.1:6380> INCR visits

(integer) 2
✅A pointer and a length travel together C

Any function that takes bytes takes two parameters, the pointer and the length, in that order. A structure that holds bytes holds both fields side by side. The habit is older than kv. The read and write calls of POSIX, the Portable Operating System Interface, take a pointer and a length. Redis keeps a length in front of every string it stores. A C string is only for text the program itself writes, such as an error message, where the zero byte is guaranteed.

Go: two kinds of string, and a copy between them#

Go has both answers to the question. A string is immutable bytes with a length. A []byte is mutable bytes with a length. Converting one to the other copies the bytes, because the string must not change when the slice does. The store uses a string for the key, because a map key must support ==, and a slice does not. The value stays a slice:

// store keeps every key and its value. The parser allocated each slice, so the store can
// keep them without copying: nothing else holds them.
type store struct {
	m map[string][]byte
}

func newStore() *store {
	return &store{m: make(map[string][]byte)}
}

func (s *store) get(key []byte) ([]byte, bool) {
	v, ok := s.m[string(key)]
	return v, ok
}

func (s *store) set(key, val []byte) {
	s.m[string(key)] = val
}

func (s *store) del(key []byte) bool {
	_, ok := s.m[string(key)]
	delete(s.m, string(key))
	return ok
}
go/ch05/store.go · region store

The value is not copied on set. The key is converted to a string, which copies it once, because a map key must be comparable. The parser allocated a fresh slice for every argument, and nothing else holds it. The garbage collector keeps it alive for as long as the map refers to it. The question that took a paragraph of comments in C does not arise. The cost is in the collector, which must find out, from time to time, which slices are still referred to.

// The two ways INCR fails. Their text is what the client sees.
var (
	errNotInteger = errors.New("ERR value is not an integer or out of range")
	errOverflow   = errors.New("ERR increment or decrement would overflow")
)
go/ch05/store.go · region errors
Exercise 05.2GoAdd one to a value

Write the incr method. Read the current value if there is one, parse it with strconv.ParseInt, and return errNotInteger when parsing fails. Return errOverflow when the value is already math.MaxInt64. Otherwise store the new value, formatted with strconv.FormatInt, and return it.

func (s *store) incr(key []byte) (int64, error) {
	var n int64
	// your code here
	return n, nil
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
// incr adds one to the integer stored under key, which counts as 0 when absent.
func (s *store) incr(key []byte) (int64, error) {
	var n int64
	if cur, ok := s.get(key); ok {
		var err error
		n, err = strconv.ParseInt(string(cur), 10, 64)
		if err != nil {
			return 0, errNotInteger
		}
	}
	if n == math.MaxInt64 {
		return 0, errOverflow
	}
	n++
	s.set(key, []byte(strconv.FormatInt(n, 10)))
	return n, nil
}
go/ch05/store.go · region incr

The two errors are package-level values with the exact text the client should see. The command switch does not look inside them. It writes err.Error() as the reply, which keeps the knowledge of what can go wrong next to the code that knows it:

// execute runs one command against the store and writes its reply.
func execute(args [][]byte, s *store, w *bufio.Writer) error {
	if len(args) == 0 {
		return writeError(w, "ERR empty command")
	}
	name := strings.ToUpper(string(args[0]))
	switch name {
	case "PING":
		switch len(args) {
		case 1:
			return writeSimple(w, "PONG")
		case 2:
			return writeBulk(w, args[1])
		}
		return writeArity(w, "ping")
	case "ECHO":
		if len(args) != 2 {
			return writeArity(w, "echo")
		}
		return writeBulk(w, args[1])
	case "SET":
		if len(args) != 3 {
			return writeArity(w, "set")
		}
		s.set(args[1], args[2])
		return writeSimple(w, "OK")
	case "GET":
		if len(args) != 2 {
			return writeArity(w, "get")
		}
		if v, ok := s.get(args[1]); ok {
			return writeBulk(w, v)
		}
		return writeNil(w)
	case "DEL", "EXISTS":
		if len(args) < 2 {
			return writeArity(w, strings.ToLower(name))
		}
		n := 0
		for _, key := range args[1:] {
			var hit bool
			if name == "DEL" {
				hit = s.del(key)
			} else {
				_, hit = s.get(key)
			}
			if hit {
				n++
			}
		}
		return writeInt(w, int64(n))
	case "INCR":
		if len(args) != 2 {
			return writeArity(w, "incr")
		}
		n, err := s.incr(args[1])
		if err != nil {
			return writeError(w, err.Error())
		}
		return writeInt(w, n)
	case "COMMAND":
		return writeArrayLen(w, 0)
	}
	shown := string(args[0])
	if len(shown) > 40 {
		shown = shown[:40]
	}
	return writeError(w, "ERR unknown command '"+shown+"'")
}

func writeArity(w *bufio.Writer, name string) error {
	return writeError(w, "ERR wrong number of arguments for '"+name+"' command")
}
go/ch05/exec.go · region exec
⚠️ParseInt is more generous than Redis Go

strconv.ParseInt accepts +5 and 007. Redis stores both and then refuses to INCR them, and the C version of kv does the same. The Go version accepts them, and so does the Rust version, because both standard libraries parse numbers the same generous way. The book leaves the difference in and tells you about it, because that is what happens in real ports. A strict parser in Go is a short function, and the C version shows what it must check.

Rust: a value has one owner#

Rust has the same two kinds as Go, String for text and Vec<u8> for bytes, with one more rule. A String must be valid UTF-8, and the compiler will not let you build one from bytes without a check. A Redis key is bytes, so the store is a map from Vec<u8> to Vec<u8>. A Vec can be a key because it supports hashing and equality:

/// Every key and its value. The store owns both: a key or value that is put in is moved,
/// not copied, and is freed when it is replaced, deleted or the store is dropped.
#[derive(Default)]
pub struct Store {
    map: HashMap<Vec<u8>, Vec<u8>>,
}

impl Store {
    pub fn new() -> Self {
        Self::default()
    }

    /// A borrowed view of the value, which lives as long as the borrow of the store.
    pub fn get(&self, key: &[u8]) -> Option<&[u8]> {
        self.map.get(key).map(Vec::as_slice)
    }

    pub fn set(&mut self, key: Vec<u8>, val: Vec<u8>) {
        self.map.insert(key, val);
    }

    pub fn del(&mut self, key: &[u8]) -> bool {
        self.map.remove(key).is_some()
    }
}
rust/ch05/src/store.rs · region store

Two signatures carry the whole ownership story. get takes &self and returns Option<&[u8]>, a borrowed view that lives only as long as the borrow of the store. set takes Vec<u8> by value, which means the caller gives the bytes away. Nothing is copied, and nothing can be used twice.

The command function takes its arguments by value for the same reason. For SET, mem::take moves the key and the value out of the argument list and leaves empty vectors behind. The store receives them without a copy:

/// Run one command against the store and write its reply. The command is taken by value,
/// so that SET can move the key and the value into the store instead of copying them.
pub fn execute(mut args: Command, store: &mut Store, w: &mut impl Write) -> io::Result<()> {
    let Some(name) = args.first() else {
        return resp::write_error(w, "ERR empty command");
    };
    let name = name.to_ascii_uppercase();
    match (name.as_slice(), args.as_mut_slice()) {
        (b"PING", [_]) => resp::write_simple(w, "PONG"),
        (b"PING", [_, msg]) => resp::write_bulk(w, msg),
        (b"PING", _) => write_arity(w, "ping"),
        (b"ECHO", [_, msg]) => resp::write_bulk(w, msg),
        (b"ECHO", _) => write_arity(w, "echo"),
        (b"SET", [_, key, val]) => {
            store.set(mem::take(key), mem::take(val));
            resp::write_simple(w, "OK")
        }
        (b"SET", _) => write_arity(w, "set"),
        (b"GET", [_, key]) => match store.get(key) {
            Some(val) => resp::write_bulk(w, val),
            None => resp::write_nil(w),
        },
        (b"GET", _) => write_arity(w, "get"),
        (b"DEL", [_, keys @ ..]) if !keys.is_empty() => {
            let n = keys.iter().filter(|k| store.del(k)).count();
            resp::write_int(w, n as i64)
        }
        (b"DEL", _) => write_arity(w, "del"),
        (b"EXISTS", [_, keys @ ..]) if !keys.is_empty() => {
            let n = keys.iter().filter(|k| store.get(k).is_some()).count();
            resp::write_int(w, n as i64)
        }
        (b"EXISTS", _) => write_arity(w, "exists"),
        (b"INCR", [_, key]) => match store.incr(key) {
            Ok(n) => resp::write_int(w, n),
            Err(e) => resp::write_error(w, e.message()),
        },
        (b"INCR", _) => write_arity(w, "incr"),
        (b"COMMAND", _) => resp::write_array_len(w, 0),
        _ => {
            let shown = String::from_utf8_lossy(&args[0][..args[0].len().min(40)]);
            resp::write_error(w, &format!("ERR unknown command '{shown}'"))
        }
    }
}

fn write_arity(w: &mut impl Write, name: &str) -> io::Result<()> {
    resp::write_error(
        w,
        &format!("ERR wrong number of arguments for '{name}' command"),
    )
}
rust/ch05/src/cmd.rs · region exec

The match is on a pair, the command name and the argument slice. So one arm can say "GET with exactly one key". The next arm can say "GET with anything else". The pattern [_, keys @ ..] binds the rest of the slice, which is how DEL takes any number of keys.

/// Why INCR could not add one.
pub enum IncrError {
    NotInteger,
    Overflow,
}

impl IncrError {
    pub fn message(&self) -> &'static str {
        match self {
            Self::NotInteger => "ERR value is not an integer or out of range",
            Self::Overflow => "ERR increment or decrement would overflow",
        }
    }
}
/// The bytes as a signed 64-bit integer, if that is what they are.
fn parse_int(bytes: &[u8]) -> Option<i64> {
    std::str::from_utf8(bytes).ok()?.parse().ok()
}

Exercise 05.3RustAdd one to a value

Write the incr method. Use parse_int for the current value. Turn its None into IncrError::NotInteger with ok_or. Use checked_add, which returns None on overflow, and turn that into IncrError::Overflow. Store the new value with to_string and into_bytes, and return it.

pub fn incr(&mut self, key: &[u8]) -> Result<i64, IncrError> {
    let n = match self.get(key) {
        None => 0,
        Some(bytes) => parse_int(bytes).ok_or(IncrError::NotInteger)?,
    };
    // your code here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
impl Store {
    /// Add one to the integer stored under key, which counts as 0 when absent.
    pub fn incr(&mut self, key: &[u8]) -> Result<i64, IncrError> {
        let n = match self.get(key) {
            None => 0,
            Some(bytes) => parse_int(bytes).ok_or(IncrError::NotInteger)?,
        };
        let n = n.checked_add(1).ok_or(IncrError::Overflow)?;
        self.set(key.to_vec(), n.to_string().into_bytes());
        Ok(n)
    }
}
rust/ch05/src/store.rs · region incr

IncrError is an enum with two cases, and the command code matches on it to write the reply. This is the Rust form of the two Go sentinel values. The difference is that a match on an enum must handle every case. So a third kind of failure, added later, is a compile error at every place that forgot it.

⚠️You cannot keep a value and change the store Rust

A borrowed value blocks every write to the store until it is dropped. The following three lines do not compile, and the compiler says why:

let old = store.get(b"a");
store.set(b"a".to_vec(), b"2".to_vec());
println!("{:?}", old);
error[E0502]: cannot borrow `store` as mutable because it is also borrowed as immutable
   |     let old = store.get(b"a");
   |               ----- immutable borrow occurs here
   |     store.set(b"a".to_vec(), b"2".to_vec());
   |     ^^^^^ mutable borrow occurs here
   |     println!("{:?}", old);
   |                      --- immutable borrow later used here

In C the same three lines compile, and old points at freed memory after the set. The Rust error is the C bug, caught before the program runs. When you need the old value after a write, copy it first with to_vec(). That is what the C code did with kv_bytes_dup, and it is what the compiler is asking for.

Who owns the bytes#

Every version of SET had to answer one question. The bytes arrived in a request buffer. Who keeps them, and who frees them? C answered with a copy and a comment. The store makes its own copy with kv_bytes_dup and frees it on replacement. The comment on the field is the only record of the rule. Go answered with the collector. The store keeps the parser's slice. The collector frees it when nothing refers to it any more, at a time the program does not choose. Rust answered with a move. The store takes the bytes from the argument list, which is left empty. The compiler refuses any later use of what was moved.

All three answers work. The C answer costs a copy and a rule the compiler does not know. The Go answer costs memory until the next collection and a pause when it runs. The Rust answer costs nothing at run time. It costs a type signature that says Vec<u8> in place of the char * of C. Chapter 6 makes the costs larger, because the store becomes a structure that must free and move things itself.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
A key is bytesOKStrongOKC carries a length beside every pointer, Go has []byte and string, and Rust makes you pick Vec<u8> because String rejects binary data.
Copying and owningWeakStrongStrongC copies and comments, Go lets the collector own everything, and Rust moves and checks.
Parsing a numberWeakOKOKC needs a careful parser to avoid undefined behaviour. The other two accept forms Redis rejects.
OverflowWeakOKStrongC must check before it adds and Go checks against a constant, while Rust has checked_add built in.
A missing caseWeakWeakStrongAn enum match in Rust must be complete. In C and Go, a new error kind can be forgotten at a call site.
Reading the codeOKStrongOKThe Go store is the shortest. The Rust signatures say more and take longer to read.
Next · Building kv06Your own hash table20 min

Your own hash table#

Building kv · Chapter 06· 20 min read· 349 lines of code

The list from chapter 5 gets slower with every key. This chapter replaces it with a hash table that you write yourself, in all three languages. In C the table becomes the store. In Go and Rust the standard map stays, and your table stands beside it. The chapter is about memory, because a hash table must allocate, move and free its own parts.

With 20,000 keys in the store, the C server from chapter 5 answers about 4,400 GET requests a second from one client. Each lookup walks half the list on average. The same server with the hash table from this chapter answers about 19,400. That is the measurement on the author's machine, and chapter 12 shows how to take it yourself. The difference grows with the number of keys, because the list takes time in proportion to the keys and the table does not.

A hash table turns a key into a number, called its hash. The low bits of that number are an index into an array of buckets. Two keys may land in the same bucket, so each bucket holds a chain of entries, and a lookup walks that short chain. When the table holds more entries than buckets, the array doubles and every entry moves to its new bucket. The chapter also adds two commands that ask about the whole table, DBSIZE and KEYS.

The tableCGoRust
A bucketa pointer to the first entry of a chaina slice of entriesa Vec of entries
A chain linka next pointer the entry ownsthe next element of the slicethe next element of the Vec
Growingcalloc a bigger array, relink every entrymake a bigger slice, append every entrydrain every chain into a bigger Vec
Deletingunlink, then free the key, the value and the entrymove the last entry into the holeswap_remove, which drops the entry
What frees the old arrayyou, with freethe garbage collector, laterthe old Vec is dropped at the end of the function
The hashFNV-1a, written hereFNV-1a, written hereFNV-1a here, SipHash in the standard map
Who would use iteveryone, because C has no mapnobody, the map is built innobody, HashMap is built in
📜Why Redis rehashes a little at a time

Doubling the array is the simplest way to grow a table, and it is what this chapter does. It has a cost. The move of every entry takes a time proportional to the table, and a Redis server with ten million keys cannot stop for that long. So the Redis dictionary keeps two arrays during a resize and moves a few buckets on every operation. The function is dictRehash(dict *d, int n) in dict.c, and a field named rehashidx records how far the move has gone. The built-in map of Go also grows incrementally. It splits a large map into several small tables that grow one at a time. The server kv needs no such trick, and the simple version is easier to see.

C: an array of chains, and every pointer accounted for#

The hash is FNV-1a, a few lines that multiply and exclusive-or. It is fast and spreads keys well, and it is not safe against an attacker who chooses keys that collide. The Rust section says why that matters:

/* FNV-1a: a small, fast hash of bytes. Not safe against an attacker who chooses keys
 * to collide, which Redis guards against with a secret seed. Chapter 11 returns to it. */
static uint64_t kv_hash(const char *s, size_t n) {
    uint64_t h = 14695981039346656037ULL;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        h ^= (unsigned char)s[i];
        h *= 1099511628211ULL;
    }
    return h;
}
c/ch06/kv.c · region hash

An entry now has a next pointer, and the comment says that the entry owns it. That one word decides who frees a chain. The table is an array of chain heads. The count of buckets is always a power of two, so that the index is the hash masked with cap - 1 instead of a division:

/* One key and its value, in a chain of entries that share a bucket. The key and the
 * value are owned copies. The next pointer is owned too: freeing an entry frees its
 * chain. */
typedef struct kv_entry {
    char *key;
    size_t klen;
    char *val;
    size_t vlen;
    struct kv_entry *next;
} kv_entry;

/* A hash table with separate chaining. buckets[i] is the head of a chain, or NULL. The
 * table grows when it holds more entries than buckets, so a chain stays short. */
typedef struct {
    kv_entry **buckets; /* owned array of cap pointers */
    size_t cap;         /* a power of two, or 0 before the first insert */
    size_t len;         /* how many entries, over all chains */
} kv_store;

static kv_entry *kv_store_find(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen) {
    if (s->cap == 0) {
        return NULL;
    }
    size_t i = kv_hash(key, klen) & (s->cap - 1);
    for (kv_entry *e = s->buckets[i]; e != NULL; e = e->next) {
        if (kv_bytes_eq(e->key, e->klen, key, klen)) {
            return e;
        }
    }
    return NULL;
}
c/ch06/kv.c · region table

Growing allocates a new array with calloc, which returns memory set to zero, so every new bucket starts as an empty chain. Then it walks every old chain and pushes each entry onto the front of its new one. No key or value is copied. Only the next pointers change, which is the point of keeping entries as separate allocations:

/* Double the bucket array and move every entry to its new chain. No entry is copied or
 * freed: only the next pointers change. */
static bool kv_store_grow(kv_store *s) {
    size_t cap = s->cap ? s->cap * 2 : 16;
    kv_entry **buckets = calloc(cap, sizeof *buckets);
    if (buckets == NULL) {
        return false;
    }
    for (size_t i = 0; i < s->cap; i++) {
        kv_entry *e = s->buckets[i];
        while (e != NULL) {
            kv_entry *next = e->next; /* read it before the chain is changed */
            size_t j = kv_hash(e->key, e->klen) & (cap - 1);
            e->next = buckets[j];
            buckets[j] = e;
            e = next;
        }
    }
    free(s->buckets);
    s->buckets = buckets;
    s->cap = cap;
    return true;
}
c/ch06/kv.c · region grow
⚠️Read next before you change it C

The line kv_entry *next = e->next must come first. The loop sets e->next to the head of the new chain, and after that the old chain is gone. Swap the two lines and the loop follows the new chain instead of the old one. It loses every entry after the first, and ends in a cycle or a crash. The compiler accepts both orders. Nothing but your reading of the code tells them apart.

Inserting has three allocations that can each fail, and the cleanup must free exactly the ones that succeeded. The code calls free on every pointer, because free(NULL) does nothing. That rule makes the failure path three lines instead of a tree of conditions:

/* Store a copy of val under a copy of key, replacing any old value. */
static bool kv_store_set(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen, const char *val,
                         size_t vlen) {
    char *v = kv_bytes_dup(val, vlen);
    if (v == NULL) {
        return false;
    }
    kv_entry *e = kv_store_find(s, key, klen);
    if (e != NULL) {
        free(e->val);
        e->val = v;
        e->vlen = vlen;
        return true;
    }
    if (s->len >= s->cap && !kv_store_grow(s)) {
        free(v);
        return false;
    }
    e = malloc(sizeof *e);
    char *k = kv_bytes_dup(key, klen);
    if (e == NULL || k == NULL) {
        free(e);
        free(k);
        free(v);
        return false;
    }
    size_t i = kv_hash(key, klen) & (s->cap - 1);
    *e = (kv_entry){k, klen, v, vlen, s->buckets[i]};
    s->buckets[i] = e;
    s->len++;
    return true;
}
c/ch06/kv.c · region set
Exercise 06.1CUnlink an entry

Write kv_store_del. Walk the chain with a pointer to a pointer. Then removing the first entry of a chain and removing a middle one are the same operation. When the key matches, make the previous link skip the entry, free its key, its value and the entry itself, and reduce the count.

static bool kv_store_del(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen) {
    if (s->cap == 0) return false;
    size_t i = kv_hash(key, klen) & (s->cap - 1);
    for (kv_entry **link = &s->buckets[i]; *link != NULL; link = &(*link)->next) {
        /* your code here */
    }
    return false;
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
/* Remove a key. The chain is walked with a pointer to the pointer that leads to each
 * entry, so that the head of the chain and the middle of it are the same case. */
static bool kv_store_del(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen) {
    if (s->cap == 0) {
        return false;
    }
    size_t i = kv_hash(key, klen) & (s->cap - 1);
    for (kv_entry **link = &s->buckets[i]; *link != NULL; link = &(*link)->next) {
        kv_entry *e = *link;
        if (kv_bytes_eq(e->key, e->klen, key, klen)) {
            *link = e->next;
            free(e->key);
            free(e->val);
            free(e);
            s->len--;
            return true;
        }
    }
    return false;
}

static void kv_store_free(kv_store *s) {
    for (size_t i = 0; i < s->cap; i++) {
        kv_entry *e = s->buckets[i];
        while (e != NULL) {
            kv_entry *next = e->next;
            free(e->key);
            free(e->val);
            free(e);
            e = next;
        }
    }
    free(s->buckets);
    s->buckets = NULL;
    s->cap = s->len = 0;
}
c/ch06/kv.c · region del

The pointer-to-pointer walk is an old C idiom, and it is worth a moment. link points at whichever pointer currently leads to e. At the head of the chain that is the bucket itself. In the middle it is the next field of the entry before. Writing *link = e->next removes e in both cases without a special case for the head. Linus Torvalds has said that code which special-cases the head tells him "this person doesn't understand pointers". The version without the special case is also harder to read the first time.

KEYS walks every bucket and every chain. It needs a pattern matcher, and the one here supports * and ?. The Go and Rust files hold the same function in their glob regions. The reply is an array whose length is known only after the walk, so the keys are collected in a second buffer first:

/* KEYS pattern: every matching key, as an array. The count is known only after the
 * walk, so the keys are collected first and the header is written in front of them. */
static bool kv_exec_keys(const kv_cmd *c, kv_store *s, kv_buf *out) {
    kv_buf keys = {0};
    size_t n = 0;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < s->cap; i++) {
        for (kv_entry *e = s->buckets[i]; e != NULL; e = e->next) {
            if (kv_glob(c->argv[1], c->arglen[1], e->key, e->klen)) {
                if (!kv_reply_bulk(&keys, e->key, e->klen)) {
                    kv_buf_free(&keys);
                    return false;
                }
                n++;
            }
        }
    }
    bool ok = kv_reply_array(out, n) && kv_buf_append(out, keys.data, keys.len);
    kv_buf_free(&keys);
    return ok;
}
c/ch06/kv.c · region keys
✅Every malloc has one free you can point to C

Open kv.c and find each malloc and calloc. For each one, find the free that releases it. The value of an entry is freed in kv_store_set on replacement. The key and the value are freed in kv_store_del and in kv_store_free. The bucket array is freed in kv_store_grow and in kv_store_free. If you cannot find the pair for an allocation, that is a leak, and chapter 11 shows how Valgrind finds it for you.

Go: the same table, with nothing to free#

The Go version keeps the shape of the C one and drops the bookkeeping. A chain is a slice, so there is no next pointer. The entry has two fields:

// fnv1a hashes bytes the way kv does in C, so the two tables spread keys the same way.
func fnv1a(b []byte) uint64 {
	h := uint64(14695981039346656037)
	for _, c := range b {
		h ^= uint64(c)
		h *= 1099511628211
	}
	return h
}
go/ch06/table.go · region hash
// entry is one key and its value in a chain. There is no next pointer: a chain is a
// slice, and the garbage collector owns everything.
type entry struct {
	key []byte
	val []byte
}

// table is a hash table with separate chaining. It grows when it holds more entries
// than buckets, so a chain stays short.
type table struct {
	buckets [][]entry
	n       int
}

func (t *table) get(key []byte) ([]byte, bool) {
	if len(t.buckets) == 0 {
		return nil, false
	}
	chain := t.buckets[fnv1a(key)&uint64(len(t.buckets)-1)]
	for i := range chain {
		if string(chain[i].key) == string(key) {
			return chain[i].val, true
		}
	}
	return nil, false
}
go/ch06/table.go · region table
Exercise 06.2GoGrow the table

Write the grow method. Make a slice of buckets twice as long. Walk every old chain, and append each entry to its new chain. Use fnv1a and the new length to find the index. Then replace t.buckets. Nothing is freed, because nothing in Go is freed by you.

func (t *table) grow() {
	buckets := make([][]entry, 2*len(t.buckets))
	// your code here
	t.buckets = buckets
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
func (t *table) set(key, val []byte) {
	if len(t.buckets) == 0 {
		t.buckets = make([][]entry, 16)
	}
	i := fnv1a(key) & uint64(len(t.buckets)-1)
	chain := t.buckets[i]
	for j := range chain {
		if string(chain[j].key) == string(key) {
			chain[j].val = val
			return
		}
	}
	if t.n >= len(t.buckets) {
		t.grow()
		i = fnv1a(key) & uint64(len(t.buckets)-1)
	}
	t.buckets[i] = append(t.buckets[i], entry{key, val})
	t.n++
}

// grow doubles the bucket slice and moves every entry to its new chain. The old slices
// become garbage, and the collector frees them.
func (t *table) grow() {
	buckets := make([][]entry, 2*len(t.buckets))
	for _, chain := range t.buckets {
		for _, e := range chain {
			i := fnv1a(e.key) & uint64(len(buckets)-1)
			buckets[i] = append(buckets[i], e)
		}
	}
	t.buckets = buckets
}
go/ch06/table.go · region set

The old slices become garbage when t.buckets is replaced, and the collector frees them at some later moment. Deleting moves the last entry of a chain into the hole, as the C list did in chapter 5. The removed entry is garbage too:

func (t *table) del(key []byte) bool {
	if len(t.buckets) == 0 {
		return false
	}
	i := fnv1a(key) & uint64(len(t.buckets)-1)
	chain := t.buckets[i]
	for j := range chain {
		if string(chain[j].key) == string(key) {
			// move the last entry into the hole, then shorten the chain by one
			chain[j] = chain[len(chain)-1]
			t.buckets[i] = chain[:len(chain)-1]
			t.n--
			return true
		}
	}
	return false
}
go/ch06/table.go · region del

The table is never used by the server. It is checked by a test, which runs five thousand random operations against a built-in map and stops at the first disagreement. The test is in table_test.go, and go test finds it by the file name and the function name:

// TestTableAgainstMap runs random operations on the table and on a map, and checks
// after each one that the table agrees with the map.
func TestTableAgainstMap(t *testing.T) {
	var tb table
	ref := map[string]string{}
	r := rand.New(rand.NewPCG(1, 2))
	for step := range 5000 {
		key := fmt.Sprintf("k%d", r.IntN(200))
		switch r.IntN(3) {
		case 0:
			val := fmt.Sprintf("v%d", step)
			tb.set([]byte(key), []byte(val))
			ref[key] = val
		case 1:
			_, want := ref[key]
			if got := tb.del([]byte(key)); got != want {
				t.Fatalf("step %d: del %q = %v, want %v", step, key, got, want)
			}
			delete(ref, key)
		case 2:
			want, ok := ref[key]
			got, found := tb.get([]byte(key))
			if found != ok || string(got) != want {
				t.Fatalf("step %d: get %q = %q,%v, want %q,%v", step, key, got, found, want, ok)
			}
		}
		if tb.n != len(ref) {
			t.Fatalf("step %d: table has %d entries, map has %d", step, tb.n, len(ref))
		}
	}
}
go/ch06/table_test.go · region test
$ go test
PASS
ok  	kv	0.021s
$ go test -bench . -benchmem
BenchmarkTableGet-12    	73166086	        15.76 ns/op	       0 B/op	       0 allocs/op
BenchmarkMapGet-12      	53613301	        21.07 ns/op	       0 B/op	       0 allocs/op

The second command runs the two benchmarks at the bottom of the test file. On the author's machine the hand-written table answers a lookup in 16 nanoseconds and the built-in map in 21. That is not a reason to use your own. The built-in map grows without a pause, and is what every Go programmer reads without thinking. The store keeps the map, and adds the two new commands:

// store keeps every key and its value in a map. The hand-written table in table.go
// would work too, and the tests show it does, but the map is faster and nobody would
// use anything else in Go.
type store struct {
	m map[string][]byte
}

func newStore() *store {
	return &store{m: make(map[string][]byte)}
}

func (s *store) get(key []byte) ([]byte, bool) {
	v, ok := s.m[string(key)]
	return v, ok
}

func (s *store) set(key, val []byte) {
	s.m[string(key)] = val
}

func (s *store) del(key []byte) bool {
	_, ok := s.m[string(key)]
	delete(s.m, string(key))
	return ok
}

func (s *store) size() int {
	return len(s.m)
}

// keys returns every key that matches the pattern, in no particular order.
func (s *store) keys(pattern []byte) [][]byte {
	var out [][]byte
	for k := range s.m {
		if glob(pattern, []byte(k)) {
			out = append(out, []byte(k))
		}
	}
	return out
}
go/ch06/store.go · region store
⚠️A chain slice may share memory with its old self Go

append may or may not allocate. When a chain has room, the new entry goes into the existing array, and any other slice that pointed at that array sees it. In del, the shortened slice chain[:len(chain)-1] still points at the same array as before. The slot past the new end still holds a copy of the entry that was moved. Its key and value stay reachable until the next append writes over them. None of this is a bug here. It is the reason that a Go programmer must know what a slice is. A slice is a pointer, a length and a capacity, and not an array.

Rust: the table that drops itself#

In Rust the chain is a Vec<Entry>, and the entry owns its key and value. Dropping the table drops the outer Vec, which drops every chain, which drops every entry, which frees every byte. No function in table.rs frees anything, and nothing leaks:

/// FNV-1a, the same hash the C version of kv uses.
fn fnv1a(bytes: &[u8]) -> u64 {
    bytes.iter().fold(0xcbf2_9ce4_8422_2325, |h, &b| {
        (h ^ u64::from(b)).wrapping_mul(0x0100_0000_01b3)
    })
}
rust/ch06/src/table.rs · region hash
/// One key and its value in a chain. The chain is a Vec, which owns its entries, and
/// the entries own their bytes. Dropping the table drops all of it.
struct Entry {
    key: Vec<u8>,
    val: Vec<u8>,
}

/// A hash table with separate chaining. It grows when it holds more entries than
/// buckets, so a chain stays short.
#[derive(Default)]
pub struct Table {
    buckets: Vec<Vec<Entry>>,
    len: usize,
}

impl Table {
    fn index(&self, key: &[u8]) -> usize {
        (fnv1a(key) & (self.buckets.len() as u64 - 1)) as usize
    }

    pub fn get(&self, key: &[u8]) -> Option<&[u8]> {
        if self.buckets.is_empty() {
            return None;
        }
        let chain = &self.buckets[self.index(key)];
        chain
            .iter()
            .find(|e| e.key == key)
            .map(|e| e.val.as_slice())
    }
rust/ch06/src/table.rs · region table
Exercise 06.3RustInsert, and grow when full

Write set. If the buckets are empty, create sixteen. Find the chain by index. If an entry with the key exists, replace its value. Otherwise grow when the table is full, compute the index again, because the length changed, and push a new entry. The compiler will tell you if you hold a borrow of a chain across the call to grow.

pub fn set(&mut self, key: Vec<u8>, val: Vec<u8>) {
    if self.buckets.is_empty() {
        self.buckets.resize_with(16, Vec::new);
    }
    // your code here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
pub fn set(&mut self, key: Vec<u8>, val: Vec<u8>) {
    if self.buckets.is_empty() {
        self.buckets.resize_with(16, Vec::new);
    }
    let i = self.index(&key);
    if let Some(e) = self.buckets[i].iter_mut().find(|e| e.key == key) {
        e.val = val;
        return;
    }
    if self.len >= self.buckets.len() {
        self.grow();
    }
    let i = self.index(&key);
    self.buckets[i].push(Entry { key, val });
    self.len += 1;
}

/// Double the buckets and move every entry to its new chain. The entries are moved,
/// not copied: `drain` takes them out of the old chains.
fn grow(&mut self) {
    let mut buckets: Vec<Vec<Entry>> = Vec::new();
    buckets.resize_with(self.buckets.len() * 2, Vec::new);
    let mask = buckets.len() as u64 - 1;
    for chain in &mut self.buckets {
        for e in chain.drain(..) {
            let i = (fnv1a(&e.key) & mask) as usize;
            buckets[i].push(e);
        }
    }
    self.buckets = buckets;
}
rust/ch06/src/table.rs · region set

Look at grow. The call chain.drain(..) moves every entry out of the old chain and into the new one. There is no copy of a key or a value, as in C, and the old chains are empty when the loop ends. The last line replaces self.buckets, and the old outer Vec is dropped at that moment, not later. Deleting uses swap_remove, which does what the Go version did and drops the removed entry on the same line:

    pub fn del(&mut self, key: &[u8]) -> bool {
        if self.buckets.is_empty() {
            return false;
        }
        let i = self.index(key);
        let chain = &mut self.buckets[i];
        match chain.iter().position(|e| e.key == key) {
            Some(j) => {
                // move the last entry into the hole; the removed entry is dropped here
                chain.swap_remove(j);
                self.len -= 1;
                true
            }
            None => false,
        }
    }

    pub fn len(&self) -> usize {
        self.len
    }
}
rust/ch06/src/table.rs · region del

The test lives in the same file, in a module marked #[cfg(test)], which the compiler leaves out of the real binary:

#[cfg(test)]
mod tests {
    use super::Table;
    use std::collections::HashMap;

    /// A small deterministic random number generator, so the test is the same every run.
    fn next(state: &mut u64) -> u64 {
        *state ^= *state << 13;
        *state ^= *state >> 7;
        *state ^= *state << 17;
        *state
    }

    #[test]
    fn agrees_with_hashmap() {
        let mut table = Table::default();
        let mut reference: HashMap<Vec<u8>, Vec<u8>> = HashMap::new();
        let mut rng = 0x2545_f491_4f6c_dd1d_u64;
        for step in 0..5000 {
            let key = format!("k{}", next(&mut rng) % 200).into_bytes();
            match next(&mut rng) % 3 {
                0 => {
                    let val = format!("v{step}").into_bytes();
                    table.set(key.clone(), val.clone());
                    reference.insert(key, val);
                }
                1 => {
                    assert_eq!(
                        table.del(&key),
                        reference.remove(&key).is_some(),
                        "step {step}"
                    );
                }
                _ => {
                    assert_eq!(table.get(&key), reference.get(&key).map(Vec::as_slice));
                }
            }
            assert_eq!(table.len(), reference.len());
        }
    }
}
rust/ch06/src/table.rs · region test
$ cargo test
running 1 test
test table::tests::agrees_with_hashmap ... ok

As in Go, the server keeps the standard HashMap. The standard map uses a hash called SipHash 1-3, which the documentation describes as "selected to provide resistance against HashDoS attacks". An attacker who knows the hash function can send thousands of keys that land in one bucket. Every lookup of such a key then walks that one chain, which holds every key the attacker sent. FNV-1a has no defence against that. Redis uses SipHash too, and seeds it with random bytes at startup, for the same reason.

⚠️The dead code warning is a feature Rust

A module nobody calls produces a warning for every function in it. The binary never calls Table, so the compiler reports each method as dead code, and the book's clippy -D warnings turns that into an error. The file opens with #![allow(dead_code)] and a comment that says why. In C, an unused function that is not static compiles in silence, because the compiler cannot know whether another file calls it. A static one gets a warning under -Wall, which the book's -Werror turns into an error.

Memory, three ways#

The chapter touched memory in every function. The table shows what each language asked of you.

The questionCGoRust
Who frees an entryyou, in three places that must agreethe collector, when nothing refers to itthe owner, at the end of its scope
Forgetting to freea leak that grows until the process diesimpossible for unreachable memoryimpossible, the compiler inserts the drop
Freeing twiceundefined behaviour, often a security holeimpossibledoes not compile
Using after freeundefined behaviour, often a security holeimpossible, the reference keeps it alivedoes not compile
Keeping a pointer across a moveyour responsibility, and grow relies on itsafe, the entry is reachable from the new slicechecked, and set recomputes the index after grow
The costyour attentionmemory held until the next collection, and a pausethe time to satisfy the borrow checker

The C table is about 140 lines, and about a third of them exist to allocate, check and free. The Go table is 75 lines with none of that. The price is a collector that runs in the background and holds freed memory until it does. The Rust table is also 75 lines, and it frees memory at the moment the C one does. The price is a compiler that refused several early drafts of set. Each table is the right answer for its language, and the differences between them are the differences between the languages.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
Writing the tableWeakStrongOKC needs an owner comment on every pointer and three cleanup paths, Go is the shortest, and Rust argues about borrows in set.
Freeing memoryWeakStrongStrongC must free in three places that agree. Go and Rust free for you, in different ways.
Freeing on timeStrongWeakStrongC and Rust free on the line that removes an entry. Go frees at the next collection.
Testing itWeakStrongStrongC has no test runner in the box. go test and cargo test are built in.
Shipping unused codeWeakOKStrongC warns only for static functions and Go ignores unused functions, while Rust reports each one.
Needing to write oneStrongWeakWeakIn C the table is the store. In Go and Rust it is an exercise, because the map is built in.
Next · Building kv07Modules, packages and dependencies17 min

Modules, packages and dependencies#

Building kv · Chapter 07· 17 min read· 166 lines of code

kv is five hundred lines in one file, and the next chapters double it. This chapter splits the server into parts, in each language's own way. C gets header files and a Makefile that knows about them. Go gets packages under internal. Rust gets a library crate with a thin binary. It also covers dependencies, and the tools that keep code in one style.

The server does not change in this chapter. Every test from chapter 6 still passes, and no command is added. What changes is where the code lives, how one part sees another, and how a reader finds anything. That sounds like housekeeping. It is also where the three languages differ most in their daily feel. A module system is what you touch every time you open a file.

The unitCGoRust
A modulea .c file with a .h beside ita folder, one packagea file, declared with mod
A private namestatica lower-case first letterthe default, without pub
A public namedeclared in the header, prefixed kv_an upper-case first letterpub
Using another module#include "store.h", copied in by the preprocessorimport "kv/internal/store"use kv::store::Store
Building many filesa Makefile that lists them, or finds themgo build ./cmd/kvcargo build
A dependencycopy the source in, or a system packagego get, recorded in go.modcargo add, recorded in Cargo.toml
The exact versionswherever you wrote them downgo.sumCargo.lock
The registrynoneany Git host, through a proxycrates.io
The formatterclang-format, with a config filegofmt, with no optionsrustfmt, with few
📜Why Go has a compatibility promise and Rust has editions

A module system is a promise about what stays the same. Go 1 was released in 2012 with a written promise. Programs written to the Go 1 specification "will continue to compile and run correctly, unchanged, over the lifetime of that specification". Fourteen years later the promise holds, and a Go package from 2012 is meant to still build. Rust made a different promise. The language can change in ways that would break old code, but only in a new edition. A crate names its edition in Cargo.toml. The 2024 edition shipped with Rust 1.85 in February 2025. A crate on the 2015 edition still builds with today's compiler, next to one on the 2024 edition, in the same program. C made no promise at all, and relies on the fact that no compiler vendor dares to break the code that already exists.

C: headers, static, and a Makefile that knows about both#

C has no modules. It has files, and a convention. A module is a .c file that holds the code and a .h file that holds the declarations other files may use. The buffer from chapter 4 becomes two files. The header is the public part:

#ifndef KV_BUF_H
#define KV_BUF_H

#include <stdbool.h>
#include <stddef.h>

/* A growable byte buffer. It owns data, and kv_buf_free releases it. Start one with
 * kv_buf b = {0}. */
typedef struct {
    char *data; /* owned; NULL until the first append */
    size_t len;
    size_t cap;
} kv_buf;

bool kv_buf_append(kv_buf *b, const void *src, size_t n);
void kv_buf_consume(kv_buf *b, size_t n);
void kv_buf_free(kv_buf *b);

#endif
c/ch07/src/buf.h

Three conventions are at work. The first lines and the last line are a header guard. The preprocessor copies a header into every file that includes it. A file that includes buf.h twice, once directly and once through resp.h, would see the typedef twice. The guard makes the second copy empty. The second convention is the prefix. C has one namespace for the whole program. So every public name in kv begins with kv_, and a name from another library cannot collide with it. The third is that the header says what the caller must do, in comments, because nothing else will. The .c file holds the rest, and marks the one function that only it uses as static:

/* Private: only this file calls it, so it is static and not in the header. */
static bool kv_buf_reserve(kv_buf *b, size_t extra) {
    if (b->len + extra <= b->cap) {
        return true;
    }
    size_t cap = b->cap ? b->cap * 2 : 1024;
    while (cap < b->len + extra) {
        cap *= 2;
    }
    char *p = realloc(b->data, cap);
    if (p == NULL) {
        return false; /* the old block is still valid and still ours */
    }
    b->data = p;
    b->cap = cap;
    return true;
}
c/ch07/src/buf.c · region static

A static function is invisible outside its file. That is the whole of information hiding in C. There is no private keyword and no module boundary, only a function the linker never hears about. Every file in src/ follows the same shape. The header of the store is the most useful one to read. It says who owns every pointer, and how long a pointer from kv_store_find stays valid:

Exercise 07.1CWrite the header

Write store.h for the hash table from chapter 6. It needs a guard, the two structures, and a declaration for each of the four public functions. Put a comment on the entry that says what it owns, and a comment on kv_store_find that says how long the returned pointer is valid. Everything that only store.c uses stays out of the header and becomes static. That includes the hash function and the grow function.

#ifndef KV_STORE_H
#define KV_STORE_H

#include <stdbool.h>
#include <stddef.h>

/* your declarations here */

#endif
Stuck?Show the full listing
#ifndef KV_STORE_H
#define KV_STORE_H

#include <stdbool.h>
#include <stddef.h>

/* The keys and values, in a hash table with separate chaining. */

/* One key and its value, in a chain of entries that share a bucket. The key, the value
 * and the next entry are owned. */
typedef struct kv_entry {
    char *key;
    size_t klen;
    char *val;
    size_t vlen;
    struct kv_entry *next;
} kv_entry;

/* Start one with kv_store s = {0}, and end it with kv_store_free. */
typedef struct {
    kv_entry **buckets; /* owned array of cap pointers */
    size_t cap;         /* a power of two, or 0 before the first insert */
    size_t len;         /* how many entries, over all chains */
} kv_store;

/* The entry for key, or NULL. The pointer is valid until the next set or del. */
kv_entry *kv_store_find(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen);

/* Store a copy of val under a copy of key, replacing any old value. */
bool kv_store_set(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen, const char *val, size_t vlen);
bool kv_store_del(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen);
void kv_store_free(kv_store *s);

#endif
c/ch07/src/store.h

The main file now includes four headers and nothing else of its own. Those four lines are the C form of an import list:

#include "buf.h"
#include "cmd.h"
#include "resp.h"
#include "store.h"
c/ch07/src/main.c · region includes

With six source files, the Makefile from chapter 2 is not enough. The one below finds every .c file, compiles each into an object file under build/, and links them. The -MMD flag asks the compiler to write a small file beside each object that lists the headers it included. make reads those files, so a change to store.h rebuilds exactly the files that include it:

CC       ?= cc
CFLAGS   ?= -std=c17 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -O2 -g
SANITIZE ?=

# every .c under src/ becomes a .o under build/, and kv is linked from all of them
SRC := $(wildcard src/*.c)
OBJ := $(SRC:src/%.c=build/%.o)

kv: $(OBJ)
	$(CC) $(CFLAGS) $(SANITIZE) -o $@ $(OBJ)

# -MMD writes a .d file beside each .o that lists the headers it included, so that a
# change to a header rebuilds exactly the files that use it
build/%.o: src/%.c | build
	$(CC) $(CFLAGS) $(SANITIZE) -MMD -c -o $@ $<

build:
	mkdir -p build

-include $(OBJ:.o=.d)

clean:
	rm -rf kv kv.dSYM build

.PHONY: clean
c/ch07/Makefile
⚠️A header without a guard C

Include a header twice and the compiler reports a redefinition. main.c includes buf.h directly and again through cmd.h. Without the guard the second copy defines kv_buf a second time, and the error names the header, not the file that included it twice. Every header gets a guard on the day it is created. Many compilers accept #pragma once as a shorter form, and it is not in the standard.

✅Include your own header first C

A .c file includes its own header before any other, as buf.c does. Suppose the header forgot an include it needs, such as stddef.h for size_t. The mistake then shows in the file that owns the header. It does not hide in another file that included things in a luckier order.

Dependencies in C: the package manager that is not there#

C has no package manager, and that sentence has to be said carefully, because it has several. The operating system has one. A C program that needs a library usually says apt install libssl-dev or brew install openssl. Then it asks a tool called pkg-config where the headers went. The second way is to copy the source into your own tree, which is called vendoring. Redis does this. Its deps/ folder holds its own copies of the libraries it needs, and they build with the rest. The third way is a package manager that is not part of the language, such as vcpkg. Microsoft describes vcpkg as "a free and open-source C/C++ package manager maintained by Microsoft and the C++ community". Conan is another. It works with CMake, Make and MSBuild. Both are used. There is no default.

Each way has a cost. A system package is whatever version the operating system has. A vendored copy must be updated yourself. A third-party manager is one more thing to install before the build works. The server kv has no dependency. That is not unusual for a C program, and it is one reason C programs so often write things themselves.

Go: a folder is a package, and a capital letter is an export#

A Go package is a folder. Every file in the folder begins with the same package line and sees everything in the other files. Another package sees only the names that begin with a capital letter. The spec puts it in one sentence. An identifier is exported if "the first character of the identifier's name is a Unicode uppercase letter". There is no keyword. The protocol code moves to internal/resp, and readCommand becomes ReadCommand:

// ReadCommand reads one request: an array of bulk strings, or one inline line of words.
// It returns io.EOF when the client closed the connection between commands.
func ReadCommand(r *bufio.Reader) ([][]byte, error) {
	line, err := readLine(r)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}
	if len(line) == 0 || line[0] != '*' {
		return bytes.Fields(line), nil
	}
	n, err := strconv.Atoi(string(line[1:]))
	if err != nil || n < 0 || n > maxArgs {
		return nil, ErrProtocol
	}
	args := make([][]byte, 0, n)
	for range n {
		line, err := readLine(r)
		if err != nil {
			return nil, unexpected(err)
		}
		if len(line) == 0 || line[0] != '$' {
			return nil, ErrProtocol
		}
		size, err := strconv.Atoi(string(line[1:]))
		if err != nil || size < 0 || size > maxBulk {
			return nil, ErrProtocol
		}
		buf := make([]byte, size+2)
		if _, err := io.ReadFull(r, buf); err != nil {
			return nil, unexpected(err)
		}
		if buf[size] != '\r' || buf[size+1] != '\n' {
			return nil, ErrProtocol
		}
		args = append(args, buf[:size])
	}
	return args, nil
}
go/ch07/internal/resp/resp.go · region exported

The folder is named internal on purpose. The Go 1.4 release notes state the rule. A package with internal in its path "can be imported only by code in the directory tree rooted at" the parent of that folder. So kv/internal/store can be used by kv/cmd/kv and by nobody outside the module. The rule is in the go tool, not in the language, and it is how a Go program says "this is not an API". The import path begins with the module path from go.mod:

// Execute runs one command against the store and writes its reply. The two imports
// above are this module's own packages: kv is the module path from go.mod.
func Execute(args [][]byte, s *store.Store, w *bufio.Writer) error {
go/ch07/internal/cmd/cmd.go · region imports

The store exports a type and its methods, and keeps the map itself private, so that no other package can reach into it:

// Store keeps every key and its value in a map. Exported, so that package cmd can use
// it; its field is not, so that only this package can touch the map.
type Store struct {
	m map[string][]byte
}

func New() *Store {
	return &Store{m: make(map[string][]byte)}
}

func (s *Store) Get(key []byte) ([]byte, bool) {
	v, ok := s.m[string(key)]
	return v, ok
}

func (s *Store) Set(key, val []byte) {
	s.m[string(key)] = val
}

func (s *Store) Del(key []byte) bool {
	_, ok := s.m[string(key)]
	delete(s.m, string(key))
	return ok
}

func (s *Store) Len() int {
	return len(s.m)
}

// Keys returns every key that matches the pattern, in no particular order.
func (s *Store) Keys(pattern []byte) [][]byte {
	var out [][]byte
	for k := range s.m {
		if glob(pattern, []byte(k)) {
			out = append(out, []byte(k))
		}
	}
	return out
}
go/ch07/internal/store/store.go · region exported
