Stuck?Show the full listing
func TestGlob(t *testing.T) {
	cases := []struct {
		pat, key string
		want     bool
	}{
		{"*", "", true},
		{"*", "anything", true},
		{"user:*", "user:1", true},
		{"user:*", "order:1", false},
		{"us?r:1", "user:1", true},
		{"us?r:1", "usr:1", false},
		{"*:2", "user:2", true},
		{"a*b*c", "aXXbYYc", true},
		{"a*b*c", "aXXbYY", false},
	}
	for _, c := range cases {
		if got := glob([]byte(c.pat), []byte(c.key)); got != c.want {
			t.Errorf("glob(%q, %q) = %v, want %v", c.pat, c.key, got, c.want)
		}
	}
}
go/ch07/internal/store/glob_test.go · region test
$ go build -o kv ./cmd/kv
$ go test ./...
?   	kv/cmd/kv	[no test files]
?   	kv/internal/cmd	[no test files]
?   	kv/internal/resp	[no test files]
ok  	kv/internal/store	0.020s

kv still has no dependency, and Go makes that a normal state. The standard library has the network, the buffered reader, the map and the test runner, and most Go programs of this size use nothing else. When a dependency is needed, go get adds a line to go.mod and a hash to go.sum. The hash is checked on every later download, against the file and against a public log that Google runs. So a changed upload cannot replace what you tested. There is no central registry. A module path is a URL, and the go tool fetches through a proxy that caches every version it has seen.

⚠️An import you do not use is an error Go

Delete the last use of a package and the build fails. The error is "strings" imported and not used. Go does this on purpose, so that a dependency graph never holds a package that nothing needs, and the build stays fast. Editors remove the line for you, and new Go programmers curse it for a week.

Rust: a module is a file you must announce#

kv already had modules in Rust. Chapter 4 wrote mod resp; and mod cmd;, and each line told the compiler to read a file of that name. The new step is to put the modules in a library. Then a test or a benchmark can use the parser and the store without starting a server. A crate with both src/lib.rs and src/main.rs builds a library and a binary of the same name:

pub mod cmd;
pub mod resp;
pub mod store;
pub mod table;
rust/ch07/src/lib.rs · region mods

The pub on each mod line makes the module visible outside the library, and the pub on each function inside makes the function visible. Without either, the item is private to the file. The binary uses the library through its name:

use kv::store::Store;
use kv::{cmd, resp};
rust/ch07/src/main.rs · region use
Exercise 07.3RustMake the crate a library

Create src/lib.rs with four public mod lines. Remove the mod lines from main.rs. Replace them with use lines that name the library, such as kv::store::Store. Build, and run cargo clippy. It will tell you that Store has a public len and no is_empty. That is a convention it enforces for public types, and it never mentioned it while the type was private. Add a one-line is_empty that returns self.map.is_empty().

// src/lib.rs
// your four mod lines here

// src/main.rs
// your use lines here
Stuck?Show the full listing
//! kv as a library: the protocol, the store and the commands. The binary in main.rs is a
//! thin wrapper, and a test or a benchmark can use the crate without starting a server.

pub mod cmd;
pub mod resp;
pub mod store;
pub mod table;
rust/ch07/src/lib.rs
use kv::store::Store;
use kv::{cmd, resp};
rust/ch07/src/main.rs · region use

A dependency in Rust is a line in Cargo.toml, which cargo add writes for you, and a crate on crates.io, which is the one registry. The Cargo book draws the line between the two files in one sentence each. "Cargo.toml is about describing your dependencies in a broad sense, and is written by you." "Cargo.lock contains exact information about your dependencies. It is maintained by Cargo and should not be manually edited." The first dependency of kv arrives in chapter 8. That is when the lock file appears.

⚠️A file is not a module until something declares it Rust

Add src/glob.rs and nothing happens. The compiler reads only the files that a mod line names, starting from lib.rs or main.rs. A forgotten mod line produces an error about an unresolved name somewhere else, and the file that holds the name is never mentioned. Go reads every file in the folder. C reads only what the Makefile lists. Rust reads what the tree of mod declarations reaches.

Three answers to "where does code come from"#

Set the three languages side by side and the pattern of the book repeats. C has a convention where the other two have a tool, and the convention has several competing forms. Go has one tool with no registry, which fits a company that hosts its own code. Rust has one tool with one registry, which fits a community that shares small crates. The number of dependencies a program of this size has in practice follows from that. A C program has few, because each one is work. A Go program has few, because the standard library is wide. A Rust program often has dozens, because adding one is a single command, and that is the weakness of the strongest answer. Chapter 13 shows what every dependency costs at the border, and chapter 14 what a C one costs.

One style, enforced by a tool#

All three languages now have a formatter, and the difference is who decides the style. clang-format takes a configuration file, and the one for kv is four settings:

# The layout every C file in kv keeps. clang-format -i src/*.c src/*.h applies it.
BasedOnStyle: LLVM
IndentWidth: 4
ColumnLimit: 100
AllowShortFunctionsOnASingleLine: Inline
c/ch07/.clang-format

It comes with clang, so it is installed on macOS with Xcode and on Linux as a package. A project that uses it must agree on the configuration first. gofmt has no configuration. The Go team decided the style when the language was released. The result is that every Go file anyone has ever read looks the same. rustfmt is between clang-format and gofmt. It has a configuration file, rustfmt.toml, and almost nobody writes one. The book runs all three formatters in make verify. A file that is not formatted fails the build.

Beside each formatter is a linter that finds mistakes the compiler allows. clang-tidy for C must be installed and configured. go vet comes with Go and runs in make verify, and staticcheck adds more. cargo clippy comes with Rust, has more than 800 checks, and is the tool that asked for is_empty above. The verdict for this chapter is mostly about these tools, because the day-to-day experience of a language is the experience of its tools.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
Splitting codeWeakStrongOKC needs guards, prefixes and a Makefile that tracks headers, while Go needs a folder and Rust a mod line that is easy to forget.
Hiding a nameOKStrongStrongC has static, for functions only. Go and Rust hide by default or by a letter.
Adding a dependencyWeakOKStrongC has three competing ways and no default, while Go and Rust each have one command, and Rust also has one registry.
Having too manyStrongStrongWeakC and Go programs stay small by cost and by a wide standard library. Rust programs grow dozens of crates.
Keeping one styleOKStrongStrongclang-format needs a config everyone agrees on. gofmt and rustfmt need nothing.
Finding mistakesWeakOKStrongclang-tidy must be installed. go vet is built in. clippy is built in and has 800 checks.
Next · Building kv08Many clients24 min

Many clients#

Building kv · Chapter 08· 24 min read· 364 lines of code

kv serves one client at a time. This chapter makes it serve thousands. C gets an event loop on one thread, built on poll. Go gets a goroutine for each client and a lock on the store. Rust gets a thread for each client, then the same server on tokio, the first dependency in the book. The chapter is about concurrency, the problem Go and Rust solve.

Start the chapter 7 server and run redis-benchmark against it with its default 50 connections. It never finishes. The server accepts the first connection and serves it until it closes, and the benchmark keeps every connection open until the end. The other 49 wait in the operating system's queue for a turn that never comes. A real client does the same thing, and a second user of kv would wait for the first one to log out.

There are two ways out, and the three languages take both. The first is to keep one thread and never block it. The thread asks the operating system which sockets have bytes waiting, reads from those, and asks again. That is an event loop, and it is what Redis does. The second is to give each client its own thread of control and let it block. The operating system or a runtime then switches between them. Go does that with goroutines. Rust does it with threads first, and then with tasks on an asynchronous runtime. That runtime is an event loop written as if each client had its own thread.

ConcurrencyCGoRust
One client isan entry in an array of pollfda goroutinea thread, then a task
Who waitspoll, for all clients at onceeach goroutine, on its own readeach thread on its read, then the tokio runtime
Memory for a clienttwo buffers you sizea stack of a few kilobytes that growsa 2 MiB stack, then a task of a few kilobytes, mostly its buffers
The store is shared bynobody, there is one threada sync.RWMutexan Arc<Mutex<Store>>
Forgetting the locknot possiblea data race, which go test -race may finddoes not compile
Using all the coresnoyes, the runtime spreads goroutinesyes, with threads or with tokio
Lines changedabout 200 new1 in main, 12 in the store6 in main, then a new binary
📜Readiness and completion: two shapes of the same question

The question "which socket can I use now?" got different answers on different systems, and every language has to hide the difference. Unix from Berkeley, the Berkeley Software Distribution (BSD), answered first with select. poll came later and takes an array instead of a fixed-size set of bits. FreeBSD 4.1 added kqueue, which is what macOS uses. Linux added epoll in October 2002. All of those report readiness. They say "this socket has bytes", and the program then reads. Windows NT 3.5 took the other shape with I/O completion ports. A program hands the operating system a buffer and a read request, and the port reports completion. It says "this read has finished, and the bytes are in your buffer". The Go runtime and tokio both contain a layer that makes the two shapes look the same. That is why the Go and Rust versions of kv run on Windows unchanged, and the C one does not.

C: one thread, and poll to say who is ready#

The loop keeps a structure for each client, holding the socket, the bytes read so far, and the replies not yet written. The buffers are the ones from chapter 4. Beside the clients is an array of struct pollfd, which is what poll reads and writes:

/* One client: its socket, the bytes read so far, and the replies not yet written. */
typedef struct {
    int fd;
    kv_buf in;
    kv_buf out;
    bool closing; /* after a protocol error: close once out is written */
} kv_conn;

/* Every client, beside the pollfd array that poll() reads. pfds[0] is the listening
 * socket, and pfds[i + 1] belongs to conns[i]. Both arrays are owned. */
typedef struct {
    kv_conn *conns;
    struct pollfd *pfds;
    size_t len;
    size_t cap;
} kv_clients;
c/ch08/src/server.c · region conn

Every socket in the loop is non-blocking. A blocking read on a socket with no bytes would stop the one thread, and every other client with it. A non-blocking one returns -1 with errno set to EAGAIN, which means "nothing right now", and the loop moves on:

/* A non-blocking socket returns EAGAIN instead of waiting, which the loop relies on. */
static bool kv_set_nonblocking(int fd) {
    int flags = fcntl(fd, F_GETFL, 0);
    return flags >= 0 && fcntl(fd, F_SETFL, flags | O_NONBLOCK) >= 0;
}
c/ch08/src/server.c · region nonblock

Accepting clients follows the same rule. When poll says the listening socket is ready, there may be several connections waiting, so the loop accepts until accept itself says EAGAIN:

/* Accept every waiting client. The listening socket is non-blocking, so accept()
 * returns EAGAIN when the queue is empty. */
static void kv_accept_all(int listen_fd, kv_clients *cs) {
    for (;;) {
        int fd = accept(listen_fd, NULL, NULL);
        if (fd < 0) {
            if (errno == EAGAIN || errno == EWOULDBLOCK || errno == EINTR) {
                return;
            }
            perror("accept");
            return;
        }
        if (!kv_set_nonblocking(fd) || !kv_clients_add(cs, fd)) {
            close(fd);
        }
    }
}
c/ch08/src/server.c · region accept

Reading from a ready client follows the pattern once more. Read until the socket is empty, then run every complete request in the buffer and queue the replies in the output buffer. The parser from chapter 4 was written for exactly this moment. Its KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE status looked like extra work in a one-client server. Here it lets the loop stop in the middle of a request and come back later:

/* Read what has arrived, run every complete request, and queue the replies. Returns
 * false when the client has gone or must be dropped. */
static bool kv_conn_input(kv_conn *c, kv_store *store) {
    char tmp[4096];
    for (;;) {
        ssize_t n = read(c->fd, tmp, sizeof tmp);
        if (n < 0) {
            if (errno == EAGAIN || errno == EWOULDBLOCK) {
                break; /* nothing more for now */
            }
            if (errno == EINTR) {
                continue;
            }
            return false;
        }
        if (n == 0) {
            return false; /* the client closed */
        }
        if (!kv_buf_append(&c->in, tmp, (size_t)n)) {
            return false;
        }
    }
    for (;;) {
        kv_cmd cmd;
        size_t used = 0;
        kv_parse_status st = kv_parse(c->in.data, c->in.len, &cmd, &used);
        if (st == KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE) {
            if (c->in.len > KV_MAX_REQUEST) {
                st = KV_PARSE_BAD;
            } else {
                break;
            }
        }
        if (st == KV_PARSE_BAD) {
            kv_reply_error(&c->out, "ERR Protocol error");
            c->closing = true;
            break;
        }
        if (!kv_exec(&cmd, store, &c->out)) {
            return false;
        }
        kv_buf_consume(&c->in, used);
    }
    return true;
}
c/ch08/src/server.c · region input
Exercise 08.1CWrite what the socket will take

Write kv_conn_output. Loop while the output buffer holds bytes. Call write with everything that is queued, and consume as many bytes as it took. When it returns EAGAIN, return true and stop, because the socket is full and poll will say when it has room. Retry on EINTR. Return false on any other error.

static bool kv_conn_output(kv_conn *c) {
    while (c->out.len > 0) {
        /* your code here */
    }
    return true;
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
/* Write as much of the queued reply as the socket will take right now. */
static bool kv_conn_output(kv_conn *c) {
    while (c->out.len > 0) {
        ssize_t w = write(c->fd, c->out.data, c->out.len);
        if (w < 0) {
            if (errno == EAGAIN || errno == EWOULDBLOCK) {
                return true; /* the socket is full: poll() will say when to continue */
            }
            if (errno == EINTR) {
                continue;
            }
            return false;
        }
        kv_buf_consume(&c->out, (size_t)w);
    }
    return true;
}
c/ch08/src/server.c · region output

The loop itself is short, and three lines in it are the heart of every event loop ever written. The first sets POLLOUT only for clients with something to write. A socket is almost always writable, so asking about it would make poll return at once and the loop spin. The second is the backwards walk, so that removing a client by moving the last one into its slot does not skip anyone. The third is the closing check, which closes a client after a protocol error only once its error reply has gone out:

int kv_server_run(int listen_fd, kv_store *store) {
    kv_clients cs = {0};
    if (!kv_set_nonblocking(listen_fd) || !kv_clients_add(&cs, listen_fd)) {
        return 1;
    }
    cs.len = 0; /* the listening socket took slot 0 of pfds; it is not a client */
    cs.pfds[0] = (struct pollfd){listen_fd, POLLIN, 0};
    for (;;) {
        /* ask for POLLOUT only when there is something to write, or poll() returns at once */
        for (size_t i = 0; i < cs.len; i++) {
            cs.pfds[i + 1].events = POLLIN | (cs.conns[i].out.len > 0 ? POLLOUT : 0);
        }
        int ready = poll(cs.pfds, (nfds_t)(cs.len + 1), -1);
        if (ready < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) {
                continue;
            }
            perror("poll");
            break;
        }
        if (cs.pfds[0].revents & POLLIN) {
            kv_accept_all(listen_fd, &cs);
        }
        /* walk backwards, because removing a client moves the last one into its slot */
        for (size_t i = cs.len; i-- > 0;) {
            short ev = cs.pfds[i + 1].revents;
            kv_conn *c = &cs.conns[i];
            bool alive = true;
            if (ev & (POLLIN | POLLHUP | POLLERR)) {
                alive = kv_conn_input(c, store);
            }
            if (alive && (ev & POLLOUT || c->out.len > 0)) {
                alive = kv_conn_output(c);
            }
            if (!alive || (c->closing && c->out.len == 0)) {
                kv_clients_remove(&cs, i);
            }
        }
    }
    for (size_t i = cs.len; i-- > 0;) {
        kv_clients_remove(&cs, i);
    }
    free(cs.conns);
    free(cs.pfds);
    return 1;
}
c/ch08/src/server.c · region loop

Two hundred lines of new code, and the server now serves fifty clients from one thread. It also uses one core, which is the limit of this design, and the one Redis still lives with for running commands. The main function shrinks to a call:

int main(void) {
    int port = kv_port();
    int fd = kv_listen(port);
    if (fd < 0) {
        return 1;
    }
    fprintf(stderr, "kv listening on port %d\n", port);
    kv_store store = {0};
    int rc = kv_server_run(fd, &store);
    kv_store_free(&store);
    close(fd);
    return rc;
}
c/ch08/src/main.c · region main
⚠️One blocking call stops every client C

Forget O_NONBLOCK on one socket and a slow client stops the server. poll says the socket is readable, and the loop calls read. A client that sent half a request and went away keeps the one thread waiting for the other half. The other forty-nine clients see a server that has stopped answering. Nothing crashes and nothing is reported, which makes it one of the harder bugs to find in a C server. That is one reason the kv_set_nonblocking call is the first thing done with every new socket.

🖥poll, kqueue, epoll and IOCP

poll is in POSIX, the Portable Operating System Interface, so the loop above builds on macOS and Linux. Its cost is that every call hands the kernel the whole array, and the kernel walks it. So the cost grows with the number of clients. kqueue on macOS and epoll on Linux keep the list in the kernel. They report only the sockets that changed. Redis uses them, and uses select on a system that has no better call. The shape of the loop is the same. On Windows, a server that wants to scale uses completion ports, and the loop is inside out. The program starts reads, and the port reports which ones finished. Chapter 13 comes back to what that means for porting the C version.

Go: one word, and one lock#

The Go change in main is the keyword go. The call serve(conn, s) runs on a new goroutine, and the loop goes straight back to Accept. The serving function is unchanged. It still blocks on its reads, which is fine, because it blocks only its own goroutine, and the runtime runs another:

func main() {
	ln, err := net.Listen("tcp", ":"+port())
	if err != nil {
		fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, err)
		os.Exit(1)
	}
	fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, "kv listening on port", port())
	s := store.New()
	for {
		conn, err := ln.Accept()
		if err != nil {
			fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, "accept:", err)
			continue
		}
		// the one word that changes: the client is served on its own goroutine, and
		// the loop goes straight back to accept
		go func() {
			if err := serve(conn, s); err != nil {
				fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, "client:", err)
			}
		}()
	}
}
go/ch08/cmd/kv/main.go · region main

A goroutine is not a thread. The Go FAQ says one costs "little overhead beyond the memory for the stack". That stack is a few kilobytes. The runtime multiplexes goroutines "onto a set of threads". Fifty goroutines, or fifty thousand, run on as many threads as there are cores. The event loop that C needed is inside the runtime, and it talks to kqueue or epoll or a completion port on your behalf.

The price appears in the store. Fifty goroutines now call Set and Get at the same time, and a Go map is not safe for that. The store gets a read-write lock. Readers take RLock and may hold it together. Writers take Lock and hold it alone:

// Store keeps every key and its value in a map, behind a lock. Many goroutines read and
// write the store at once, and a Go map is not safe for that without one.
type Store struct {
	mu sync.RWMutex
	m  map[string][]byte
}

func New() *Store {
	return &Store{m: make(map[string][]byte)}
}

// Get takes the read lock, which any number of readers may hold together.
func (s *Store) Get(key []byte) ([]byte, bool) {
	s.mu.RLock()
	defer s.mu.RUnlock()
	v, ok := s.m[string(key)]
	return v, ok
}

// Set takes the write lock, which excludes every other reader and writer.
func (s *Store) Set(key, val []byte) {
	s.mu.Lock()
	defer s.mu.Unlock()
	s.m[string(key)] = val
}

func (s *Store) Del(key []byte) bool {
	s.mu.Lock()
	defer s.mu.Unlock()
	_, ok := s.m[string(key)]
	delete(s.m, string(key))
	return ok
}

func (s *Store) Len() int {
	s.mu.RLock()
	defer s.mu.RUnlock()
	return len(s.m)
}
go/ch08/internal/store/store.go · region store
Exercise 08.2GoMake INCR atomic

Rewrite Incr so that it holds the write lock from the moment it reads the old value to the moment it writes the new one. Do not call Get and Set inside it. Each of those takes the lock again, and a Go lock cannot be taken twice by the same goroutine. Read and write the map directly.

func (s *Store) Incr(key []byte) (int64, error) {
	// your code here: lock, read s.m, parse, add, write s.m, unlock
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
// Incr adds one to the integer stored under key, which counts as 0 when absent. It
// holds the write lock from the read to the write, so that two clients adding one at
// the same moment cannot both read the same old value.
func (s *Store) Incr(key []byte) (int64, error) {
	s.mu.Lock()
	defer s.mu.Unlock()
	var n int64
	if cur, ok := s.m[string(key)]; ok {
		var err error
		n, err = strconv.ParseInt(string(cur), 10, 64)
		if err != nil {
			return 0, ErrNotInteger
		}
	}
	if n == math.MaxInt64 {
		return 0, ErrOverflow
	}
	n++
	s.m[string(key)] = []byte(strconv.FormatInt(n, 10))
	return n, nil
}
go/ch08/internal/store/store.go · region incr

Without the single lock, two clients can both read 41, both write 42, and one increment is lost. The test below adds one to the same key from fifty goroutines, two hundred times each, and expects exactly ten thousand:

// TestIncrFromManyGoroutines adds one to the same key from 50 goroutines, 200 times
// each. With the lock the result is 10000. Without it, go test -race reports the data
// race, and the count comes out short.
func TestIncrFromManyGoroutines(t *testing.T) {
	s := New()
	var wg sync.WaitGroup
	for range 50 {
		wg.Go(func() {
			for range 200 {
				if _, err := s.Incr([]byte("n")); err != nil {
					t.Error(err)
				}
			}
		})
	}
	wg.Wait()
	got, _ := s.Get([]byte("n"))
	if string(got) != "10000" {
		t.Fatalf("n = %s, want 10000", got)
	}
}
go/ch08/internal/store/store_test.go · region test
$ go test -race ./...
ok  	kv/internal/store	1.514s

The -race flag turns on the race detector. It watches every memory access during the test, and reports two goroutines that touch the same byte without a lock between them. Remove the lock from Incr and the test fails twice. The count comes out short, and the detector prints both stack traces. Keep the flag on in every test run, which the book's make verify does.

⚠️The race detector finds races that happen, not races that exist Go

A race the test never provokes is a race the detector never reports. The detector watches real accesses, so a missing lock on a path the test does not run stays hidden until production runs it. It is a tool for finding races, not a proof that there are none. The Rust compiler makes the other kind of claim in the next section. The difference between the detector and the compiler is one of the largest in the book.

Rust: a thread for each client, and a compiler that checks the sharing#

The thread version is the Go version with the sharing made explicit. The store is wrapped in two types. An Arc counts how many owners the store has. A Mutex lets one thread at a time use it. Each new thread gets a clone of the Arc, which is a new owner of the same store:

fn main() -> io::Result<()> {
    let port = port();
    let listener = TcpListener::bind(format!("0.0.0.0:{port}"))?;
    eprintln!("kv listening on port {port}");
    let store = Arc::new(Mutex::new(Store::new()));
    for stream in listener.incoming() {
        match stream {
            Ok(stream) => {
                // a clone of the Arc is a new owner of the same store, moved into the thread
                let store = Arc::clone(&store);
                thread::spawn(move || {
                    if let Err(e) = serve(stream, store) {
                        eprintln!("client: {e}");
                    }
                });
            }
            Err(e) => eprintln!("accept: {e}"),
        }
    }
    Ok(())
}
rust/ch08/src/main.rs · region main

Inside the serving function, the lock is taken for one command. It is released at the end of the line, when the guard goes out of scope. The unwrap_or_else handles a poisoned lock, which is what a mutex becomes when a thread panics while holding it. The store is still usable, so the server takes it anyway:

/// The store is shared: an Arc counts the owners, and a Mutex lets one thread at a
/// time use it. The lock is taken for one command and released at the end of the line.
fn serve(stream: TcpStream, store: Arc<Mutex<Store>>) -> io::Result<()> {
    let mut reader = BufReader::new(stream.try_clone()?);
    let mut writer = BufWriter::new(stream);
    loop {
        match resp::read_command(&mut reader) {
            Ok(Some(args)) => {
                let mut store = store.lock().unwrap_or_else(|e| e.into_inner());
                cmd::execute(args, &mut store, &mut writer)?;
            }
            Ok(None) => return Ok(()),
            Err(e) if e.kind() == io::ErrorKind::InvalidData => {
                resp::write_error(&mut writer, "ERR Protocol error")?;
                writer.flush()?;
                return Err(e);
            }
            Err(e) => return Err(e),
        }
        if reader.buffer().is_empty() {
            writer.flush()?;
        }
    }
}
rust/ch08/src/main.rs · region serve

What happens without the Arc and the Mutex? The compiler refuses. Try to move one Store into two threads and it says error[E0382]: use of moved value: store. The note is "value moved into closure here, in previous iteration of loop". Try to share a reference instead and the compiler refuses that too, because the thread may outlive main. The only way through is a type that is safe to share, and Arc<Mutex<T>> is the simplest one. The rule is the one the borrow checker applied in chapter 5. A value can have many readers or one writer, never both, and a thread is only another reader or writer. A data race in a Rust program that compiles without unsafe is not possible. The race detector that Go runs at test time is work the Rust compiler does at compile time.

Rust again: tasks on tokio#

A thread for each client works for fifty clients and for five thousand. Each thread has a 2 MiB stack, on every main platform. So fifty thousand threads would reserve a hundred gigabytes of stack. The operating system limits how many threads a process may create long before that. The answer is the C answer, an event loop, with the Go answer on top of it. That is a cheap unit of concurrency that looks like a thread. In Rust that is an async task, and the runtime that schedules tasks is a library, not part of the language. The one almost everyone uses is tokio, and it becomes the first line in the [dependencies] section of Cargo.toml:

$ cargo add tokio@1 --features rt-multi-thread,net,io-util,macros
      Adding tokio v1.53.2 to dependencies

That command also writes Cargo.lock, with the exact version of tokio and of the fourteen crates it depends on. Every later build uses those versions until you ask for new ones, which is what the lock file is for. The server is a second binary in the same crate, src/bin/kv_async.rs. Its serving function looks like the thread version with one word added after every read and write:

/// The replies are written into a Vec first, with the ordinary synchronous command
/// code from the library, and then sent with one awaited write. The lock is never held
/// across an await, so a plain std Mutex is the right one.
async fn serve(stream: TcpStream, store: Arc<Mutex<Store>>) -> io::Result<()> {
    let mut reader = BufReader::new(stream);
    let mut out = Vec::new();
    loop {
        match read_command(&mut reader).await {
            Ok(Some(args)) => {
                let mut store = store.lock().unwrap_or_else(|e| e.into_inner());
                cmd::execute(args, &mut store, &mut out)?;
            }
            Ok(None) => return Ok(()),
            Err(e) if e.kind() == ErrorKind::InvalidData => {
                resp::write_error(&mut out, "ERR Protocol error")?;
                reader.get_mut().write_all(&out).await?;
                return Err(e);
            }
            Err(e) => return Err(e),
        }
        if reader.buffer().is_empty() {
            reader.get_mut().write_all(&out).await?;
            out.clear();
        }
    }
}
rust/ch08/src/bin/kv_async.rs · region serve

An async fn does not run when it is called. It returns a future, which is a value that describes the work, and the runtime runs it. Each .await is a point where the task may stop and let another task use the thread, when the socket has nothing to give yet. That is the event loop from the C section, with the bookkeeping done by the compiler. The parser from chapter 4 has to be written again with .await after each read. A function that blocks must not be called from a task, because it stops the thread and every task on it:

/// The parser from chapter 4 again, with `.await` after every read. A blocking read
/// would stop the whole thread; an awaited one lets the thread run another task.
async fn read_line(r: &mut BufReader<TcpStream>) -> io::Result<Option<Vec<u8>>> {
    let mut line = Vec::new();
    if r.read_until(b'\n', &mut line).await? == 0 {
        return Ok(None);
    }
    if line.pop() != Some(b'\n') {
        return Err(ErrorKind::UnexpectedEof.into());
    }
    if line.last() == Some(&b'\r') {
        line.pop();
    }
    Ok(Some(line))
}

fn parse_len(digits: &[u8]) -> io::Result<usize> {
    std::str::from_utf8(digits)
        .ok()
        .and_then(|s| s.parse().ok())
        .ok_or_else(protocol_error)
}

async fn read_command(r: &mut BufReader<TcpStream>) -> io::Result<Option<Command>> {
    let Some(line) = read_line(r).await? else {
        return Ok(None);
    };
    if line.first() != Some(&b'*') {
        let words = line.split(|b| *b == b' ').filter(|w| !w.is_empty());
        return Ok(Some(words.map(<[u8]>::to_vec).collect()));
    }
    let n = parse_len(&line[1..])?;
    if n > MAX_ARGS {
        return Err(protocol_error());
    }
    let mut args = Vec::with_capacity(n);
    for _ in 0..n {
        let line = read_line(r).await?.ok_or(ErrorKind::UnexpectedEof)?;
        if line.first() != Some(&b'$') {
            return Err(protocol_error());
        }
        let size = parse_len(&line[1..])?;
        if size > MAX_BULK {
            return Err(protocol_error());
        }
        let mut buf = vec![0u8; size + 2];
        r.read_exact(&mut buf).await?;
        if &buf[size..] != b"\r\n" {
            return Err(protocol_error());
        }
        buf.truncate(size);
        args.push(buf);
    }
    Ok(Some(args))
}

Exercise 08.3RustAccept tasks instead of threads

Write the main of the tokio version. Mark it #[tokio::main] and make it async. Bind a tokio::net::TcpListener. In a loop, await accept and clone the Arc. Then tokio::spawn an async block that calls serve. The shape is the thread version with thread::spawn replaced and .await added.

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> io::Result<()> {
    // your code here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> io::Result<()> {
    let port = port();
    let listener = TcpListener::bind(format!("0.0.0.0:{port}")).await?;
    eprintln!("kv (tokio) listening on port {port}");
    let store = Arc::new(Mutex::new(Store::new()));
    loop {
        let (stream, _) = listener.accept().await?;
        let store = Arc::clone(&store);
        // a task, not a thread: it costs a few hundred bytes and no kernel resources
        tokio::spawn(async move {
            if let Err(e) = serve(stream, store).await {
                eprintln!("client: {e}");
            }
        });
    }
}
rust/ch08/src/bin/kv_async.rs · region main
⚠️A lock held across an await Rust

Hold a std::sync::Mutex guard across .await and the task will not compile. The error is future cannot be sent between threads safely. The note says that MutexGuard is not Send, and that "await occurs here, with mut guard maybe used later". The reason is real. A task may resume on another thread after an await, and a mutex must be unlocked by the thread that locked it. The serving function above takes the lock, runs the command into a buffer, and drops the guard before the write is awaited. The tokio crate has its own mutex for the cases where a lock must be held across an await. It is slower, which is why the code avoids needing it.

✅Keep the blocking code and call it from the task Rust

The command code in the library did not change for tokio. It writes replies into any Write, and a Vec<u8> is one. The task fills a vector with the synchronous code and sends the vector with one awaited write. That keeps the async surface of a program small, which makes the compiler's messages shorter and the code easier to test without a runtime. The Go version has the same shape by accident, because Go has no async and every function is callable from a goroutine.

What the four servers measure#

Each server was given fifty connections and two hundred thousand requests by redis-benchmark, driven from four threads, on a machine with twelve cores. The numbers are requests a second, and chapter 12 explains how to take and read such numbers properly.

C, poll, one thread      SET  72,700   GET  79,900
Go, goroutines           SET  88,700   GET  79,800
Rust, threads            SET  99,900   GET  88,800
Rust, tokio              SET  99,800   GET  88,800

Three things in the table are worth more than the numbers. The C server uses one core and is within a third of the others, which is why Redis stayed single-threaded for so long. The Go and Rust servers use every core. A lock on one store stops them from using the cores well. Chapter 12 returns to that. And the tokio version is no faster than the thread version at fifty clients, because fifty threads are cheap. Its advantage starts where threads run out, at tens of thousands of connections.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
Serving many clientsWeakStrongOKC needs 200 lines of event loop and Go one keyword, while Rust needs Arc, Mutex and a clone per thread.
Using every coreWeakStrongStrongThe C loop is one thread by design. Go and Rust spread clients over every core.
A forgotten lockn/aWeakStrongGo compiles it and the detector may catch it. Rust refuses to compile it.
Memory for a clientStrongStrongOKC sizes two buffers and a goroutine is a few kilobytes, while a Rust thread is 2 MiB until tokio fixes that at the cost of a dependency.
Reading the codeWeakStrongOKThe C loop is subtle and the Go version is the chapter 7 code, while the tokio version is a second copy of the parser.
WindowsWeakStrongStrongpoll is POSIX. Both runtimes hide completion ports.
Next · Building kv09Expiry18 min

Expiry#

Building kv · Chapter 09· 18 min read· 287 lines of code

A cache forgets. This chapter gives kv keys that die on time. It adds two options to SET, and the commands EXPIRE and TTL. A key dies in two ways, when it is touched and when a sweep finds it. The chapter is about time, which every language reads from the same two clocks. It is also about background work, which each language runs in its own way.

Redis is used as a cache more than as anything else, and a cache entry is only useful with a lifetime. A session that lasts an hour is a key with a deadline. So is a rate limit that resets each minute, and a lock that must not outlive a crashed holder. After the deadline the key must behave as if it had been deleted, whether or not anybody asks for it.

That last clause is where the work is. A key that is read after its deadline is the simple case. The read notices and deletes it. A key that nobody reads again must still go, or the server fills with dead entries. So there are two mechanisms. Lazy expiry checks the deadline on every access. Active expiry runs on a timer and removes dead keys that nobody has touched. Redis does both, and so does kv.

Time and background workCGoRust
A deadlinean int64_t of milliseconds, 0 for nonea time.Time, zero for nonean Option<Instant>
Reading the clockclock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC)time.Now(), which carries both clocksInstant::now()
Lazy expirykv_store_find unlinks a dead entryGet reports it absent, the sweep removes itget filters it out, the sweep removes it
The sweep runsin the event loop, between poll callson a goroutine with a tickeron a thread with a sleep, then a tokio task with an interval
A bounded sweepa cursor and a budgeta budget on the random map orderretain, which walks everything
A test without sleepingnot writtena clock the test passes ina clock the test passes in

The whole chapter rests on one question, "is this entry dead yet?", and each language answers it in its own shape. The C entry stores a deadline as a number and treats zero as never. Go stores a time.Time and treats the zero time as never. Rust stores an Option, so "never" is a case the compiler makes every reader handle:

C
/* 0 means the key never expires */
typedef struct kv_entry {
    /* ... key, value ... */
    int64_t expires_at;
    struct kv_entry *next;
} kv_entry;

static bool kv_expired(const kv_entry *e, int64_t now) {
    return e->expires_at != 0 && e->expires_at <= now;
}
Go
// the zero time means the key never expires
type entry struct {
	val     []byte
	expires time.Time
}

func (e entry) expired(now time.Time) bool {
	return !e.expires.IsZero() && !now.Before(e.expires)
}
Rust
// None means the key never expires
struct Entry {
    val: Vec<u8>,
    expires: Option<Instant>,
}

impl Entry {
    fn expired(&self, now: Instant) -> bool {
        self.expires.is_some_and(|at| at <= now)
    }
}
📜Two clocks, and why a deadline uses the boring one

Every operating system offers two clocks. The wall clock says what time it is, and it can jump, when an administrator sets it or when the network corrects it. The monotonic clock says how much time has passed since some moment, and it only moves forward. A deadline measured on the wall clock expires at once when the clock jumps ahead an hour, and never when it jumps back. Redis stores expiry times as Unix timestamps, on the wall clock, because it must write them to disk and read them back on another machine. Its documentation warns that setting the computer time forward expires keys at once. The server kv keeps everything in memory, so it uses the monotonic clock. A deadline is then a reading of a clock that only moves forward, and the number means nothing to another process.

C: a deadline on the entry, and a sweep with a cursor#

The clock is one function, in a file of its own because chapter 10 will need it too. CLOCK_MONOTONIC is POSIX, the Portable Operating System Interface, and the call is clock_gettime:

/* Milliseconds on the monotonic clock, which only moves forward. It is not the time of
 * day: that clock jumps when the system time is set, and a deadline must not. */
int64_t kv_now_ms(void);
c/ch09/src/clock.h · region clock

An entry gains a field. The comment says what zero means, which matters because every check in the file depends on it:

/* One key and its value. expires_at is a deadline in milliseconds on the monotonic
 * clock, or 0 for a key that never expires. The key, the value and the next entry are
 * owned. */
typedef struct kv_entry {
    char *key;
    size_t klen;
    char *val;
    size_t vlen;
    int64_t expires_at;
    struct kv_entry *next;
} kv_entry;
c/ch09/src/store.h · region entry

Lazy expiry goes into kv_store_find, which now takes the current time. An entry past its deadline is unlinked on the spot, which is why the walk uses the pointer-to-pointer form from chapter 6 again. The special value now = 0 turns the check off, for the callers that have already decided the key is alive:

static bool kv_expired(const kv_entry *e, int64_t now) {
    return e->expires_at != 0 && e->expires_at <= now;
}

/* Unlink and free the entry that *link points to. */
static void kv_unlink(kv_store *s, kv_entry **link) {
    kv_entry *e = *link;
    *link = e->next;
    kv_entry_free(e);
    s->len--;
}

kv_entry *kv_store_find(kv_store *s, const char *key, size_t klen, int64_t now) {
    if (s->cap == 0) {
        return NULL;
    }
    size_t i = kv_hash(key, klen) & (s->cap - 1);
    for (kv_entry **link = &s->buckets[i]; *link != NULL; link = &(*link)->next) {
        kv_entry *e = *link;
        if (kv_bytes_eq(e->key, e->klen, key, klen)) {
            if (kv_expired(e, now)) {
                kv_unlink(s, link); /* lazy expiry: the key dies when it is touched */
                return NULL;
            }
            return e;
        }
    }
    return NULL;
}
c/ch09/src/store.c · region expired
Exercise 09.1CSweep a little at a time

Write kv_store_sweep. Start at the bucket the store's cursor names, walk its chain, unlink every expired entry, and move to the next bucket. Stop after looking at budget entries or after one full round of the buckets, and leave the cursor where the next call should start. Count what you removed. A bucket index wraps with the mask cap - 1, because cap is a power of two.

size_t kv_store_sweep(kv_store *s, int64_t now, size_t budget) {
    size_t removed = 0;
    size_t seen = 0;
    /* your code here */
    return removed;
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
size_t kv_store_sweep(kv_store *s, int64_t now, size_t budget) {
    size_t removed = 0;
    size_t seen = 0;
    for (size_t n = 0; n < s->cap && seen < budget; n++) {
        kv_entry **link = &s->buckets[s->cursor];
        while (*link != NULL && seen < budget) {
            seen++;
            if (kv_expired(*link, now)) {
                kv_unlink(s, link); /* *link now points at the next entry */
                removed++;
            } else {
                link = &(*link)->next;
            }
        }
        s->cursor = (s->cursor + 1) & (s->cap - 1);
    }
    return removed;
}
c/ch09/src/store.c · region sweep

The budget matters because the sweep runs on the one thread that serves every client. A sweep that walks ten million keys stops the server for the time that takes. The cursor makes a long walk into many short ones. Redis uses a different bound, a random sample, but the purpose is the same. The sweep is called from the event loop. The loop now waits at most a tenth of a second, so that keys expire on time even when no client is talking:

/* Wait at most 100 ms, so that keys expire even when no client is talking. */
int ready = poll(cs.pfds, (nfds_t)(cs.len + 1), 100);
if (ready < 0) {
    if (errno == EINTR) {
        continue;
    }
    perror("poll");
    break;
}
kv_store_sweep(store, kv_now_ms(), 200);
c/ch09/src/server.c · region tick

The SET command grows two options, and a plain SET removes any deadline, because that is what Redis does and what clients expect. A number that is not a number, and a lifetime of zero, get the two error texts Redis uses:

/* SET key value [EX seconds | PX milliseconds]. A plain SET removes any deadline, as
 * Redis does; a SET with EX or PX sets a new one. */
static bool kv_exec_set(const kv_cmd *c, kv_store *s, kv_buf *out, int64_t now) {
    if (c->argc != 3 && c->argc != 5) {
        return kv_reply_arity(out, "set");
    }
    int64_t ttl_ms = 0;
    if (c->argc == 5) {
        long long n;
        bool ex = kv_arg_is(c, 3, "EX");
        if (!ex && !kv_arg_is(c, 3, "PX")) {
            return kv_reply_error(out, "ERR syntax error");
        }
        if (!kv_parse_int(c->argv[4], c->arglen[4], &n)) {
            return kv_reply_error(out, "ERR value is not an integer or out of range");
        }
        if (n <= 0 || n > INT64_MAX / 1000) {
            return kv_reply_error(out, "ERR invalid expire time in 'set' command");
        }
        ttl_ms = ex ? n * 1000 : n;
    }
    if (!kv_store_set(s, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1], c->argv[2], c->arglen[2])) {
        return false;
    }
    kv_entry *e = kv_store_find(s, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1], 0);
    e->expires_at = ttl_ms ? now + ttl_ms : 0;
    return kv_reply_simple(out, "OK");
}
c/ch09/src/cmd.c · region set

EXPIRE and TTL are short. The one surprise is that EXPIRE with zero or a negative number deletes the key at once. The Redis documentation says a non-positive timeout results in the key "being deleted rather than expired", and the test file checks for it:

/* EXPIRE key seconds: 1 if the key exists and got a deadline, 0 if it does not exist. */
static bool kv_exec_expire(const kv_cmd *c, kv_store *s, kv_buf *out, int64_t now) {
    long long secs;
    if (!kv_parse_int(c->argv[2], c->arglen[2], &secs)) {
        return kv_reply_error(out, "ERR value is not an integer or out of range");
    }
    if (secs > INT64_MAX / 1000) { /* now + secs * 1000 must not overflow */
        return kv_reply_error(out, "ERR invalid expire time in 'expire' command");
    }
    kv_entry *e = kv_store_find(s, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1], now);
    if (e == NULL) {
        return kv_reply_int(out, 0);
    }
    if (secs <= 0) {
        kv_store_del(s, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1]); /* a deadline in the past is a DEL */
    } else {
        e->expires_at = now + secs * 1000;
    }
    return kv_reply_int(out, 1);
}

/* TTL key: the seconds left, -1 for a key with no deadline, -2 for a missing key. */
static bool kv_exec_ttl(const kv_cmd *c, kv_store *s, kv_buf *out, int64_t now) {
    kv_entry *e = kv_store_find(s, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1], now);
    if (e == NULL) {
        return kv_reply_int(out, -2);
    }
    if (e->expires_at == 0) {
        return kv_reply_int(out, -1);
    }
    return kv_reply_int(out, (e->expires_at - now + 500) / 1000); /* nearest, as Redis */
}
c/ch09/src/cmd.c · region ttl
⚠️A find that deletes C

kv_store_find can now free the entry it is asked about. Any pointer to that entry held across the call is a pointer to freed memory. Inside kv_exec_incr the code finds the entry and reads its deadline. Then it calls kv_store_set and finds the entry again. The first pointer must not be trusted after a call that can change the table. This rule was true in chapter 6. It became easier to break in this chapter, because a function named find no longer sounds like one that writes.

Go: a ticker, and a clock the test can hold still#

A Go time.Time carries both clocks. The documentation says a value from time.Now "contains both a wall clock reading and a monotonic clock reading". Sub and Before use the monotonic one. So a deadline is a plain time.Time, and a zero value means none:

// entry is a value and its deadline. A zero time means the key never expires. The
// deadline is a time.Time, which carries a monotonic reading, so a change to the
// system clock does not move it.
type entry struct {
	val     []byte
	expires time.Time
}

func (e entry) expired(now time.Time) bool {
	return !e.expires.IsZero() && !now.Before(e.expires)
}
go/ch09/internal/store/store.go · region entry

Every method takes now as a parameter instead of calling time.Now itself. That is a small decision with a large effect. The command code reads the clock once per command. So every check inside one command, such as the expiry test and the deadline it sets, sees the same moment. And a test can pass any time it likes, which is how the expiry test runs in a millisecond without sleeping:

// TestExpiry uses a clock the test controls, so it never sleeps.
func TestExpiry(t *testing.T) {
	s := New()
	t0 := time.Now()
	s.Set([]byte("k"), []byte("v"))
	s.SetExpiry([]byte("k"), t0.Add(time.Second), t0)
	if _, ok := s.Get([]byte("k"), t0.Add(999*time.Millisecond)); !ok {
		t.Fatal("key died early")
	}
	if _, ok := s.Get([]byte("k"), t0.Add(time.Second)); ok {
		t.Fatal("key outlived its deadline")
	}
	if n := s.Sweep(t0.Add(time.Second), 100); n != 1 {
		t.Fatalf("sweep removed %d keys, want 1", n)
	}
	if s.Len() != 0 {
		t.Fatalf("store holds %d keys after the sweep, want 0", s.Len())
	}
}
go/ch09/internal/store/store_test.go · region expiry-test

Get holds the read lock, and a dead key cannot be removed under a read lock. So Get reports the key absent and leaves the removal to the sweep. The sweep takes the write lock, walks the map, and stops after a budget. Go promises no order for a map walk, and the runtime starts each walk at a different place. That gives the sweep a fresh sample without any cursor:

// SetExpiry gives a live key a deadline. It reports whether the key was there.
func (s *Store) SetExpiry(key []byte, at time.Time, now time.Time) bool {
	s.mu.Lock()
	defer s.mu.Unlock()
	e, ok := s.m[string(key)]
	if !ok || e.expired(now) {
		return false
	}
	e.expires = at
	s.m[string(key)] = e
	return true
}

// TTL returns the time left, and a status: -2 for a missing key, -1 for a key with
// no deadline, 0 for a key with one.
func (s *Store) TTL(key []byte, now time.Time) (time.Duration, int) {
	s.mu.RLock()
	defer s.mu.RUnlock()
	e, ok := s.m[string(key)]
	if !ok || e.expired(now) {
		return 0, -2
	}
	if e.expires.IsZero() {
		return 0, -1
	}
	return e.expires.Sub(now), 0
}

// Sweep removes keys past their deadline, looking at no more than budget of them. The
// map iterates in a different order each time, so each call sees a fresh sample.
func (s *Store) Sweep(now time.Time, budget int) int {
	s.mu.Lock()
	defer s.mu.Unlock()
	removed := 0
	for k, e := range s.m {
		if budget == 0 {
			break
		}
		budget--
		if e.expired(now) {
			delete(s.m, k)
			removed++
		}
	}
	return removed
}
go/ch09/internal/store/store.go · region expire

Start a goroutine in main that calls s.Sweep ten times a second with a budget of 200. Use time.Tick, which returns a channel that delivers the current time on every tick, and range over it. The goroutine takes the store's lock like any other, and the ticker makes it sleep in between.

s := store.New()
go func() {
	// your code here
}()

// Ten times a second, a goroutine of its own removes keys past their deadline, so
// that a key nobody reads still dies on time. It takes the store's lock like any
// other goroutine, and the ticker makes it sleep in between.
go func() {

go/ch09/cmd/kv/main.go · region sweeper
✅Pass the clock in Go

A function that calls time.Now can only be tested with real time, which means tests that sleep and tests that fail on a slow machine. A function that takes now can be tested with any time at all. The store does the second. The command layer reads the clock once and passes it down. The same shape works in Rust and in C, and the C version of kv does it too, in kv_exec, though without the test.

Rust: an Option for the deadline, a thread and then a task for the sweep#

Rust separates the clocks into two types. SystemTime is the wall clock. Instant is the monotonic one, and its documentation says it "will never go backwards". A deadline is Option<Instant>, so the "none" case is a case the compiler makes you handle rather than a zero you must remember:

/// A value and its deadline. `None` means the key never expires. An `Instant` is a
/// reading of the monotonic clock, so a change to the system time cannot move it.
struct Entry {
    val: Vec<u8>,
    expires: Option<Instant>,
}

impl Entry {
    fn expired(&self, now: Instant) -> bool {
        self.expires.is_some_and(|at| at <= now)
    }
}
rust/ch09/src/store.rs · region entry

get borrows the store immutably, so it filters a dead key out and leaves it for the sweep, as the Go version does. TTL has three answers, and the enum Ttl gives each a name. The command code then matches on three names instead of on -2, -1 and a number:

/// Give a live key a deadline. Reports whether the key was there.
pub fn set_expiry(&mut self, key: &[u8], at: Instant, now: Instant) -> bool {
    match self.map.get_mut(key) {
        Some(e) if !e.expired(now) => {
            e.expires = Some(at);
            true
        }
        _ => false,
    }
}

pub fn ttl(&self, key: &[u8], now: Instant) -> Ttl {
    match self.map.get(key) {
        None => Ttl::Missing,
        Some(e) if e.expired(now) => Ttl::Missing,
        Some(Entry { expires: None, .. }) => Ttl::Never,
        Some(Entry {
            expires: Some(at), ..
        }) => Ttl::Left(*at - now),
    }
}

/// Remove every key past its deadline. `retain` walks the whole map, which is fine
/// for a store of this size; Redis samples instead, to bound the time.
pub fn sweep(&mut self, now: Instant) -> usize {
    let before = self.map.len();
    self.map.retain(|_, e| !e.expired(now));
    before - self.map.len()
}
rust/ch09/src/store.rs · region expire

The SET options are a second match arm with five elements. The inner match on the unit and the parsed number has one arm for each error text. The compiler checks that the arms are complete:

(b"SET", [_, key, val]) => {
    store.set(mem::take(key), mem::take(val));
    resp::write_simple(w, "OK")
}
(b"SET", [_, key, val, unit, n]) => {
    let ttl = match (unit.to_ascii_uppercase().as_slice(), parse_i64(n)) {
        (b"EX" | b"PX", None) => {
            return resp::write_error(w, "ERR value is not an integer or out of range");
        }
        (b"EX", Some(n)) if n > 0 && n <= i64::MAX / 1000 => Duration::from_secs(n as u64),
        (b"PX", Some(n)) if n > 0 => Duration::from_millis(n as u64),
        (b"EX" | b"PX", Some(_)) => {
            return resp::write_error(w, "ERR invalid expire time in 'set' command");
        }
        _ => return resp::write_error(w, "ERR syntax error"),
    };
    let Some(at) = now.checked_add(ttl) else {
        return resp::write_error(w, "ERR invalid expire time in 'set' command");
    };
    let key = mem::take(key);
    store.set(key.clone(), mem::take(val));
    store.set_expiry(&key, at, now);
    resp::write_simple(w, "OK")
}
(b"SET", _) => write_arity(w, "set"),
rust/ch09/src/cmd.rs · region set
Exercise 09.3RustSweep from a thread, then from a task

In the thread version, spawn a thread that sleeps 100 milliseconds, locks the store, and calls sweep with Instant::now(), forever. It needs its own clone of the Arc. In the tokio version, spawn a task instead, with tokio::time::interval and tick().await. The tokio feature time must be added to Cargo.toml for the interval to exist.

let sweeper = Arc::clone(&store);
thread::spawn(move || {
    // your code here
});
Stuck?Show the full listing
// Ten times a second, a thread of its own removes keys past their deadline. It
// takes the lock like any other thread, and sleeps in between.
let sweeper = Arc::clone(&store);
thread::spawn(move || {
    loop {
        thread::sleep(Duration::from_millis(100));
        let mut store = sweeper.lock().unwrap_or_else(|e| e.into_inner());
        store.sweep(Instant::now());
    }
});
rust/ch09/src/main.rs · region sweeper
// The sweep is a task like the clients. The await on the interval costs no thread.
let sweeper = Arc::clone(&store);
tokio::spawn(async move {
    let mut tick = tokio::time::interval(Duration::from_millis(100));
    loop {
        tick.tick().await;
        let mut store = sweeper.lock().unwrap_or_else(|e| e.into_inner());
        store.sweep(Instant::now());
    }
});
rust/ch09/src/bin/kv_async.rs · region sweeper

The two sweepers differ in one way that matters. The thread version occupies a thread that spends almost all its time asleep, which is cheap at one sweeper and wasteful at a thousand timers. The task version occupies nothing while it waits. The await on the interval hands the thread back to the runtime, and the runtime wakes the task when the tick arrives. That is the same trade as chapter 8, seen from the other side.

⚠️retain walks the whole map Rust

The Rust sweep has no budget. retain visits every entry under the lock, which is fine at ten thousand keys and a pause at ten million. The C version has a cursor, and the Go version has a budget on an iteration that starts at a random place. A Rust HashMap iterates in a fixed order for a given state, so taking the first 200 entries would sweep the same 200 every time. A bounded sweep in Rust needs its own cursor, or a random sample, as Redis takes. The book leaves it unbounded and says so, which is the honest version of a difference between the three standard libraries.

Background work, three ways#

The sweep is the first work in kv that no client asked for. Each language had a natural place to put it. In C it went into the loop that already existed, with a timeout on poll, and the loop does the sweep between clients. There is no second thread and no lock. In Go it went into a goroutine with a ticker, and the lock on the store made that safe without a thought. In Rust it went into a thread, and then into a task, and the compiler checked that the store was shared correctly both times.

Each placement reflects the chapter before. The C loop is one thread, so the sweep must share it and must be short, which is why it has a budget. The Go and Rust servers have many threads of control, so the sweep can be one more. The cost is a lock that every client also pays. Chapter 10 adds the second kind of background work, writing to disk, where the cost of sharing is higher and the three answers differ more.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
A deadlineOKStrongStrongC uses zero to mean none, which the comments must say. Go's zero Time and Rust's Option say it in the type.
The clockOKStrongStrongC picks the clock by name, while Go carries both in one value and Rust gives each a type.
Lazy expiryStrongOKOKC deletes on find. Go and Rust must leave it to the sweep because of the read lock or the borrow.
The sweepStrongStrongWeakC has a cursor and Go a random sample. Rust's retain walks the whole map.
Testing timeWeakStrongStrongGo and Rust tests pass a clock in. The C store has no test runner yet.
Background workOKStrongStrongC has to make room in the loop. A goroutine or a task is one more of what the server already has.
Next · Building kv10Persistence18 min

Persistence#

Building kv · Chapter 10· 18 min read· 338 lines of code

Stop kv and every key is gone. This chapter gives it an append-only file, a log of every write in the bytes the client sent. A restart replays the log through the parser from chapter 4, and the store comes back. The chapter is about files, about fsync, and about what a crash can and cannot take from you.

A cache may forget, but a store that forgets on every restart is not much use. The simplest way to remember is to write down every command that changed something, in order. At startup, run them all again. Redis calls this the append-only file, and its documentation says the commands "are logged using the same format as the Redis protocol itself". That one decision means the parser you already have is also the loader, and a text editor can read the file.

Two details make it work. The first is expiry. A command such as SET k v EX 60 means "sixty seconds from now", and "now" is gone by the time the file is replayed. So the file stores a deadline on the wall clock instead, as PEXPIREAT k 1760000000000, which is a Redis command too. The second is fsync. A write call hands bytes to the kernel, which keeps them in memory and writes them to the disk when it likes. fsync waits until they are on the disk. The first survives a crash of the process. Only the second survives a crash of the machine, and it is slow.

The fileCGoRust
Open for appendingopen(path, O_WRONLY | O_CREAT | O_APPEND)os.OpenFile with the same flagsOpenOptions::new().create(true).append(true)
One recordencode into a kv_buf, then writeencode into a bufio.Writer, then Flushencode into a BufWriter, then flush
Forcing it to diskfsync(fd)f.Sync()file.sync_all()
The everysec timerthe event loop's ticka goroutine on a tickerthe sweeper thread, every tenth tick
Replaykv_parse in a loop over the fileresp.ReadCommand on a bufio.Readerresp::read_command on a BufReader
Log and store togetherone thread, so no questionthe log has its own lockone Mutex around a Db with both
A truncated last recorddropped, with a messagedropped, with a messagedropped, with a message
📜Why the log is in RESP, and why Redis rewrites it

The append-only file arrived in Redis 1.1, and it has kept the protocol format since. The persistence documentation gives the reason in practical terms. The file is "an easy to understand and parse format". If you flush the database by mistake, "you can still save your data set just by stopping the server, removing the latest command, and restarting". The cost is size. A counter incremented a hundred times is one key in memory and a hundred records on disk. Redis solves that by rewriting the file in the background, as "the minimal set of operations needed to create the current data set". The server kv does not rewrite, and the chapter says where that leaves it.

C: a file descriptor, a write, and an fsync on a timer#

The file is opened once, with O_APPEND, which makes every write go to the end of the file whatever else has happened to it. The policy is one of three, and they are the three that Redis offers under the names always, everysec and no:

/* How often the file is forced to disk. Redis has the same three settings. */
typedef enum {
    KV_FSYNC_ALWAYS,   /* after every command: slow and safe */
    KV_FSYNC_EVERYSEC, /* once a second: lose at most a second of writes */
    KV_FSYNC_NO        /* never: the operating system decides */
} kv_fsync_policy;

typedef struct {
    int fd; /* -1 when persistence is off */
    kv_fsync_policy policy;
    bool dirty;        /* bytes written since the last fsync */
    int64_t last_sync; /* monotonic ms of the last fsync */
} kv_aof;
c/ch10/src/aof.h · region policy
bool kv_aof_open(kv_aof *a, const char *path, kv_fsync_policy policy) {
    /* O_APPEND: every write goes to the end, even if another process appends too */
    int fd = open(path, O_WRONLY | O_CREAT | O_APPEND, 0644);
    if (fd < 0) {
        perror(path);
        return false;
    }
    *a = (kv_aof){fd, policy, false, kv_now_ms()};
    return true;
}
c/ch10/src/aof.c · region open

Recording a command means encoding it as an array of bulk strings, which is the format the client sent and the format kv_parse reads. The one rewrite turns a relative expiry into an absolute one, with the wall clock from a second function in clock.c. The record goes to the kernel with write at once, and to the disk with fsync only when the policy says so:

static bool kv_write_all(int fd, const char *p, size_t n) {
    size_t off = 0;
    while (off < n) {
        ssize_t w = write(fd, p + off, n - off);
        if (w < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) {
                continue;
            }
            perror("aof write");
            return false;
        }
        off += (size_t)w;
    }
    return true;
}

/* PEXPIREAT key <ms since 1970>, as the absolute form of EX, PX and EXPIRE. */
static bool kv_encode_pexpireat(kv_buf *out, const char *key, size_t klen, int64_t at) {
    char digits[32];
    int n = snprintf(digits, sizeof digits, "%lld", (long long)at);
    const char *argv[3] = {"PEXPIREAT", key, digits};
    size_t arglen[3] = {9, klen, (size_t)n};
    return kv_aof_encode(out, 3, argv, arglen);
}

bool kv_aof_record(kv_aof *a, const kv_cmd *c, int64_t wall_now) {
    if (a->fd < 0) {
        return true;
    }
    kv_buf out = {0};
    bool ok;
    long long n;
    if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "SET") && c->argc == 5 && kv_parse_int(c->argv[4], c->arglen[4], &n) &&
        n > 0) {
        /* SET key value EX|PX n becomes SET key value, then PEXPIREAT key <absolute> */
        int64_t at = wall_now + (kv_arg_is(c, 3, "EX") ? n * 1000 : n);
        ok = kv_aof_encode(&out, 3, c->argv, c->arglen) &&
             kv_encode_pexpireat(&out, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1], at);
    } else if (kv_arg_is(c, 0, "EXPIRE") && c->argc == 3 &&
               kv_parse_int(c->argv[2], c->arglen[2], &n)) {
        if (n <= 0) {
            const char *argv[2] = {"DEL", c->argv[1]};
            size_t arglen[2] = {3, c->arglen[1]};
            ok = kv_aof_encode(&out, 2, argv, arglen);
        } else {
            ok = kv_encode_pexpireat(&out, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1], wall_now + n * 1000);
        }
    } else {
        ok = kv_aof_encode(&out, c->argc, c->argv, c->arglen);
    }
    ok = ok && kv_write_all(a->fd, out.data, out.len);
    kv_buf_free(&out);
    if (!ok) {
        return false;
    }
    a->dirty = true;
    if (a->policy == KV_FSYNC_ALWAYS) {
        kv_aof_tick(a, INT64_MAX);
    }
    return true;
}
c/ch10/src/aof.c · region record
void kv_aof_tick(kv_aof *a, int64_t now) {
    if (a->fd < 0 || !a->dirty || a->policy == KV_FSYNC_NO) {
        return;
    }
    if (a->policy == KV_FSYNC_EVERYSEC && now - a->last_sync < 1000) {
        return;
    }
    if (fsync(a->fd) < 0) {
        perror("fsync");
    }
    a->dirty = false;
    a->last_sync = now;
}
c/ch10/src/aof.c · region tick

The event loop has woken ten times a second since chapter 9, for the sweep, and the fsync for the everysec policy joins it. In the input function, a write command is recorded after it runs and before its reply is sent. So a client never sees OK for a write the file does not have:

/* The command ran; now it goes to the file, before the reply goes to the client. */
if (kv_cmd_is_write(&cmd) && !kv_aof_record(aof, &cmd, kv_wall_ms())) {
    return false;
}
c/ch10/src/server.c · region log
Exercise 10.1CReplay the file

Write kv_aof_load. Open the file read-only, and return 0 when it does not exist, because a missing file is an empty store. Read it in chunks into a kv_buf. After each chunk, run kv_parse in a loop. Execute each complete command with kv_exec into a reply buffer that nobody reads. Count the commands. Bytes left over at the end are a truncated last command. Report them and drop them.

long kv_aof_load(const char *path, kv_store *store) {
    int fd = open(path, O_RDONLY);
    if (fd < 0) {
        return errno == ENOENT ? 0 : -1;
    }
    /* your code here */
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
long kv_aof_load(const char *path, kv_store *store) {
    int fd = open(path, O_RDONLY);
    if (fd < 0) {
        return errno == ENOENT ? 0 : -1; /* no file yet is an empty store, not an error */
    }
    kv_buf in = {0};
    kv_buf out = {0}; /* the replies, which nobody reads */
    char tmp[65536];
    long applied = 0;
    for (;;) {
        ssize_t n = read(fd, tmp, sizeof tmp);
        if (n < 0 && errno == EINTR) {
            continue;
        }
        if (n <= 0) {
            break;
        }
        if (!kv_buf_append(&in, tmp, (size_t)n)) {
            applied = -1;
            break;
        }
        /* the same parser and the same executor as a live client */
        for (;;) {
            kv_cmd cmd;
            size_t used = 0;
            kv_parse_status st = kv_parse(in.data, in.len, &cmd, &used);
            if (st != KV_PARSE_OK) {
                break;
            }
            kv_exec(&cmd, store, &out);
            out.len = 0;
            kv_buf_consume(&in, used);
            applied++;
        }
    }
    if (in.len > 0) {
        fprintf(stderr, "aof: dropped %zu trailing bytes of a truncated command\n", in.len);
    }
    kv_buf_free(&in);
    kv_buf_free(&out);
    close(fd);
    return applied;
}
c/ch10/src/aof.c · region load

The absolute deadline needs a command of its own, PEXPIREAT, which turns a wall-clock time back into a monotonic one for the store. A deadline already in the past deletes the key, which is how an expired key stays expired across a restart:

/* PEXPIREAT key <ms since 1970>: the absolute form that the append-only file uses. The
 * wall-clock deadline is turned into a monotonic one for the store. */
static bool kv_exec_pexpireat(const kv_cmd *c, kv_store *s, kv_buf *out, int64_t now) {
    long long at;
    if (!kv_parse_int(c->argv[2], c->arglen[2], &at)) {
        return kv_reply_error(out, "ERR value is not an integer or out of range");
    }
    kv_entry *e = kv_store_find(s, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1], now);
    if (e == NULL) {
        return kv_reply_int(out, 0);
    }
    int64_t left = at - kv_wall_ms();
    if (left <= 0) {
        kv_store_del(s, c->argv[1], c->arglen[1]);
    } else {
        e->expires_at = now + left;
    }
    return kv_reply_int(out, 1);
}
c/ch10/src/cmd.c · region pexpireat

Startup replays the file before it serves the first client, so that no client sees an empty store, and then opens the file for appending:

/* KV_AOF names the file. Replay it first, then open it for appending. */
kv_aof aof = {-1, KV_FSYNC_EVERYSEC, false, 0};
const char *path = getenv("KV_AOF");
if (path != NULL && *path != '\0') {
    kv_fsync_policy policy;
    if (!kv_fsync_policy_parse(getenv("KV_FSYNC"), &policy)) {
        fprintf(stderr, "KV_FSYNC must be always, everysec or no\n");
        return 1;
    }
    long applied = kv_aof_load(path, &store);
    if (applied < 0) {
        perror(path);
        return 1;
    }
    fprintf(stderr, "kv replayed %ld commands from %s\n", applied, path);
    if (!kv_aof_open(&aof, path, policy)) {
        return 1;
    }
}
c/ch10/src/main.c · region startup
⚠️write is not fsync C

After write returns, the bytes are in the kernel, not on the disk. If the process is killed, the kernel still has them and writes them out. If the machine loses power, they are gone. fsync makes the kernel write them now and wait. The Linux manual adds that even then the directory entry for a new file may not have reached the disk. On macOS the manual says the drive "may not physically write the data to the platters for quite some time". A separate call, F_FULLFSYNC, asks it to. Most databases have a paragraph like this one in their source, and kv has the simple version.

Go: the same reader, backwards#

The Go log is a struct with a lock, because many goroutines record into it. The policy is parsed from the environment into one of three constants:

// Policy says how often the file is forced to disk. Redis has the same three settings.
type Policy int

const (
	Always   Policy = iota // after every command: slow and safe
	EverySec               // once a second: lose at most a second of writes
	No                     // never: the operating system decides
)

func ParsePolicy(s string) (Policy, error) {
	switch strings.ToLower(s) {
	case "", "everysec":
		return EverySec, nil
	case "always":
		return Always, nil
	case "no":
		return No, nil
	}
	return 0, fmt.Errorf("KV_FSYNC must be always, everysec or no, not %q", s)
}

// Log is the open file. Many goroutines record into it, so it has a lock of its own.
type Log struct {
	mu     sync.Mutex
	f      *os.File
	w      *bufio.Writer
	policy Policy
	dirty  bool
}
go/ch10/internal/aof/aof.go · region policy

Recording uses the WriteArrayLen and WriteBulk functions from the protocol package, which were written to answer clients and serve for log records too. The buffer is flushed after every record. The book's own test found the bug that this line fixes, and the gotcha below tells the story:

// Record appends one command. A relative expiry is rewritten as an absolute PEXPIREAT,
// because the file may be replayed much later. The bytes reach the kernel before this
// returns: a policy delays only the fsync, never the write, so a crash of the process
// loses nothing and only a crash of the machine can lose the last second.
func (l *Log) Record(args [][]byte, now time.Time) error {
	l.mu.Lock()
	defer l.mu.Unlock()
	for _, cmd := range rewrite(args, now) {
		if err := resp.WriteArrayLen(l.w, len(cmd)); err != nil {
			return err
		}
		for _, a := range cmd {
			if err := resp.WriteBulk(l.w, a); err != nil {
				return err
			}
		}
	}
	if err := l.w.Flush(); err != nil {
		return err
	}
	l.dirty = true
	if l.policy == Always {
		return l.syncLocked()
	}
	return nil
}

// rewrite turns one command into the commands the file stores.
func rewrite(args [][]byte, now time.Time) [][][]byte {
	name := strings.ToUpper(string(args[0]))
	switch {
	case name == "SET" && len(args) == 5:
		n, err := strconv.ParseInt(string(args[4]), 10, 64)
		if err != nil || n <= 0 {
			break
		}
		d := time.Duration(n) * time.Millisecond
		if strings.ToUpper(string(args[3])) == "EX" {
			d = time.Duration(n) * time.Second
		}
		return [][][]byte{args[:3], pexpireat(args[1], now.Add(d))}
	case name == "EXPIRE" && len(args) == 3:
		n, err := strconv.ParseInt(string(args[2]), 10, 64)
		if err != nil {
			break
		}
		if n <= 0 {
			return [][][]byte{{[]byte("DEL"), args[1]}}
		}
		return [][][]byte{pexpireat(args[1], now.Add(time.Duration(n)*time.Second))}
	}
	return [][][]byte{args}
}

func pexpireat(key []byte, at time.Time) [][]byte {
	return [][]byte{[]byte("PEXPIREAT"), key, []byte(strconv.FormatInt(at.UnixMilli(), 10))}
}
go/ch10/internal/aof/aof.go · region record
Exercise 10.2GoReplay with the client's parser

Write Replay. Open the file, and return 0 and no error when it does not exist. Wrap it in a bufio.Reader and call resp.ReadCommand in a loop, handing each command to apply. Stop at io.EOF. Treat io.ErrUnexpectedEOF as a truncated last command, print a line about it, and return what you have.

func Replay(path string, apply func(args [][]byte)) (int, error) {
	f, err := os.Open(path)
	// your code here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
// Replay reads the file with the same parser a client goes through and hands each
// command to apply. A truncated last command is dropped, as Redis drops it. A missing
// file is an empty store, not an error.
func Replay(path string, apply func(args [][]byte)) (int, error) {
	f, err := os.Open(path)
	if errors.Is(err, os.ErrNotExist) {
		return 0, nil
	}
	if err != nil {
		return 0, err
	}
	defer f.Close()
	r := bufio.NewReaderSize(f, 1<<16)
	n := 0
	for {
		args, err := resp.ReadCommand(r)
		if err == io.EOF {
			return n, nil
		}
		if err == io.ErrUnexpectedEOF {
			fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, "aof: dropped a truncated command at the end")
			return n, nil
		}
		if err != nil {
			return n, err
		}
		apply(args)
		n++
	}
}
go/ch10/internal/aof/aof.go · region replay

The command function takes the log as a parameter, and records a write before running it. During replay the log is nil, so nothing is recorded twice. The replies during replay go to a writer over io.Discard. Because the log and the store have different locks, two goroutines can record in one order and run in the other. A replay can then end with a different value for a key that both wrote. The Rust version below closes that gap with one lock:

// Execute runs one command against the store and writes its reply. A write command is
// also recorded in the log, when there is one. During replay the log is nil.
func Execute(args [][]byte, s *store.Store, log *aof.Log, w *bufio.Writer) error {
	now := time.Now()
	if len(args) == 0 {
		return resp.WriteError(w, "ERR empty command")
	}
	name := strings.ToUpper(string(args[0]))
	if log != nil && aof.IsWrite(name) {
		if err := log.Record(args, now); err != nil {
			return err
		}
	}
	return run(name, args, s, w, now)
}
go/ch10/internal/cmd/cmd.go · region execute
// KV_AOF names the file. Replay it first, with no log, then open it for appending.
var log *aof.Log
if path := os.Getenv("KV_AOF"); path != "" {
	policy, err := aof.ParsePolicy(os.Getenv("KV_FSYNC"))
	if err != nil {
		fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, err)
		os.Exit(1)
	}
	sink := bufio.NewWriter(io.Discard)
	n, err := aof.Replay(path, func(args [][]byte) { cmd.Execute(args, s, nil, sink) })
	if err != nil {
		fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, "aof:", err)
		os.Exit(1)
	}
	fmt.Fprintf(os.Stderr, "kv replayed %d commands from %s\n", n, path)
	if log, err = aof.Open(path, policy); err != nil {
		fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, err)
		os.Exit(1)
	}
	go func() {
		for range time.Tick(time.Second) {
			if err := log.Sync(); err != nil {
				fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, "aof:", err)
			}
		}
	}()
}
go/ch10/cmd/kv/main.go · region startup
⚠️A buffered writer and a kill Go

The first Go version lost every write on restart, and the test caught it. The log used a bufio.Writer, as every Go file writer does, and flushed it once a second from the ticker. The test stops the server with a signal, which runs no Go code. So the bytes still in the buffer never reached the kernel. The C version had no such bug, because it has no buffer between the record and write. The fix is the Flush after every record. A policy decides when to fsync. It must never decide when to write.

Rust: the store and the log under one lock#

The Rust log is a struct around a BufWriter<File>, and it has no lock of its own. Instead the store and the log live in one struct, Db, and the one Mutex from chapter 8 now guards both. A command and its record happen together, and no other command can come between them:

/// Everything a command may touch. One Mutex around this struct means a command and its
/// record in the log happen together, with no other command in between.
pub struct Db {
    pub store: Store,
    pub aof: Option<Aof>,
}
rust/ch10/src/db.rs · region struct
Exercise 10.3RustRecord, then run

Write Db::execute. If there is a log and the command is a write, record it with the current wall-clock time, then run it with cmd::execute. The order matters. cmd::execute takes the arguments by value and moves the key and the value into the store, so they must be recorded first. Use a let chain, which the 2024 edition allows, one if with two conditions joined by &&.

pub fn execute(&mut self, args: Command, w: &mut impl Write) -> io::Result<()> {
    // your code here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
impl Db {
    /// Record a write command, then run it. Recording comes first because `execute`
    /// moves the key and the value out of the arguments.
    pub fn execute(&mut self, args: Command, w: &mut impl Write) -> io::Result<()> {
        if let Some(log) = self.aof.as_mut()
            && args.first().is_some_and(|name| aof::is_write(name))
        {
            log.record(&args, aof::wall_ms())?;
        }
        crate::cmd::execute(args, &mut self.store, w)
    }
}
rust/ch10/src/db.rs · region execute

Recording and syncing mirror the C version. The flush after each record is the same fix as in Go. The first Rust version had the same bug, and the same test found it:
