/// Append one command. A relative expiry is rewritten as an absolute PEXPIREAT,
/// because the file may be replayed much later. The bytes reach the kernel before this
/// returns: a policy delays only the fsync, never the write, so a crash of the process
/// loses nothing and only a crash of the machine can lose the last second.
pub fn record(&mut self, args: &[Vec<u8>], now_ms: i64) -> io::Result<()> {
    for cmd in rewrite(args, now_ms) {
        resp::write_array_len(&mut self.file, cmd.len())?;
        for a in &cmd {
            resp::write_bulk(&mut self.file, a)?;
        }
    }
    self.file.flush()?;
    self.dirty = true;
    if self.policy == Policy::Always {
        self.sync()?;
    }
    Ok(())
}

/// Flush the buffer and force the file to disk, when there is something to force.
pub fn sync(&mut self) -> io::Result<()> {
    self.file.flush()?;
    if self.dirty && self.policy != Policy::No {
        self.file.get_ref().sync_all()?;
        self.dirty = false;
    }
    Ok(())
}
rust/ch10/src/aof.rs · region record

Replay opens the file in a BufReader, which implements BufRead, which is what read_command asks for. The parser does not know whether it is reading a socket or a file, and the replies go into io::sink():

/// Replay the file into the store, through the same parser a client goes through. A
/// truncated last command is dropped, as Redis drops it. A missing file is an empty store.
pub fn replay(path: &Path, store: &mut Store) -> io::Result<usize> {
    let file = match File::open(path) {
        Ok(f) => f,
        Err(e) if e.kind() == io::ErrorKind::NotFound => return Ok(0),
        Err(e) => return Err(e),
    };
    let mut reader = BufReader::with_capacity(1 << 16, file);
    let mut sink = io::sink();
    let mut n = 0;
    loop {
        match resp::read_command(&mut reader) {
            Ok(Some(args)) => {
                crate::cmd::execute(args, store, &mut sink)?;
                n += 1;
            }
            Ok(None) => return Ok(n),
            Err(e) if e.kind() == io::ErrorKind::UnexpectedEof => {
                eprintln!("aof: dropped a truncated command at the end");
                return Ok(n);
            }
            Err(e) => return Err(e),
        }
    }
}
rust/ch10/src/aof.rs · region replay

The everysec fsync runs from the sweeper thread, every tenth tick, and from the sweeper task in the tokio version. The lock is held for the sweep and the sync together, which is a few milliseconds a second:

// every tenth tick, once a second, the everysec policy forces the log to disk
if tick.is_multiple_of(10)
    && let Some(Err(e)) = db.aof.as_mut().map(Aof::sync)
{
    eprintln!("aof: {e}");
}
rust/ch10/src/main.rs · region sync
⚠️Drop does not run when the process is killed Rust

The log has a Drop that syncs the file, and a signal never calls it. Drop runs when a value goes out of scope in a program that is still running. A process ended by kill, by the test runner, or by a power cut runs no more Rust code. So the Drop is a courtesy for a clean exit. The flush after every record is what the durability of the log rests on, exactly as in Go. Rust's ownership rules say nothing about this. The operating system does.

What a crash takes#

Three kinds of failure, and three policies, make a small table that is worth learning. Every version of kv behaves the same way, because all three call the same kernel.

After a crash ofCGoRust
The process, with any policynothing lost, the kernel has every recordthe samethe same
The machine, policy alwaysnothing lostthe samethe same
The machine, policy everysecup to one second of writes lostthe samethe same
The machine, policy nowhatever the kernel had not written, usually secondsthe samethe same
A truncated last recorddropped at replaythe samethe same

The price of always is large, and it is why Redis defaults to everysec. On the author's machine, the C server took 20,000 SET commands from 50 clients. With everysec it answered about 39,900 a second. With always it answered about 6,700. The no policy was no faster than everysec, because one fsync a second costs almost nothing. The log for those 20,000 commands was 900,000 bytes, 45 bytes a command, which is the cost of a format a person can read. The chapter on benchmarking shows how to take such numbers yourself.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
Writing the fileOKStrongStrongC encodes into a buffer and calls write. Go and Rust reuse the protocol writers and flush.
ReplayStrongStrongStrongAll three reuse the parser from chapter 4 unchanged, which is the point of a log in RESP.
A crash of the processStrongOKOKC writes straight through. Go and Rust needed a flush the book's test had to find.
The clock on diskOKStrongOKC converts between two clocks itself, while Go's time.Time holds both and Rust converts with a helper.
Log and store togetherStrongOKStrongOne C thread needs no lock, Go takes two locks, and Rust takes one around both.
fsync on a timerStrongStrongOKC has the loop and Go a goroutine. Rust reuses the sweeper, which is a compromise.
Next · Quality and shipping11Testing and fuzzing14 min

Testing and fuzzing#

Quality and shipping · Chapter 11· 14 min read· 143 lines of code

Every chapter so far was checked by one script that talks to kv through redis-cli. This chapter adds the tests that live beside the code, in each language's own runner, and the tools that find what tests cannot. A fuzzer throws millions of inputs at the parser. A sanitizer or a race detector watches the program run. The chapter is about what each language can prove, and when.

The protocol tests in tests/protocol/ are the same for all three servers, and that is their value. They do not say which function is wrong, and they only send what a person thought to write. The parser from chapter 4 has five ways to fail on bad input, and the script tries one. A unit test can try all five, and a fuzzer can try five million things nobody thought of.

The toolsCGoRust
A unit testa main you write, with a CHECK macroa TestXxx function in a _test.go filea #[test] function in a mod tests
Running themmake test, a target you writego test ./...cargo test
A fuzzerlibFuzzer, with a clang that has itgo test -fuzz, built incargo-fuzz, on the nightly toolchain
Memory errorsAddressSanitizer, or Valgrind on Linuxnot possible in safe codenot possible in safe code
Data racesThreadSanitizer, in theorygo test -racethe compiler, at compile time
Undefined behaviourUndefinedBehaviorSanitizerdefined, except under a data raceMiri, for unsafe code
What the book runsthe tests under ASan and UBSango vet, go test -racecargo test, clippy -D warnings
📜Why C needs tools the other two do not

C has no test runner in its standard library, and no way to find a bad pointer at run time. Both gaps were filled from outside. Valgrind, released by Julian Seward in 2002, runs a program on a simulated processor and watches every memory access. The program runs twenty to thirty times slower. AddressSanitizer, from Google in 2012, puts the checks into the compiled code instead, at about twice the cost. clang and gcc both carry it. Go and Rust were designed after both tools existed and built the answers in. Go's runtime can watch for races and its test tool can fuzz. Rust's compiler refuses the programs that the sanitizer would have caught. The C tools remain the reason that C is still written at all.

C: a runner you write, and a sanitizer that watches#

A C test is a program. The runner for kv is twenty lines. A counter, and a macro that prints the file and the line of a failed check and carries on:

static int failed = 0;
static int passed = 0;

/* Report a failure with its location, and carry on to the next check. */
#define CHECK(cond)                                                                                \
    do {                                                                                           \
        if (cond) {                                                                                \
            passed++;                                                                              \
        } else {                                                                                   \
            failed++;                                                                              \
            fprintf(stderr, "%s:%d: check failed: %s\n", __FILE__, __LINE__, #cond);               \
        }                                                                                          \
    } while (0)
c/ch11/tests/test_resp.c · region harness

The tests for the parser cover the three answers from chapter 4, and the inline form. The one that matters most is the second. It cuts a valid request at every byte and checks that the parser says "incomplete" and never "bad". The event loop depends on exactly that:

Exercise 11.1CEvery prefix is incomplete

Write test_parse_incomplete_at_every_cut. Take a valid request, and for every length from 0 to one less than the whole, call kv_parse on that prefix. Each call must return KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE. A single KV_PARSE_BAD would make the server drop a client in the middle of a slow request.

static void test_parse_incomplete_at_every_cut(void) {
    const char *req = "*2\r\n$4\r\nECHO\r\n$2\r\nhi\r\n";
    /* your loop here */
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
static void test_parse_complete(void) {
    const char *req = "*2\r\n$4\r\nECHO\r\n$2\r\nhi\r\n";
    kv_cmd cmd;
    size_t used = 0;
    CHECK(kv_parse(req, strlen(req), &cmd, &used) == KV_PARSE_OK);
    CHECK(used == strlen(req));
    CHECK(cmd.argc == 2);
    CHECK(cmd.arglen[0] == 4 && memcmp(cmd.argv[0], "ECHO", 4) == 0);
    CHECK(cmd.arglen[1] == 2 && memcmp(cmd.argv[1], "hi", 2) == 0);
}

static void test_parse_incomplete_at_every_cut(void) {
    /* every prefix of a valid request is incomplete, never bad */
    const char *req = "*2\r\n$4\r\nECHO\r\n$2\r\nhi\r\n";
    for (size_t n = 0; n < strlen(req); n++) {
        kv_cmd cmd;
        size_t used = 0;
        CHECK(kv_parse(req, n, &cmd, &used) == KV_PARSE_INCOMPLETE);
    }
}

static void test_parse_bad(void) {
    const char *bad[] = {"*x\r\n", "*1\r\n#4\r\nECHO\r\n", "*1\r\n$4\r\nECHOXX",
                         "*1\r\n$99999999\r\n", "*100\r\n"};
    for (size_t i = 0; i < sizeof bad / sizeof bad[0]; i++) {
        kv_cmd cmd;
        size_t used = 0;
        CHECK(kv_parse(bad[i], strlen(bad[i]), &cmd, &used) == KV_PARSE_BAD);
    }
}

static void test_parse_inline(void) {
    const char *req = "  SET  a   b\r\n";
    kv_cmd cmd;
    size_t used = 0;
    CHECK(kv_parse(req, strlen(req), &cmd, &used) == KV_PARSE_OK);
    CHECK(cmd.argc == 3);
    CHECK(cmd.arglen[2] == 1 && cmd.argv[2][0] == 'b');
    CHECK(used == strlen(req));
}
c/ch11/tests/test_resp.c · region parse

The Makefile gains a test target. It compiles the test file with every source file except main.c, all under AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer, and runs the result. The sanitizers are the point. A test that passes under them has also proved that it read no freed memory and overflowed no integer on the way:

# The unit tests: every source under src/ except main.c, plus the test file, compiled
# together under the sanitizers. make test fails when a check fails.

test: build/test_resp
	./build/test_resp

build/test_resp: tests/test_resp.c $(SRC) | build
	$(CC) $(CFLAGS) -fsanitize=address,undefined -o $@ tests/test_resp.c $(filter-out src/main.c,$(SRC))
c/ch11/Makefile · region test
$ make test
53 checks passed, 0 failed

To see what the sanitizer does when there is something to find, the snapshot includes the bug from the chapter 4 gotcha, on purpose. It keeps a pointer into a buffer, grows the buffer, and reads through the pointer:

int main(void) {
    kv_buf b = {0};
    kv_buf_append(&b, "GET key\r\n", 9);
    const char *key = b.data + 4; /* points at "key", inside the buffer */
    char filler[2048];
    memset(filler, 'x', sizeof filler);
    kv_buf_append(&b, filler, sizeof filler); /* the buffer grows, and may move */
    printf("%.3s\n", key);                    /* reads through the old pointer */
    kv_buf_free(&b);
    return 0;
}
c/ch11/bugs/use_after_free.c · region bug
$ make bugs
==32154==ERROR: AddressSanitizer: heap-use-after-free on address 0x619000000584
READ of size 1 at 0x619000000584 thread T0
    #2 0x00010cec1930 in main use_after_free.c:16
0x619000000584 is located 4 bytes inside of 1024-byte region
freed by thread T0 here:
    #0 0x00010d3d9a47 in realloc+0x87
    #1 0x00010cec1aa1 in kv_buf_append buf.c:27
    #2 0x00010cec191e in main use_after_free.c:15

The report names the line that read the freed memory, the line that freed it, and the realloc inside kv_buf_append that did the freeing. Without the sanitizer the program prints key most of the time, because the old bytes are usually still there. That is the whole problem with undefined behaviour. On Linux, Valgrind gives the same report without recompiling, and also lists every block that was never freed. The book ran the test binary under it in a container:

$ valgrind --leak-check=full ./test_resp
53 checks passed, 0 failed
==215== All heap blocks were freed -- no leaks are possible
==215== ERROR SUMMARY: 0 errors from 0 contexts

The binary for Valgrind was built without the sanitizer flags, because the two tools cannot share a process.

A fuzzer is the last tool. The one that comes with clang is called libFuzzer. It calls one function of yours millions of times with inputs it mutates. It watches which branches each input reaches, so that it can mutate the interesting ones further. The target for kv feeds the input through the parser and the executor, as the server would:

int LLVMFuzzerTestOneInput(const uint8_t *data, size_t size) {
    kv_store store = {0};
    kv_buf out = {0};
    const char *p = (const char *)data;
    size_t len = size;
    /* run every complete command in the input, as the server would */
    for (;;) {
        kv_cmd cmd;
        size_t used = 0;
        if (kv_parse(p, len, &cmd, &used) != KV_PARSE_OK) {
            break;
        }
        kv_exec(&cmd, &store, &out);
        p += used;
        len -= used;
    }
    kv_buf_free(&out);
    kv_store_free(&store);
    return 0;
}
c/ch11/fuzz/fuzz_parse.c · region target
🖥Apple's clang has no libFuzzer

The clang that comes with Xcode cannot link a fuzzer. The error is library 'libclang_rt.fuzzer_osx.a' not found. The fuzz target of the Makefile runs in a Linux container instead, with the one-line docker run in its comment. The clang 19 in the container links it without complaint. The fuzzer ran there and found nothing in the kv parser. That is the usual result after the unit tests have done their work, and no reason to skip it.

✅Run the tests under the sanitizers, every time C

A C test suite without sanitizers checks the answers and nothing else. With -fsanitize=address,undefined it also checks every memory access and every arithmetic operation the tests perform. The cost is about twice the run time, which for a test suite is nothing. kv's make test always builds with them, and so should yours.

Go: the runner is in the box, and so is the fuzzer#

Go tests live in files that end in _test.go, in the same package as the code, and go test finds them by name. The parser test is a table, which is the Go shape for "the same check on many inputs". Each row is a subtest, so that a failure names the case:

func TestReadCommand(t *testing.T) {
	cases := []struct {
		name  string
		input string
		want  []string
		err   error
	}{
		{"array", "*2\r\n$4\r\nECHO\r\n$2\r\nhi\r\n", []string{"ECHO", "hi"}, nil},
		{"inline", "  SET  a   b\r\n", []string{"SET", "a", "b"}, nil},
		{"bare LF", "PING\n", []string{"PING"}, nil},
		{"empty value", "*3\r\n$3\r\nSET\r\n$1\r\nk\r\n$0\r\n\r\n", []string{"SET", "k", ""}, nil},
		{"closed", "", nil, io.EOF},
		{"cut short", "*2\r\n$4\r\nEC", nil, io.ErrUnexpectedEOF},
		{"not a number", "*x\r\n", nil, ErrProtocol},
		{"wrong marker", "*1\r\n#4\r\nECHO\r\n", nil, ErrProtocol},
		{"too many", "*100\r\n", nil, ErrProtocol},
	}
	for _, c := range cases {
		t.Run(c.name, func(t *testing.T) {
			got, err := ReadCommand(bufio.NewReader(strings.NewReader(c.input)))
			if err != c.err {
				t.Fatalf("err = %v, want %v", err, c.err)
			}
			if len(got) != len(c.want) {
				t.Fatalf("got %q, want %q", got, c.want)
			}
			for i := range got {
				if string(got[i]) != c.want[i] {
					t.Fatalf("arg %d = %q, want %q", i, got[i], c.want[i])
				}
			}
		})
	}
}
go/ch11/internal/resp/resp_test.go · region table
Exercise 11.2GoFuzz the parser

Write FuzzReadCommand. Add three seed inputs with f.Add. Then call f.Fuzz with a function that parses the input and returns on an error. When the parse succeeds, write the arguments back out with the protocol writers, parse them again, and fail the test if anything changed. That round trip is a property the parser must keep for every input, and it is the kind of check a fuzzer is good at.

func FuzzReadCommand(f *testing.F) {
	f.Add([]byte("*2\r\n$4\r\nECHO\r\n$2\r\nhi\r\n"))
	f.Fuzz(func(t *testing.T, data []byte) {
		// your code here
	})
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
// FuzzReadCommand lets the fuzzer mutate the input. Whatever it sends, the parser must
// not panic, and a request it accepts must survive a round trip through the writers.
func FuzzReadCommand(f *testing.F) {
	f.Add([]byte("*2\r\n$4\r\nECHO\r\n$2\r\nhi\r\n"))
	f.Add([]byte("PING\r\n"))
	f.Add([]byte("*1\r\n$3\r\nGET\r\n"))
	f.Fuzz(func(t *testing.T, data []byte) {
		args, err := ReadCommand(bufio.NewReader(bytes.NewReader(data)))
		if err != nil {
			return
		}
		var buf bytes.Buffer
		w := bufio.NewWriter(&buf)
		WriteArrayLen(w, len(args))
		for _, a := range args {
			WriteBulk(w, a)
		}
		w.Flush()
		again, err := ReadCommand(bufio.NewReader(&buf))
		if err != nil {
			t.Fatalf("round trip failed: %v", err)
		}
		if len(again) != len(args) {
			t.Fatalf("round trip changed the argument count")
		}
		for i := range args {
			if !bytes.Equal(again[i], args[i]) {
				t.Fatalf("round trip changed argument %d", i)
			}
		}
	})
}
go/ch11/internal/resp/resp_test.go · region fuzz
$ go test ./...                         # the seeds run as ordinary tests
ok  	kv/internal/resp	0.021s
$ go test -fuzz=FuzzReadCommand -fuzztime=10s ./internal/resp
fuzz: elapsed: 11s, execs: 581307 (50220/sec), new interesting: 34 (total: 37)
PASS

The fuzzer ran half a million inputs in ten seconds. Had one failed, Go would have written it to testdata/fuzz/FuzzReadCommand/. From then on go test would run it with the seeds, as a regression test, until the bug was fixed. The race detector from chapter 8 is the other built-in tool, and it is worth seeing what it prints. A scratch program that runs the chapter 5 INCR from two goroutines with no lock prints this under the detector:

$ go run -race .
WARNING: DATA RACE
Write at 0x00c000186000 by goroutine 7:
  runtime.mapassign_faststr()
  main.main.func1()
Previous read at 0x00c000186000 by goroutine 6:
  runtime.mapaccess2_faststr()
Goroutine 7 (running) created at:
  sync.(*WaitGroup).Go()
⚠️A detector is not a proof Go

The race detector and the fuzzer report what they saw, not what exists. A race on a path the test never ran is not reported. An input the fuzzer never generated is not tried. Both tools are excellent and both are statistical. That is why kv runs the race detector in make verify on every change and keeps the fuzz corpus. The number of things seen then keeps growing. The next section shows the other kind of tool.

Rust: the compiler already ran, and the tools check the rest#

Rust tests live in the file they test, in a module marked #[cfg(test)], and cargo test compiles that module and runs each #[test]. The parser tests use one trick worth knowing. A slice of bytes implements BufRead, so the parser that was written for a socket reads a string literal without any wrapper:

Exercise 11.3RustA request survives a round trip

Write the test a_request_survives_a_round_trip. Build a command with three arguments, one of which contains a line ending. Write it with write_array_len and write_bulk into a Vec<u8>. Parse the vector, and assert that the result equals the command. The line ending inside a value is the case that a length-prefixed format exists for.

#[test]
fn a_request_survives_a_round_trip() {
    let args = vec![b"SET".to_vec(), b"k".to_vec(), b"a value\r\nwith a line".to_vec()];
    // your code here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
#[cfg(test)]
mod tests {
    use super::*;

    /// A slice of bytes is a BufRead, so a test needs no socket.
    fn parse(input: &[u8]) -> io::Result<Option<Command>> {
        let mut r = input;
        read_command(&mut r)
    }

    #[test]
    fn an_array_of_bulk_strings() {
        let got = parse(b"*2\r\n$4\r\nECHO\r\n$2\r\nhi\r\n").unwrap().unwrap();
        assert_eq!(got, vec![b"ECHO".to_vec(), b"hi".to_vec()]);
    }

    #[test]
    fn an_inline_line_and_a_bare_lf() {
        let got = parse(b"  SET  a   b\n").unwrap().unwrap();
        assert_eq!(got.len(), 3);
        assert_eq!(got[2], b"b");
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_closed_connection_is_none() {
        assert!(parse(b"").unwrap().is_none());
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_cut_request_is_unexpected_eof() {
        let err = parse(b"*2\r\n$4\r\nEC").unwrap_err();
        assert_eq!(err.kind(), io::ErrorKind::UnexpectedEof);
    }

    #[test]
    fn bad_bytes_are_invalid_data() {
        for bad in [&b"*x\r\n"[..], b"*1\r\n#4\r\nECHO\r\n", b"*100\r\n"] {
            assert_eq!(parse(bad).unwrap_err().kind(), io::ErrorKind::InvalidData);
        }
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_request_survives_a_round_trip() {
        let args = vec![
            b"SET".to_vec(),
            b"k".to_vec(),
            b"a value\r\nwith a line".to_vec(),
        ];
        let mut bytes = Vec::new();
        write_array_len(&mut bytes, args.len()).unwrap();
        for a in &args {
            write_bulk(&mut bytes, a).unwrap();
        }
        assert_eq!(parse(&bytes).unwrap().unwrap(), args);
    }
}
rust/ch11/src/resp.rs · region test

Fuzzing in Rust is cargo-fuzz, which the fuzzing book calls "the recommended tool for fuzz testing Rust code". It drives the same libFuzzer that C used, through a crate called libfuzzer-sys, and it needs the nightly toolchain. The targets live in a small crate of their own under fuzz/, and the target for kv is the C one in Rust:

fuzz_target!(|data: &[u8]| {
    let mut input = data;
    let mut store = kv::store::Store::new();
    let mut out = Vec::new();
    // run every complete command in the input, as the server would
    while let Ok(Some(args)) = kv::resp::read_command(&mut input) {
        let _ = kv::cmd::execute(args, &mut store, &mut out);
    }
});
rust/ch11/fuzz/fuzz_targets/parse.rs · region target
$ cargo install cargo-fuzz
$ cargo +nightly fuzz run parse -- -max_total_time=20
#360989	DONE   cov: 318 ft: 1597 corp: 501/34Kb exec/s: 17189
Done 360989 runs in 21 second(s)

The last tool is Miri, an interpreter that runs a Rust program slowly and checks every memory access against the rules. Its README calls it "an Undefined Behavior detection tool for Rust", and it finds the same things AddressSanitizer finds in C. The server kv has no unsafe code, so Miri has nothing to find until chapter 14. It still runs:

$ rustup +nightly component add miri
$ cargo +nightly miri test --lib
test result: ok. 8 passed; 0 failed; finished in 91.77s

Ninety seconds for tests that take a hundredth of a second natively. That is the cost of checking rules at run time that the compiler could not check at compile time. Safe Rust avoids that cost by having the compiler check them first.

⚠️Two toolchains, and a crate inside a crate Rust

cargo-fuzz and Miri both need nightly, and the fuzz crate must stay out of the main one. The nightly toolchain is a second compiler installed beside the stable one, chosen per command with +nightly. The fuzz/ directory is a separate package with an empty [workspace] table. The main Cargo.toml has one too. So a cargo command in one directory never reads the files of the other. Forget the one in fuzz/ and the first error names a workspace you did not know you had.

Fuzzing, three ways, one engine#

All three fuzzers in this chapter are coverage-guided. They watch which branches an input reaches and mutate the inputs that reach new ones. That is why a fuzzer finds the corner cases of a parser within seconds. Two of the three fuzzers are the same engine. The engine called libFuzzer is what clang links into the C target and what libfuzzer-sys links into the Rust one. Go wrote its own, with the same design, and built it into go test. The rates on the author's machine were 17,000 inputs a second for Rust and 50,000 for Go. The C rate was 300, in a container, where Docker on a Mac pays for every system call. None found a bug in kv's parser. The unit tests had already covered the cases a fuzzer finds first, which is the order the book recommends.

What finds what#

The bugCGoRust
Reading freed memoryASan at run time, if a test reaches itcannot be writtencannot be written
A data raceThreadSanitizer, rarely used-race at run time, if a test reaches itthe compiler, always
Integer overflowUBSan at run timewraps, by the language definitionpanics in a debug build, wraps in release
A panic or crash on bad inputa sanitizer, or a core dumpthe fuzzer, in go testthe fuzzer, on nightly
A wrong answeryour testsyour testsyour tests

The table is the chapter in four lines. C finds its bugs with tools that watch the program run, which means a test must reach the bug. Go has two such tools built in, and a language definition that removes the undefined cases. Rust removes two whole rows at compile time, and uses the same run-time tools as C for what remains. The last row is the same for all three, and it is where most bugs live.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
A test runnerWeakStrongStrongC has none, so you write one. Go and Rust have one in the box, with no setup.
FuzzingOKStrongOKlibFuzzer is one flag on Linux and a container on a Mac, Go has it built in, and Rust needs nightly and a second crate.
Memory errorsOKStrongStrongC finds them at run time with ASan or Valgrind. Go and Rust cannot write them in safe code.
Data racesWeakOKStrongC has a sanitizer that is rarely run and Go finds the races a test provokes, while Rust refuses them at compile time.
Undefined behaviourWeakStrongOKC has it everywhere and UBSan finds some. Go has almost none, a data race being the exception, and Rust confines it to unsafe, where Miri checks it.
Speed of the toolsStrongStrongWeakASan costs two times and the race detector two to twenty, while Miri costs nine thousand.
Next · Quality and shipping12Benchmarking and profiling15 min

Benchmarking and profiling#

Quality and shipping · Chapter 12· 15 min read· 130 lines of code

The book has quoted numbers since chapter 6. This chapter shows how to take one that means something. It also shows how to find where the time goes when the number is worse than you expected. It benchmarks and profiles all four servers, and follows one bad result in Rust from a profile to a fix. Measurement is the only argument that ends a discussion about speed.

Chapter 8 reported that the four servers answer about 70,000 to 100,000 requests a second from fifty clients. That number is true and nearly useless, because it measures a round trip on the loopback interface more than it measures kv. The Redis documentation says so in its own words. When the client and the server share a machine, "the CPU is the limiting factor with redis-benchmark". By default the tool "does not use pipelining or any parallelism at all". A benchmark is a question, and a question has to be chosen.

MeasuringCGoRust
The load generatorredis-benchmarkthe samethe same
A CPU profilesample on macOS, perf on Linuxruntime/pprof, then go tool pprofsample or perf, with symbols kept in the release build
What the profile showed90 percent in read, write and poll56 percent in system calls, the rest in the schedulerfifty threads waiting, and one lock
The surprisenonenonea wall at 200,000 requests a second with pipelining
The fixnone needednone neededa read lock, after four experiments that failed

Taking a number#

Every number in this chapter comes from one command shape. Fifty connections, four client threads so that the client is not the bottleneck, two hundred thousand requests, and two settings of the pipeline. A pipeline of 16 means each connection sends sixteen commands before it waits for a reply. The documentation calls pipelining "a feature often exploited by real world applications". It changes what is measured. Without it, the number is mostly the cost of a round trip. With it, the number is mostly the cost of the server:

$ redis-benchmark -p 6380 -c 50 -n 200000 --threads 4 -t set,get -q          # one at a time
$ redis-benchmark -p 6380 -c 50 -n 200000 --threads 4 -t set,get -q -P 16    # sixteen at a time
                         no pipeline              pipeline of 16
                         SET       GET            SET       GET
C, poll, one thread      88,700    79,900         796,800   800,000
Go, goroutines           88,700    88,700         800,000   800,000
Rust, threads            99,950    88,700         159,700   199,400
Rust, tokio              99,950    99,850         160,000   199,800

The left half says the four servers are the same, within noise, because the left half measures the loopback. The right half says something is wrong with the two Rust servers. Both stop at a fifth or a quarter of what C and Go reach. The rest of the chapter is about that column, and the tools that explain it. First, the shape of a single measurement. The full output of the benchmark, without -q, gives a distribution and not one number:

Summary:
  throughput summary: 79617.83 requests per second
  latency summary (msec):
          avg       min       p50       p95       p99       max
        0.540     0.064     0.487     0.919     1.319     5.103

The median request took half a millisecond. One in a hundred took more than 1.3 milliseconds, and the slowest took five. A server is judged by the p99 column more often than by the average, because the average hides the requests that a user notices.

📜Flame graphs, and what a profile is

A profiler interrupts a program many times a second and writes down what it was doing. The result is a list of stacks with a count against each, and the count says where the time went. In 2011 Brendan Gregg drew those stacks as a picture he called a flame graph. He describes it as "a visualization of hierarchical data that I created to visualize stack traces of profiled software". Each box is a function, and its width is the share of samples that passed through it. The boxes above it are the functions it called. The x axis "is not the passage of time". Every profiler in this chapter produces the same kind of data, and every one of them can draw that picture. The chapter reads the lists instead, because a list fits in a book.

C: a sampler and a short answer#

macOS ships a sampling profiler called sample. Given a process and a number of seconds, it writes every stack it saw. It adds a summary sorted by the function at the top of the stack. The C server was sampled for five seconds while the benchmark ran:

$ sample $(pgrep kv) 5 -file kv.sample
$ grep -A12 "Sort by top of stack" kv.sample
        read   (in libsystem_kernel.dylib)        1741
        write  (in libsystem_kernel.dylib)        1561
        poll   (in libsystem_kernel.dylib)         427
        _platform_memmove                           67
        kv_server_run  (in kv)                      31
        kv_parse  (in kv)                           17
        strncasecmp_l                               16
        kv_exec  (in kv)                            13

Three system calls hold ninety percent of the samples. The parser and the command code together hold one percent. The C server is not spending its time in C. It is spending it in the kernel, moving bytes in and out of sockets, and no change to kv_parse would be visible in the benchmark. That is the most common result of profiling a server, and the most useful, because it stops you from optimising the wrong thing. On Linux the same question is asked with perf record, which the manual describes as "Run a command and record its profile into perf.data". It is answered with perf report.

✅Measure before you optimise, and measure after C

A profile is cheap and a guess is expensive. The sampler above took five seconds to run and ten seconds to read, and it closed a question that could have cost a day. The second half of the rule matters as much. Every change made for speed is measured again with the same command. A change that does not move the number is reverted, however clever it was.

A server profile answers "where does the time go". A micro-benchmark answers "how long does this function take", which is a different question with a different tool. Go has one built in, and Rust has one on the nightly toolchain. C has a clock, which is enough:

Exercise 12.1CTime the parser

Write a benchmark for kv_parse. Build one SET request as a string and read the monotonic clock, with kv_now_ms from chapter 9. Parse the request ten million times in a loop. Read the clock again, and print the nanoseconds for one parse. Count the successful parses and print the count too, so that the compiler cannot remove the loop as unused work. The Makefile target bench builds it with the release flags and no sanitizer.

int main(void) {
    const char *req = "*3\r\n$3\r\nSET\r\n$16\r\nkey:000000001234\r\n$3\r\nxxx\r\n";
    /* your code here */
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
int main(void) {
    const char *req = "*3\r\n$3\r\nSET\r\n$16\r\nkey:000000001234\r\n$3\r\nxxx\r\n";
    size_t len = strlen(req);
    long runs = 10 * 1000 * 1000;
    kv_cmd cmd;
    size_t used = 0;
    long ok = 0;
    int64_t start = kv_now_ms();
    for (long i = 0; i < runs; i++) {
        ok += kv_parse(req, len, &cmd, &used) == KV_PARSE_OK;
    }
    int64_t elapsed = kv_now_ms() - start;
    /* ok is printed so that the compiler cannot remove the loop as unused work */
    printf("%ld parses in %lld ms: %.1f ns each (%ld ok)\n", runs, (long long)elapsed,
           elapsed * 1e6 / (double)runs, ok);
    return 0;
}
c/ch12/bench/bench_parse.c · region bench

The same request was timed in the other two languages. The Go benchmark reuses its reader. The Rust example binary has the same loop as the C one:

// BenchmarkReadCommand times the parser on one SET request. go test -bench runs the loop
// for as long as it needs to get a stable number. The reader is reused, so that the
// number is the parser and not the cost of a 4 KB buffer per request.
func BenchmarkReadCommand(b *testing.B) {
	req := []byte("*3\r\n$3\r\nSET\r\n$16\r\nkey:000000001234\r\n$3\r\nxxx\r\n")
	r := bufio.NewReader(nil)
	for b.Loop() {
		r.Reset(bytes.NewReader(req))
		if _, err := ReadCommand(r); err != nil {
			b.Fatal(err)
		}
	}
}
go/ch12/internal/resp/bench_test.go · region bench
C, kv_parse                     130 ns     0 allocations
Go, ReadCommand                 543 ns     9 allocations, 184 bytes
Rust, read_command              775 ns     one Vec per line and per bulk string

The C parser is four to six times faster, and the reason is chapter 4. It copies nothing. The arguments are pointers into the buffer, while the Go and Rust parsers allocate a slice or a Vec for every argument and every line. That is a real cost. The server profiles above say it does not matter here. The parser is one percent of a server that spends ninety percent of its time in the kernel. Both facts are true, and a micro-benchmark alone would have led you to optimise the wrong one.

Go: the profiler is a package#

Go's profiler is in the standard library. The package runtime/pprof "writes runtime profiling data in the format expected by the pprof visualization tool", and starting it is one call. The chapter 12 server starts a CPU profile when an environment variable names a file, and stops it when the server is told to stop:

Exercise 12.2GoProfile on request

At the start of main, if KV_CPUPROFILE names a file, create the file and call pprof.StartCPUProfile. Then arrange for a clean stop. Use signal.Notify to receive an interrupt or a SIGTERM on a channel. Start a goroutine that waits on the channel, stops the profile, closes the file and exits. Without the stop, the profile file stays empty.

if path := os.Getenv("KV_CPUPROFILE"); path != "" {
	// your code here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
// KV_CPUPROFILE names a file. The profile runs until the server is told to stop, with
// Ctrl-C or kill, so that a benchmark can be profiled from start to end.
if path := os.Getenv("KV_CPUPROFILE"); path != "" {
	f, err := os.Create(path)
	if err != nil {
		fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, err)
		os.Exit(1)
	}
	if err := pprof.StartCPUProfile(f); err != nil {
		fmt.Fprintln(os.Stderr, err)
		os.Exit(1)
	}
	stop := make(chan os.Signal, 1)
	signal.Notify(stop, os.Interrupt, syscall.SIGTERM)
	go func() {
		<-stop
		pprof.StopCPUProfile()
		f.Close()
		os.Exit(0)
	}()
}
go/ch12/cmd/kv/main.go · region profile
$ KV_CPUPROFILE=cpu.prof ./kv &
$ redis-benchmark -p 6380 -c 50 -n 300000 --threads 4 -t get -q
$ kill -INT %1
$ go tool pprof -top kv cpu.prof
Duration: 4.40s, Total samples = 11.61s (263.58%)
      flat  flat%   sum%        cum   cum%
     6.51s 56.07% 56.07%      6.51s 56.07%  syscall.rawsyscalln
     1.89s 16.28% 72.35%      1.89s 16.28%  runtime.pthread_cond_wait
     1.20s 10.34% 82.69%      1.20s 10.34%  runtime.kevent
     0.82s  7.06% 89.75%      0.82s  7.06%  runtime.pthread_cond_signal
     0.01s 0.086% 97.59%      3.66s 31.52%  internal/poll.(*FD).Read
     0.01s 0.086% 97.67%      2.88s 24.81%  internal/poll.(*FD).Write

The total is 263 percent, because the profile adds up the time of every thread, and the server used almost three cores. More than half of that time was in system calls. A tenth was in kevent, which is the Go runtime asking the kernel which sockets are ready. Most of the rest was in the scheduler, parking and waking threads. The cum column says a third of all time was spent somewhere under Read. Again, the code that kv wrote is not in the list. Go has a second tool for that, go test -bench, which chapter 6 used to time the hash table. That is the right tool when the question is about a function rather than a server. The C section above timed the parser in all three languages with that kind of tool.

Rust: a profile, four experiments, and a lock#

The Rust servers stop at 200,000 requests a second with pipelining, and the first question is where the time goes. The release build keeps line information when Cargo.toml says so, which costs nothing at run time and lets a profiler name inlined functions:

# Keep function names in the release build, so a profiler can show them. The code is
# as fast as without this; only the binary is larger.
[profile.release]
debug = 1
rust/ch12/Cargo.toml · region profile

The sampler then names Rust functions, and its summary for the thread server under load is this:

__recvfrom          (in libsystem_kernel.dylib)    67711
__accept            (in libsystem_kernel.dylib)     1411
__semwait_signal    (in libsystem_kernel.dylib)     1409
__sendto            (in libsystem_kernel.dylib)     1252
__psynch_mutexwait  (in libsystem_kernel.dylib)      689
core::slice::memchr::memchr_aligned  (in kv)         256
kv::cmd::execute    (in kv)                           25
kv::resp::read_line (in kv)                           24
⚠️A wall-clock sampler counts sleeping threads Rust

Sixty-seven thousand samples in recvfrom are not CPU time. The sampler records every thread on every tick, and the thread server has fifty threads that spend most of their lives blocked in a read. The C server has one thread, so its list was honest. The Rust list is dominated by waiting. The lines that describe work are lower down. The one that matters is __psynch_mutexwait, which is a thread waiting for a mutex that another thread holds. On Linux, perf samples CPU time by default and does not have this problem.

One line of a profile is a hypothesis, not an answer. The author tested it with experiments, each a few minutes, and the table is the record:

experiment                                      SET        GET      conclusion
one client, pipeline 16                                  201,000   the client limits one connection
50 clients, GET only / SET only                 200,000    253,000   the count of clients changes nothing
parking_lot's user-space mutex                  200,000    199,800   not the kind of mutex
TCP_NODELAY on the socket                       159,700    199,200   not Nagle's algorithm
one socket handle instead of try_clone          199,600    199,400   not the clone
each thread with a private store, no lock       800,000    796,800   the lock

The last line is not a server anyone would ship, because the threads no longer share data, but it answers the question. Without the shared lock the Rust server is as fast as the C one. With it, every command from every thread passes through one mutex. A contended mutex on macOS is a pthread mutex, which passes the lock from one thread to the next through the kernel. The Rust standard library uses a futex on Linux and the pthread mutex on other Unix systems. The same server in a Linux container reached 398,000. Go has the same lock around its store and reaches 800,000. The comment above the Go mutex explains why. A waiting goroutine is parked on a semaphore of the runtime. In what the comment calls "starvation mode", ownership passes "from the unlocking goroutine to the waiter at the front of the queue". The kernel is not involved.

The first fix was to shrink what the lock covers. The command code now returns the reply as a value, and the server writes it after the lock is released:

/// A reply as a value, so that a command can be run under a lock and written after
/// the lock is released. Formatting and the write to the socket cost more than the
/// store operation itself, and neither needs the store.
pub enum Reply {
    Simple(&'static str),
    Error(String),
    Bulk(Vec<u8>),
    Int(i64),
    Nil,
    Array(Vec<Vec<u8>>),
}

pub fn write_reply(w: &mut impl Write, reply: &Reply) -> io::Result<()> {
    match reply {
        Reply::Simple(s) => write_simple(w, s),
        Reply::Error(s) => write_error(w, s),
        Reply::Bulk(b) => write_bulk(w, b),
        Reply::Int(n) => write_int(w, *n),
        Reply::Nil => write_nil(w),
        Reply::Array(items) => {
            write_array_len(w, items.len())?;
            items.iter().try_for_each(|b| write_bulk(w, b))
        }
    }
}
rust/ch12/src/resp.rs · region reply

It made no difference. Both servers stayed at 200,000, because the cost was the handoff and not the hold. The change stays, because formatting under a lock is a habit worth losing, and because the second fix needed it. The second fix is a reader-writer lock. Reads of the store take a shared lock that any number of threads may hold together. Writes take the exclusive one:

Exercise 12.3RustThe read-only commands

Write execute_read. It takes the arguments by reference and the store by shared reference. It returns Some(reply) for the eight commands that only read the store, and None for anything else. The eight run from PING to COMMAND. execute calls it first, so that the arms exist once.

pub fn execute_read(args: &Command, store: &Store) -> Option<Reply> {
    let now = Instant::now();
    let name = args.first()?.to_ascii_uppercase();
    let reply = match (name.as_slice(), args.as_slice()) {
        // your arms here
        _ => return None,
    };
    Some(reply)
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
/// The commands that only read the store. They take `&Store`, so many threads can run
/// them at once under a read lock. `None` means the command is not one of them.
pub fn execute_read(args: &Command, store: &Store) -> Option<Reply> {
    let now = Instant::now();
    let name = args.first()?.to_ascii_uppercase();
    let reply = match (name.as_slice(), args.as_slice()) {
        (b"PING", [_]) => Reply::Simple("PONG"),
        (b"PING", [_, msg]) => Reply::Bulk(msg.clone()),
        (b"PING", _) => write_arity("ping"),
        (b"ECHO", [_, msg]) => Reply::Bulk(msg.clone()),
        (b"ECHO", _) => write_arity("echo"),
        (b"GET", [_, key]) => match store.get(key, now) {
            Some(val) => Reply::Bulk(val.to_vec()),
            None => Reply::Nil,
        },
        (b"GET", _) => write_arity("get"),
        (b"EXISTS", [_, keys @ ..]) if !keys.is_empty() => {
            let n = keys.iter().filter(|k| store.get(k, now).is_some()).count();
            Reply::Int(n as i64)
        }
        (b"EXISTS", _) => write_arity("exists"),
        (b"TTL", [_, key]) => Reply::Int(match store.ttl(key, now) {
            Ttl::Missing => -2,
            Ttl::Never => -1,
            // the nearest second, as Redis reports it
            Ttl::Left(left) => ((left.as_millis() + 500) / 1000) as i64,
        }),
        (b"TTL", _) => write_arity("ttl"),
        (b"DBSIZE", _) => Reply::Int(store.len() as i64),
        (b"KEYS", [_, pattern]) => Reply::Array(
            store
                .keys(pattern, now)
                .into_iter()
                .map(<[u8]>::to_vec)
                .collect(),
        ),
        (b"KEYS", _) => write_arity("keys"),
        (b"COMMAND", _) => Reply::Array(Vec::new()),
        _ => return None,
    };
    Some(reply)
}
rust/ch12/src/cmd.rs · region execute-read

The serving loop tries the shared lock first and takes the exclusive one only for a write:

// A read takes the shared lock, which many threads hold at once. A write
// takes the exclusive one. Either way the lock is released before the
// reply is formatted or written.
let reply = {
    let db = store.read().unwrap_or_else(|e| e.into_inner());
    db.execute_read(&args)
};
let reply = match reply {
    Some(reply) => reply,
    None => {
        let mut db = store.write().unwrap_or_else(|e| e.into_inner());
        db.execute(args)?
    }
};
resp::write_reply(&mut writer, &reply)?;
rust/ch12/src/main.rs · region unlock-first
                         no pipeline              pipeline of 16
                         SET       GET            SET       GET
Rust, threads, before    99,950    88,700         159,700   199,400
Rust, threads, after     99,800    99,850         264,900   796,800
Rust, tokio, after       99,850    99,255         199,600   800,000

Reads are fixed. GET with pipelining went from 200,000 to 800,000, the C and Go figure, because fifty readers no longer queue. Writes are not fixed, because fifty writers still do, and 265,000 is where the pthread handoff leaves them. The next step would be to split the store into shards with a lock each, so that writers to different keys never meet. That is what every fast Rust store does, and what kv does not need. The chapter stops here, with a number that is honest about what was fixed and what was not.

The method#

The Rust section is the method of the whole chapter. A benchmark gave a number that looked wrong. A profile gave one line to suspect. Each experiment changed one thing and measured again with the same command. The first fix was kept and measured, found to do nothing, and said so. The second fix was measured, found to fix half the problem, and said so. Nothing in that sequence needed Rust. The same steps find the same kind of answer in C and in Go, with their own profilers. The "golden rule" of the benchmark documentation applies to all of it. "Only compare apples and apples."

🖥Profilers by platform

On macOS, sample needs nothing installed, and Instruments, started from the command line with xctrace record, gives a time profile with a graphical viewer. Both are macOS tools and do not run on Linux. There, perf is the tool for C and Rust, and its output feeds the flame graph scripts directly. Go's pprof works everywhere Go does, because the profiler is inside the runtime and not in the operating system. On Windows, Visual Studio has a CPU profiler for native code, and pprof again covers Go.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
A profiler in the boxOKStrongOKC and Rust use the operating system's profiler. Go carries its own, and it works the same everywhere.
Reading the profileStrongOKWeakOne C thread gives an honest list and Go needs the cum column, while the fifty Rust threads drown the list in waiting.
A fast serverStrongStrongOKC and Go reach 800,000 with pipelining. Rust needs a read lock to match on reads and stays behind on writes.
Why it was slowOKStrongWeakThe Go runtime hides lock handoff from the kernel. Rust's standard mutex is the platform's, and macOS makes it expensive.
The fixn/an/aOKRust's type system made the read lock a small, safe change, but the platform decides what it buys.
Honest numbersStrongStrongStrongredis-benchmark and the same command shape treat all three alike.
Next · Quality and shipping13Shipping11 min

Shipping#

Quality and shipping · Chapter 13· 11 min read· 40 lines of code

kv works on the machine it was written on. This chapter builds it for other machines. From a Mac, it produces servers for Linux on two kinds of processor and for Windows, and checks the Linux ones in containers. The chapter covers linking, the C library, cross-compilation, the differences between the three operating systems, and what a release is in each language.

To ship a program, three questions need answers. What does the binary carry with it, and what does it expect to find on the target machine? Which machines is it built for, and from where? And how does a user get a particular version and know which one they have? Each language has settled answers. They differ enough that the same questions produce three different make dist targets.

ShippingCGoRust
A plain build linksthe system C library, dynamicallynothing, with cgo offthe system C library, dynamically
A static binary-static with musl, through zigthe default on Linuxthe musl target
Cross-compilingzig cc -targetGOOS= and GOARCH=rustup target add, then cargo zigbuild
A Linux server, stripped45 KB2.5 MB521 KB
Windowsno, the C code is Unix-onlyGOOS=windows, unchangedx86_64-pc-windows-gnu, unchanged
A versiona tag, and whatever you printa tag, read by go installversion in Cargo.toml, and a tag
How a user installs ita package from a distribution, or a tarballgo install, or one binarycargo install, or one binary

What a binary carries#

Chapter 2 showed what the three hello programs linked, and the picture has not changed. A C program calls the C library for everything and links it dynamically by default. The binary then holds a note that says "find libc.so.6 on this machine". On Linux, a Go program with CGO_ENABLED=0 makes its system calls itself and links nothing. On macOS it still links libSystem, as chapter 2 showed. A Rust program carries its own standard library and still calls the C library for the system. So it links libc.so.6 as the C one does.

The note is the problem. A C or Rust binary built on one Linux may ask for a version of the C library that another Linux does not have. The old answer was to build on the oldest system you support. The newer answer is to link statically against musl, a small C library written for this. The binary then carries its own copy and asks for nothing. Go made that the default. The other two can choose it.

📜Why static binaries came back

Shared libraries exist to save memory and disk. One copy of the C library serves every program on the machine, which mattered when memory was small and expensive. The cost was that a program depended on the exact library that was there. A program moved to another machine often met a different one. Containers reversed the trade. A container image holds one program, so there is nothing to share, and a binary that needs nothing can run in an empty image. Go chose to link nothing from the start, and the choice fit the container world that arrived a few years later.

C: zig as the cross-compiler#

Cross-compiling C has always been possible and has rarely been pleasant. The compiler must be built for the target, and so must the C library and its headers. Those pieces must be found and told about each other. The Zig project changed that. The project overview says that Zig "is also a C compiler" and carries a C library for 97 targets. As a result, "Zig builds for all supported targets independently of the host", with "no separate cross toolchain". One download, and zig cc -target names the machine:

Exercise 13.1CBuild for two kinds of Linux

Write the dist target. Loop over two targets, x86_64-linux-musl and aarch64-linux-musl. For each, run zig cc over every source file with the usual flags and three more. -target names the machine, -static puts the C library inside the binary, and -s strips the symbols. Name each output after its target under dist/.

ZIG ?= zig
TARGETS := x86_64-linux-musl aarch64-linux-musl

dist: $(SRC)
	mkdir -p dist
	# your loop here
Stuck?Show the full listing
# Cross-compile with zig cc, which carries a C compiler, a linker and the C libraries of
# every target in one download. -static with musl gives a binary that needs no libc file,
# and -s strips the symbols, which is most of the size.
ZIG ?= zig
TARGETS := x86_64-linux-musl aarch64-linux-musl

dist: $(SRC)
	mkdir -p dist
	for t in $(TARGETS); do \
	  $(ZIG) cc -target $$t -static -s -std=c17 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -O2 -o dist/kv-$$t $(SRC) || exit 1; \
	done
	ls -l dist
c/ch13/Makefile · region dist
$ make dist
-rwxr-xr-x  53952 kv-aarch64-linux-musl
-rwxr-xr-x  44824 kv-x86_64-linux-musl
$ file dist/kv-x86_64-linux-musl
ELF 64-bit LSB executable, x86-64, statically linked

Forty-five kilobytes, for a server with a hash table, an event loop and a log. The static musl library costs almost nothing, because only the functions kv calls are linked in. Before the -s flag the same file was 1.5 megabytes, and the difference was the symbol table and the debugging information. The binary was then run in a Linux container that had never seen a compiler:

$ docker run --rm -v "$PWD/dist":/dist redis:8 bash -c \
    'KV_PORT=6380 /dist/kv-x86_64-linux-musl & sleep 1; redis-cli -p 6380 PING'
PONG
$ docker run --rm --platform linux/arm64 -v "$PWD/dist":/dist redis:8 bash -c \
    'KV_PORT=6380 /dist/kv-aarch64-linux-musl & sleep 1; redis-cli -p 6380 PING'
PONG

The second command ran the arm64 binary under emulation on an Intel machine, which is slow and is enough to prove the build. Both answered, and both ran without a file from the system.

⚠️One source tree, one platform C

The C version of kv does not build for Windows. It uses poll and the Portable Operating System Interface (POSIX) socket calls, and chapter 8 explained that Windows wants a different loop. A C program that must run on both keeps two versions of the networking code behind one header. It chooses between them with #ifdef _WIN32. kv does not, and says so. The other two languages carry that #ifdef inside their standard libraries, which is the whole difference in the next two sections.

Go: two variables#

Go cross-compiles by design. The compiler is one program that contains the code generators for every target. The standard library is compiled for the target as part of the build. go help environment describes the two variables. GOOS is "The operating system for which to compile code", and GOARCH is "The architecture, or processor, for which to compile code". A third, CGO_ENABLED, says "Whether the cgo command is supported", and setting it to 0 keeps the C library out:

Exercise 13.2GoBuild for four targets

Write a dist target. Loop over the four targets below. Split each into the two variables, add .exe for Windows, and set CGO_ENABLED=0. Build with -ldflags="-s -w", which drops the symbol table and the debugging information.

TARGETS := linux/amd64 linux/arm64 windows/amd64 darwin/arm64

dist:
	mkdir -p dist
	# your loop here
Stuck?Show the full listing
# Cross-compile by setting two variables. CGO_ENABLED=0 keeps the binary free of the C
# library, so that it runs on any Linux of the right architecture, and in a scratch
# container. -s -w drop the symbol table and debug information, which halves the size.
TARGETS := linux/amd64 linux/arm64 windows/amd64 darwin/arm64

dist:
	mkdir -p dist
	for t in $(TARGETS); do \
	  os=$${t%/*}; arch=$${t#*/}; ext=""; [ $$os = windows ] && ext=.exe; \
	  CGO_ENABLED=0 GOOS=$$os GOARCH=$$arch go build -ldflags="-s -w" -o dist/kv-$$os-$$arch$$ext ./cmd/kv || exit 1; \
	done
	ls -l dist
go/ch13/Makefile · region dist
$ make dist
-rwxr-xr-x  2541858 kv-darwin-arm64
-rwxr-xr-x  2556064 kv-linux-amd64
-rwxr-xr-x  2490528 kv-linux-arm64
-rwxr-xr-x  2677760 kv-windows-amd64.exe

Four binaries for three operating systems, from one command, with nothing installed beyond Go. Each is about 2.5 megabytes, because each carries the runtime, and the Linux ones link nothing. They answered PING in the same containers as the C ones. The Windows one was not run, because this book has no Windows machine. The two Windows binaries in this chapter are the one case that was built and not tested. The file command at least agrees that it is "PE32+ executable (console) x86-64, for MS Windows".

✅Turn cgo off unless you need it Go

A Go program that imports a package which uses cgo links the C library and loses the freedom to run anywhere. The usual sources are the net package's name resolver and the os/user package, both of which have pure Go paths that CGO_ENABLED=0 selects. Build with it off, and turn it on only for a package that has no other way, which chapter 14 shows.

Rust: a target, a linker, and zig again#

Rust is in between. The compiler can produce code for any target, and rustup target add fetches the standard library for one. The rustup book is precise about what that gives you. "rustup target add only installs the Rust standard library for a given target", and "There are typically other tools necessary to cross-compile, particularly a linker". The linker, and the C library it links against, are the C problem again. The common answer is the same tool as in C. cargo-zigbuild describes itself as "Compile Cargo project with zig as linker for easier cross compiling":

Exercise 13.3RustA profile for shipping, and three targets

Add a dist profile to Cargo.toml that inherits from release, turns debugging information off, and strips the symbols. Then write a dist target in a Makefile. It runs cargo zigbuild --profile dist for each of the three targets below and copies each binary under dist/. Each target must first be added with rustup target add.

x86_64-unknown-linux-musl
aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu
x86_64-pc-windows-gnu

The profile comes first.

[profile.dist]
# your settings here
Stuck?Show the full listing
# Keep function names in the release build, so a profiler can show them. The code is
# as fast as without this; only the binary is larger.
[profile.release]
debug = 1

# The profile for shipping: the release settings, without the symbols.
[profile.dist]
inherits = "release"
debug = 0
strip = true
rust/ch13/Cargo.toml · region profile
# Cross-compile with cargo-zigbuild, which lets zig link for the target. Each target is
# a rustup component: rustup target add <triple>. The musl target is static. The dist
# profile in Cargo.toml strips the symbols that the release profile keeps for profiling.
TARGETS := x86_64-unknown-linux-musl aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu x86_64-pc-windows-gnu

dist:
	mkdir -p dist
	for t in $(TARGETS); do \
	  cargo zigbuild --profile dist -q --target $$t || exit 1; \
	  cp target/$$t/dist/kv dist/kv-$$t 2>/dev/null || cp target/$$t/dist/kv.exe dist/kv-$$t.exe; \
	done
	ls -l dist
rust/ch13/Makefile · region dist
$ cargo install --locked cargo-zigbuild
$ rustup target add x86_64-unknown-linux-musl aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu x86_64-pc-windows-gnu
$ make dist
-rwxr-xr-x   444416 kv-aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu
-rwxr-xr-x  1020416 kv-x86_64-pc-windows-gnu.exe
-rwxr-xr-x   521024 kv-x86_64-unknown-linux-musl

The musl binary is static and the gnu one is not. In the arm64 container, ldd lists three shared libraries for it, starting with libc.so.6. That is the note from the first section, and it ran because the container had them. The musl one ran because it needed nothing. The chapter 12 profile setting, debug = 1, is why a separate profile exists. It keeps symbols for the profiler, and a shipped binary does not want them. Stripping took the musl build from 7.3 megabytes to 521 kilobytes.

⚠️A warning from a linker you did not choose Rust

The gnu target prints a warning on every build. It reads warning: linker stderr: ignoring deprecated linker optimization setting "1", under the lint linker_messages. It comes from zig's linker, which is being driven with a flag meant for another, and the binary is fine. Cross-compiling in Rust means rustup, cargo and the linker cooperating. A message from one of them that the others do not understand is the normal cost of that.

Three sizes, three reasons#

                      Linux x86_64, stripped    what is inside
C, musl, static              44,824 bytes       kv, and the parts of musl it calls
Rust, musl, static          521,024 bytes       kv, the Rust standard library, and musl
Go, CGO_ENABLED=0         2,556,064 bytes       kv, and the Go runtime with its collector

The C binary is small because C has no runtime and the linker keeps only what is referenced. The Rust binary is ten times larger, because its standard library is inside and is itself written in Rust, with formatting, collections and the allocator. The Go binary is fifty times larger, because the garbage collector, the scheduler and the network poller are inside. They are there whether a program uses them or not. None of the three sizes is a problem for a server. All three are a fact a reader should know before choosing a language for a device with a megabyte of flash.

The three operating systems, in one place#

The platform boxes in earlier chapters each covered one difference. Set together, they are the list of what a program must know when it leaves its first machine.

The differenceCGoRust
Readiness and completionpoll and kqueue on macOS, epoll on Linux, completion ports on Windowsthe runtime hides itthe runtime hides it
Socketsone API from BSD, with Winsock variants on Windowshiddenhidden
Threads and lockspthreads, or Windows threadshiddenhidden, but the mutex differs underneath
The C librarylibc.so.6, libSystem, or the Windows C runtimenot usedused through a thin layer
Line endings and paths\n and /, or \r\n and \path/filepath and runtime.GOOSstd::path and cfg!(windows)
Where it shows in kvevery #include, and the event loopnowherethe mutex, in chapter 12

The last row is the honest one. The Go version of kv has no line that knows which operating system it runs on. The Rust version has none either. It still ran twice as fast on Linux as on macOS under contention, because the standard mutex is a different object on each. Hiding a platform is not the same as making it disappear.

Versions and releases#

A version is a promise about change, and the three ecosystems make the promise the same way, with three numbers. Semantic versioning, which Go modules and cargo both read, gives each number a meaning. Increase the "MAJOR version when you make incompatible API changes". Increase the "MINOR version when you add functionality in a backward compatible manner". Increase the "PATCH version when you make backward compatible bug fixes". kv's Rust crate has carried a version in Cargo.toml since chapter 2. Its Go module carries one as a Git tag, which go install reads. Its C version carries one wherever you choose to write it.

How a user gets the program differs more. A Go program with a public module path is installed with go install path@version, which fetches and builds it. Or it is shipped as the one binary this chapter made. A Rust program is installed with cargo install from crates.io or a Git URL, or shipped as a binary. A C program is shipped as source, and becomes a package when a distribution packages it, or as a binary built as above. Redis ships as source and as distribution packages, and that is the C norm. The one binary with nothing to install is the Go norm, and increasingly the Rust one.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
Cross-compilingOKStrongOKC needs zig and gets a small static binary. Go needs two variables, and Rust needs a target, a linker and zig.
Static linkingOKStrongOKC and Rust choose musl. Go links nothing by default.
Binary sizeStrongWeakOK45 KB, 2.5 MB and 521 KB for the same server.
WindowsWeakStrongStrongThe C code is Unix-only. Go and Rust build for Windows unchanged.
Knowing the platformWeakStrongOKC sees every difference and Go hides all of them. Rust hides most and leaks one through the mutex.
A releaseWeakStrongStrongC has no mechanism. Go and Rust read a tag or a version field and install from a path.
Next · Quality and shipping14Talking to C14 min

Talking to C#

Quality and shipping · Chapter 14· 14 min read· 127 lines of code

Every operating system, database driver and codec exposes a C interface, so Go and Rust both have a door to C. This chapter builds a small C library from kv and calls it from Go through cgo and from Rust through an extern block. Then it opens the door the other way and calls Rust from C. It measures what each crossing costs.

The hash and the pattern matcher from chapter 6 are the library. They are small, they take bytes and a length, and they allocate nothing, which makes them the easiest kind of C to call from anywhere. The same two functions are already written in Go and Rust, so each call can be checked against a native version, and timed against it.

Talking to CCGoRust
Declaring a C functiona headera C comment before import "C"an unsafe extern "C" block
Passing bytesa pointer and a size_tunsafe.Pointer and C.size_tas_ptr() and len()
Who compiles the C fileyou, with makethe go tool, from the package directorybuild.rs, with the cc crate
A trivial call costsnothingabout three times a Go callthe same as a C call
Being called from Cthe normal case//export#[unsafe(no_mangle)] pub unsafe extern "C"
What it does to the buildnothingneeds a C compiler and cgo onneeds a C compiler for the C part

The border is a C function#

A compiled function has a name, a place in memory, and a convention for where its arguments and its result go. That convention is the application binary interface (ABI), and the C one is the one every operating system defines and documents. A Go or Rust function has its own. A caller in either language cannot reach a C function without agreeing to the C convention for that one call. The Rust book puts it in one sentence. "The "C" part defines which application binary interface (ABI) the external function uses".

The border has a second property. Nothing on it is checked. The C compiler trusts the header, Go trusts the comment above import "C", and Rust trusts the extern block. A wrong type in any of them compiles and misbehaves at run time. That is why the library below is written to make the declarations hard to get wrong.

📜Why everything speaks C

Unix was written in C, and its system calls were given C wrappers. So the first interface every other language had to reach was a C one. Every operating system since has kept the habit, and so has every library that wants to be used from more than one language. Go got its door early. The Go blog described cgo in March 2011, a year before Go 1, with C as a "pseudo-package" for "C's name space". Rust had extern blocks before 1.0, and the 2024 edition made them say unsafe, because that is what they always were.

C: a library that is easy to call#

A C library is a header and an archive. The header is the only thing the other side reads, so its shape decides how pleasant the border is. The rules are short. Take a pointer and a length, never a C string, because Go and Rust bytes have no terminating zero. Return an int, not a bool, because every language agrees on what a C int is. Allocate nothing, so that no one has to ask which side frees it.

Exercise 14.1CWrite the header

Declare both functions. kvmatch_hash takes a pointer and a byte count and returns a uint64_t. kvmatch_glob takes a pattern and a key, each as a pointer and a length, and returns 1 for a match and 0 otherwise. Include the headers the types need.

#ifndef KVMATCH_H
#define KVMATCH_H
// your declarations here
#endif
Stuck?Show the full listing
/* FNV-1a over n bytes. */
uint64_t kvmatch_hash(const char *s, size_t n);

/* 1 when the key matches the Redis-style pattern (* and ? only), else 0. An int, not a
 * bool, because every language agrees on what a C int is. */
int kvmatch_glob(const char *pat, size_t plen, const char *key, size_t klen);
c/ch14/lib/kvmatch.h · region api

The bodies are the chapter 6 code with the lengths made explicit. The archive is made with ar, the tool every C library is built with:

# The hash and the matcher as a static library, for chapter 14. ar bundles object files
# into an archive that a linker can search; this is what every C library is.
lib: build/libkvmatch.a

build/libkvmatch.a: lib/kvmatch.c lib/kvmatch.h | build
	$(CC) $(CFLAGS) -c -o build/kvmatch.o lib/kvmatch.c
	ar rcs $@ build/kvmatch.o
c/ch14/Makefile · region lib
$ make lib
cc -std=c17 -Wall -Wextra -Werror -O2 -g -c -o build/kvmatch.o lib/kvmatch.c
ar rcs build/libkvmatch.a build/kvmatch.o
$ ls -l build/libkvmatch.a
-rw-r--r--  4264 build/libkvmatch.a

Four kilobytes, and the C side is done. C has no door to build, because C is the door. The rest of the chapter is the other two languages arriving at it.

Go: cgo#

cgo is part of the go tool. A Go file that has import "C" is a cgo file. The comment directly above that import is the preamble. The documentation says it "is used as a header when compiling the C parts of the package". Every C name in the preamble appears in Go under the C prefix, so C.size_t is the C type and C.kvmatch_glob is the function. Any .c file in the package directory is compiled with the system C compiler and linked in, which is why kvmatch.c sits beside cmatch.go.

Exercise 14.2GoWrap both functions

Write Glob(pat, key []byte) bool and Hash(b []byte) uint64 over the C functions. Get the pointer to a slice with unsafe.SliceData and convert it to *C.char through unsafe.Pointer. Decide what to pass for an empty slice, whose pointer may be nil.

/*
#include "kvmatch.h"
*/
import "C"

import "unsafe"

func Glob(pat, key []byte) bool {
	// your code here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
/*
#include "kvmatch.h"
*/
import "C"

import "unsafe"

// Glob reports whether key matches the pattern, by calling the C function. The slices
// are passed as a pointer and a length, and C must not keep the pointer: after the call
// returns, the garbage collector may free the bytes, or move them if they live on a stack.
func Glob(pat, key []byte) bool {
	return C.kvmatch_glob(cstr(pat), C.size_t(len(pat)), cstr(key), C.size_t(len(key))) != 0
}

// Hash returns the FNV-1a hash of b, from the C code.
func Hash(b []byte) uint64 {
	return uint64(C.kvmatch_hash(cstr(b), C.size_t(len(b))))
}

// cstr gives C a pointer to the first byte of a slice, or a pointer to a static byte for
// an empty slice, because unsafe.SliceData of an empty slice may be nil and C must not
// receive nil for a length of zero.
func cstr(b []byte) *C.char {
	if len(b) == 0 {
		return (*C.char)(unsafe.Pointer(&empty))
	}
	return (*C.char)(unsafe.Pointer(unsafe.SliceData(b)))
}

var empty byte
go/ch14/internal/cmatch/cmatch.go · region cgo

The pointer is the delicate part, because Go has a garbage collector and C does not know about it. The cgo documentation gives the rule. "All Go pointers passed to C must point to pinned Go memory". A pointer passed as a function argument has "the memory they point to implicitly pinned for the duration of the call". After that, "C code may not keep a copy of a Go pointer after the call returns". kvmatch_glob reads and returns, so it obeys without trying. A C library that keeps a buffer after the call needs runtime.Pinner, or a copy made with C.CBytes. The copy is freed with C.free, in the C way.

The test calls both matchers on the same inputs, and the benchmark times them:

// The price of the border: the same match, in Go and through cgo.
var sink bool

func BenchmarkGlobGo(b *testing.B) {
	pat, key := []byte("user:*"), []byte("user:1")
	for b.Loop() {
		sink = store.Glob(pat, key)
	}
}

func BenchmarkGlobCgo(b *testing.B) {
	pat, key := []byte("user:*"), []byte("user:1")
	for b.Loop() {
		sink = Glob(pat, key)
	}
}
go/ch14/internal/cmatch/cmatch_test.go · region bench
$ go test ./internal/cmatch/
ok  	kv/internal/cmatch	0.420s
$ go test -run xxx -bench Glob -benchmem ./internal/cmatch/
BenchmarkGlobGo-12     	49871018	        24.75 ns/op	       0 B/op	       0 allocs/op
BenchmarkGlobCgo-12    	15145387	        76.56 ns/op	       0 B/op	       0 allocs/op

The C function does the same work as the Go one and takes three times as long. The difference, about fifty nanoseconds, is the crossing. A goroutine runs on a small stack that can grow, and C expects a large fixed one. So the runtime switches stacks on the way in and back on the way out. It also tells the scheduler that this goroutine is away. Fifty nanoseconds is nothing for a call that then decodes a video frame. It is everything for a call made once per byte.

⚠️One cgo import changes the whole binary Go

A program that imports a cgo package cannot be built with CGO_ENABLED=0. The chapter 13 dist target still works here, because the kv server does not import cmatch. The moment it did, the build would need a C compiler for every target, and a cross-compiler for the foreign ones. The binary would then link the C library. The one-file freedom of chapter 13 is paid for with the rule that the program contains no C.

✅Keep the C in one package Go

Put every cgo call in one small package with a Go interface. Pass a pointer and a length, nothing that holds a Go pointer, and keep the calls coarse. One call that processes a buffer costs the same crossing as one call that processes a byte. Check the standard library first. The net resolver and os/user both have a pure Go path, and most of what a program needs from C already has one.

Rust: extern and unsafe#

Rust has no separate tool. An extern "C" block declares functions that live somewhere else, in Rust syntax with C types. The 2024 edition requires the word unsafe in front of it. The edition guide explains why. "It is the responsibility of the author of the extern block to ensure that the signatures are correct". A wrong one "may result in undefined behavior". The compiler checks that the Rust side of each call matches the declaration. It cannot check that the declaration matches the C.

Exercise 14.3RustDeclare, then wrap

Declare both functions in an unsafe extern "C" block. Use *const u8 for the pointers and usize for the lengths. The C int is std::ffi::c_int. Then write safe glob(pat: &[u8], key: &[u8]) -> bool and hash(bytes: &[u8]) -> u64 around them, each with a SAFETY comment that says why the call is sound.

unsafe extern "C" {
    // your declarations here
}

pub fn glob(pat: &[u8], key: &[u8]) -> bool {
    // your code here
}
Stuck?Show the full listing
// What the C header declares, in Rust spelling. The compiler takes these on trust: a
// wrong type here is undefined behaviour, which is why the block is unsafe to call.
unsafe extern "C" {
    fn kvmatch_hash(s: *const u8, n: usize) -> u64;
    fn kvmatch_glob(pat: *const u8, plen: usize, key: *const u8, klen: usize) -> c_int;
}
rust/ch14/src/cmatch.rs · region extern
/// Does the key match the pattern? The C function reads `plen` bytes from `pat` and
/// `klen` from `key` and keeps neither pointer, so a slice is safe to pass for the
/// duration of the call.
pub fn glob(pat: &[u8], key: &[u8]) -> bool {
    // SAFETY: both pointers come from live slices and the lengths are their lengths;
    // the C code does not write through them and does not keep them.
    unsafe { kvmatch_glob(pat.as_ptr(), pat.len(), key.as_ptr(), key.len()) != 0 }
}

/// FNV-1a, from the C code.
pub fn hash(bytes: &[u8]) -> u64 {
    // SAFETY: as above, a live slice and its length.
    unsafe { kvmatch_hash(bytes.as_ptr(), bytes.len()) }
}
rust/ch14/src/cmatch.rs · region safe

The wrapper is the whole idea of unsafe in Rust. The Rust book lists five things that only an unsafe block may do. Dereference a raw pointer. Call an unsafe function or method. Access or modify a mutable static variable. Implement an unsafe trait. Access fields of a union. A call into C is the second, and the block around it is a boundary, not a switch. Inside it, the borrow checker still runs and every other rule still holds. Only the five powers are added, and the SAFETY comment records the argument that they were used correctly. Everything that calls glob is ordinary safe Rust and cannot break the rule the comment states.

Someone must compile the C file. A build script does, before the crate compiles, using the cc crate, which finds the system compiler and links the result into the crate:

// A build script runs before the crate compiles. This one compiles the C library with
// the cc crate, which finds the system C compiler and links the result into the crate.
fn main() {
    cc::Build::new()
        .file("../../c/ch14/lib/kvmatch.c")
        .include("../../c/ch14/lib")
        .warnings(true)
        .compile("kvmatch");
    println!("cargo:rerun-if-changed=../../c/ch14/lib/kvmatch.c");
    println!("cargo:rerun-if-changed=../../c/ch14/lib/kvmatch.h");
}
rust/ch14/build.rs · region build
# The build script needs a C compiler driver; cc finds the system one.
[build-dependencies]
cc = "1"

# Besides the Rust library, produce a static library a C program can link: libkv.a.
[lib]
crate-type = ["lib", "staticlib"]
rust/ch14/Cargo.toml · region ffi
$ cargo test -q cmatch
running 3 tests
...
test result: ok. 3 passed; 0 failed

A crossing from Rust to C costs what a C to C call costs. Rust threads use the system stack, and the Rust ABI differs from the C one only in what the compiler is free to choose. So the compiler emits a plain call. The benchmark that the Go section needed does not have a result to show here, because there is no difference to measure.

⚠️miri stops at the border Rust

cargo miri test cannot run a test that calls into C. The tool interprets Rust and knows nothing about the compiled C code, so it reports an unsupported foreign function and stops. It can still check the Rust side of the border. The test for the exported function below passes under miri, because the raw pointers it builds slices from come from Rust. Test the C side with the C tools from chapter 11 and the Rust side with miri. No tool sees both at once.

The other way: C calls Rust#

Rust can also be the library. A function marked extern "C" uses the C convention, and #[unsafe(no_mangle)] keeps its name as written, so that a C linker can find it. The function below is the chapter 6 matcher behind a C signature:

/// The other direction: a Rust function with a C name and a C signature, for a C program
/// to call. It is the Rust matcher from chapter 6. `#[unsafe(no_mangle)]` keeps the name
/// as written, and `extern "C"` uses the C calling convention.
///
/// # Safety
/// `pat` must point to `plen` readable bytes and `key` to `klen`, as a C caller would
/// promise in a comment. Rust cannot check it, and a wrong length here is the same bug
/// it would be in C.
#[unsafe(no_mangle)]
