pub unsafe extern "C" fn kv_rust_glob(
    pat: *const u8,
    plen: usize,
    key: *const u8,
    klen: usize,
) -> c_int {
    if pat.is_null() || key.is_null() {
        return 0;
    }
    // SAFETY: the contract above, which the caller accepted by calling an unsafe function.
    let (pat, key) = unsafe {
        (
            std::slice::from_raw_parts(pat, plen),
            std::slice::from_raw_parts(key, klen),
        )
    };
    c_int::from(crate::store::glob(pat, key))
}
rust/ch14/src/cmatch.rs · region export

It is marked unsafe because calling it is unsafe. The caller promises the lengths, and nothing checks the promise. That is the contract every C function has always had, now written in the signature instead of in a comment. With crate-type = ["lib", "staticlib"] in Cargo.toml, cargo also produces libkv.a, and a C program links it like any archive:

/* The declaration a header would hold. Rust exported this name with #[unsafe(no_mangle)]. */
int kv_rust_glob(const char *pat, size_t plen, const char *key, size_t klen);

int main(void) {
    const char *pat = "user:*";
    const char *key = "user:1";
    int ok = kv_rust_glob(pat, strlen(pat), key, strlen(key));
    printf("kv_rust_glob(\"%s\", \"%s\") = %d\n", pat, key, ok);
    return ok ? 0 : 1;
}
c/ch14/from_rust/main.c · region call
# A C program calling Rust: build the Rust crate as a static library, then link it like
# any other. The flags after -lkv are what the Rust standard library needs from the
# system on each platform.
RUST := ../../rust/ch14
from-rust: build/from_rust
	./build/from_rust

build/from_rust: from_rust/main.c | build
	cargo build --release -q --manifest-path $(RUST)/Cargo.toml
	$(CC) $(CFLAGS) -o $@ from_rust/main.c -L$(RUST)/target/release -lkv $(RUST_LIBS)

# What the Rust standard library needs from the system. On Linux, set RUST_LIBS to the
# list that `cargo rustc --release -- --print native-static-libs` prints.
ifeq ($(shell uname),Darwin)
RUST_LIBS ?= -framework CoreFoundation
endif
c/ch14/Makefile · region from-rust
$ make from-rust
cargo build --release -q --manifest-path ../../rust/ch14/Cargo.toml
cc ... -o build/from_rust from_rust/main.c -L../../rust/ch14/target/release -lkv -framework CoreFoundation
./build/from_rust
kv_rust_glob("user:*", "user:1") = 1
$ ls -l ../../rust/ch14/target/release/libkv.a build/from_rust
-rw-r--r--  19629264 libkv.a
-rwxr-xr-x   1498400 build/from_rust

The archive is nineteen megabytes because it holds the whole crate, tokio included. The C program is 1.5 megabytes, because the linker kept only what one matcher needs. That is still the Rust runtime and its allocator. The -framework CoreFoundation at the end is what the Rust standard library asks of macOS. On Linux the list is different and longer, and cargo rustc --release -- --print native-static-libs prints it. The Rust side knows nothing of this. The C side, as always, must.

Go can be called from C as well, with a //export comment and -buildmode=c-archive. But the archive carries the Go runtime, which starts its scheduler and collector inside the host program. It is done, and it is rarely a comfortable fit. The Rust case has no runtime to start. That is why Rust libraries are the ones you find inside C programs, Python packages and browsers.

What a crossing costs#

The cost of the borderCGoRust
Per callnothingabout 50 nanoseconds and a stack switchnothing
Memory rulesyours, as everpin for the call, copy to keepthe borrow checker stops at the border
What the compiler checksthe header, against the bodythe preamble, against the Go sidethe extern block, against the Rust side
What nothing checksthe header, against the callerthe preamble, against the Cthe extern block, against the C
Tools that crossthe sanitizersthe race detector does notmiri does not
The buildmakea C compiler, per targeta C compiler, per target, for the C part

The row that matters most is the one that nothing checks. In all three languages the declaration is a promise. The chapter 6 tests, run on both sides of the border, are the only thing that keeps it. That is the C way of working, and at the border, every language works the C way.

Verdict##

TopicCGoRustWhy
Calling CStrongOKStrongGo pays a stack switch per call and loses static builds. C and Rust pay nothing per call.
Being called from CStrongWeakStrongA Go archive carries a runtime that must start. A Rust archive is a plain library.
Safety at the borderWeakOKOKC checks nothing anywhere. Go and Rust check their own side and trust the declaration.
Making the rules visibleWeakOKStrongC has comments and Go has a documented pointer rule. Rust puts unsafe in the signature.
Build cost of C codeStrongWeakOKGo needs cgo on for every target. Rust needs the cc crate and a compiler for the C part only.
Next · The verdict15The verdict17 min

The verdict#

The verdict · Chapter 15· 17 min read

Thirteen chapters each ended with a small scorecard. This chapter gathers every row into one table, reads what the pattern says about each language, and turns it into advice. When to choose C, when Go, when Rust, and when the right answer is a language this book did not cover.

One warning before the table. The rows score one project, a key-value server, built by one person who had to learn each language's way as they went. A different project moves rows. A device with sixty-four kilobytes of memory has no row for a garbage collector, because it cannot afford one. A web service has no row for binary size, because it does not care. Read the table as evidence about this kind of program, not as a ranking of languages.

The scorecard#

TopicCGoRustWhy
02  Toolchains and hello, three times
InstallingOKStrongStrongA C compiler is one system command away on macOS and Linux. Go and Rust are one download each, on every platform.
BuildingWeakStrongStrongC has no build tool, only a convention. Go and Rust each have one tool that every project uses.
Seeing what you madeStrongOKOKC shows every step from preprocessor to linker. Go and Rust hide the steps, which is usually what you want.
Binary sizeStrongWeakOK8 KB for C, 2.5 MB for Go because the runtime is inside, 415 KB for Rust.
Running elsewhereWeakStrongOKThe C binary needs the right C library on the target, the Go binary needs nothing, and Rust is in between.
03  Echo server
Opening a portWeakStrongStrongC needs four calls, a struct and an option. Go and Rust need one call that sets the option for you.
Closing it againWeakOKStrongC needs a cleanup label and Go needs a defer you must write, but Rust closes at the end of scope by itself.
The echo loopWeakStrongStrongC needs the short-write loop and the EINTR retry. Both standard libraries contain them.
A lost errorWeakWeakOKC and Go let a return value drop. Rust warns, and with the book's flags refuses.
Seeing what happensStrongOKOKThe C version shows every system call. The other two hide them, which is what you want on most days.
WindowsWeakStrongStrongThe C code needs Winsock changes. Go and Rust build unchanged.
What you must learn firstStrongOKWeakC is the calls themselves. Rust asks for Result, ?, ownership and try_clone before an echo works.
04  Speaking RESP
Reading bytesWeakStrongStrongC has stdio, which blocks, so kv grows its own buffer. Both standard libraries have one.
Holding the argumentsStrongOKOKThe C parser copies nothing. Go and Rust copy each argument once, which is simpler and almost never matters.
Saying "not RESP"OKOKStrongC and Go use a value by convention. Rust uses a kind the compiler knows about and a match the compiler checks.
Saying "the client left"OKWeakStrongGo reuses an error value for a non-error. Rust has Option for exactly this.
Passing an error upWeakWeakStrongC and Go repeat the check at every call. Rust writes one character.
Binary valuesStrongStrongOKC and Go treat all bytes alike. Rust makes you choose between String and Vec<u8>, and the wrong choice rejects real data.
05  GET and SET
A key is bytesOKStrongOKC carries a length beside every pointer, Go has []byte and string, and Rust makes you pick Vec<u8> because String rejects binary data.
Copying and owningWeakStrongStrongC copies and comments, Go lets the collector own everything, and Rust moves and checks.
Parsing a numberWeakOKOKC needs a careful parser to avoid undefined behaviour. The other two accept forms Redis rejects.
OverflowWeakOKStrongC must check before it adds and Go checks against a constant, while Rust has checked_add built in.
A missing caseWeakWeakStrongAn enum match in Rust must be complete. In C and Go, a new error kind can be forgotten at a call site.
Reading the codeOKStrongOKThe Go store is the shortest. The Rust signatures say more and take longer to read.
06  Your own hash table
Writing the tableWeakStrongOKC needs an owner comment on every pointer and three cleanup paths, Go is the shortest, and Rust argues about borrows in set.
Freeing memoryWeakStrongStrongC must free in three places that agree. Go and Rust free for you, in different ways.
Freeing on timeStrongWeakStrongC and Rust free on the line that removes an entry. Go frees at the next collection.
Testing itWeakStrongStrongC has no test runner in the box. go test and cargo test are built in.
Shipping unused codeWeakOKStrongC warns only for static functions and Go ignores unused functions, while Rust reports each one.
Needing to write oneStrongWeakWeakIn C the table is the store. In Go and Rust it is an exercise, because the map is built in.
07  Modules, packages and dependencies
Splitting codeWeakStrongOKC needs guards, prefixes and a Makefile that tracks headers, while Go needs a folder and Rust a mod line that is easy to forget.
Hiding a nameOKStrongStrongC has static, for functions only. Go and Rust hide by default or by a letter.
Adding a dependencyWeakOKStrongC has three competing ways and no default, while Go and Rust each have one command, and Rust also has one registry.
Having too manyStrongStrongWeakC and Go programs stay small by cost and by a wide standard library. Rust programs grow dozens of crates.
Keeping one styleOKStrongStrongclang-format needs a config everyone agrees on. gofmt and rustfmt need nothing.
Finding mistakesWeakOKStrongclang-tidy must be installed. go vet is built in. clippy is built in and has 800 checks.
08  Many clients
Serving many clientsWeakStrongOKC needs 200 lines of event loop and Go one keyword, while Rust needs Arc, Mutex and a clone per thread.
Using every coreWeakStrongStrongThe C loop is one thread by design. Go and Rust spread clients over every core.
A forgotten lockn/aWeakStrongGo compiles it and the detector may catch it. Rust refuses to compile it.
Memory for a clientStrongStrongOKC sizes two buffers and a goroutine is a few kilobytes, while a Rust thread is 2 MiB until tokio fixes that at the cost of a dependency.
Reading the codeWeakStrongOKThe C loop is subtle and the Go version is the chapter 7 code, while the tokio version is a second copy of the parser.
WindowsWeakStrongStrongpoll is POSIX. Both runtimes hide completion ports.
09  Expiry
A deadlineOKStrongStrongC uses zero to mean none, which the comments must say. Go's zero Time and Rust's Option say it in the type.
The clockOKStrongStrongC picks the clock by name, while Go carries both in one value and Rust gives each a type.
Lazy expiryStrongOKOKC deletes on find. Go and Rust must leave it to the sweep because of the read lock or the borrow.
The sweepStrongStrongWeakC has a cursor and Go a random sample. Rust's retain walks the whole map.
Testing timeWeakStrongStrongGo and Rust tests pass a clock in. The C store has no test runner yet.
Background workOKStrongStrongC has to make room in the loop. A goroutine or a task is one more of what the server already has.
10  Persistence
Writing the fileOKStrongStrongC encodes into a buffer and calls write. Go and Rust reuse the protocol writers and flush.
ReplayStrongStrongStrongAll three reuse the parser from chapter 4 unchanged, which is the point of a log in RESP.
A crash of the processStrongOKOKC writes straight through. Go and Rust needed a flush the book's test had to find.
The clock on diskOKStrongOKC converts between two clocks itself, while Go's time.Time holds both and Rust converts with a helper.
Log and store togetherStrongOKStrongOne C thread needs no lock, Go takes two locks, and Rust takes one around both.
fsync on a timerStrongStrongOKC has the loop and Go a goroutine. Rust reuses the sweeper, which is a compromise.
11  Testing and fuzzing
A test runnerWeakStrongStrongC has none, so you write one. Go and Rust have one in the box, with no setup.
FuzzingOKStrongOKlibFuzzer is one flag on Linux and a container on a Mac, Go has it built in, and Rust needs nightly and a second crate.
Memory errorsOKStrongStrongC finds them at run time with ASan or Valgrind. Go and Rust cannot write them in safe code.
Data racesWeakOKStrongC has a sanitizer that is rarely run and Go finds the races a test provokes, while Rust refuses them at compile time.
Undefined behaviourWeakStrongOKC has it everywhere and UBSan finds some. Go has almost none, a data race being the exception, and Rust confines it to unsafe, where Miri checks it.
Speed of the toolsStrongStrongWeakASan costs two times and the race detector two to twenty, while Miri costs nine thousand.
12  Benchmarking and profiling
A profiler in the boxOKStrongOKC and Rust use the operating system's profiler. Go carries its own, and it works the same everywhere.
Reading the profileStrongOKWeakOne C thread gives an honest list and Go needs the cum column, while the fifty Rust threads drown the list in waiting.
A fast serverStrongStrongOKC and Go reach 800,000 with pipelining. Rust needs a read lock to match on reads and stays behind on writes.
Why it was slowOKStrongWeakThe Go runtime hides lock handoff from the kernel. Rust's standard mutex is the platform's, and macOS makes it expensive.
The fixn/an/aOKRust's type system made the read lock a small, safe change, but the platform decides what it buys.
Honest numbersStrongStrongStrongredis-benchmark and the same command shape treat all three alike.
13  Shipping
Cross-compilingOKStrongOKC needs zig and gets a small static binary. Go needs two variables, and Rust needs a target, a linker and zig.
Static linkingOKStrongOKC and Rust choose musl. Go links nothing by default.
Binary sizeStrongWeakOK45 KB, 2.5 MB and 521 KB for the same server.
WindowsWeakStrongStrongThe C code is Unix-only. Go and Rust build for Windows unchanged.
Knowing the platformWeakStrongOKC sees every difference and Go hides all of them. Rust hides most and leaks one through the mutex.
A releaseWeakStrongStrongC has no mechanism. Go and Rust read a tag or a version field and install from a path.
14  Talking to C
Calling CStrongOKStrongGo pays a stack switch per call and loses static builds. C and Rust pay nothing per call.
Being called from CStrongWeakStrongA Go archive carries a runtime that must start. A Rust archive is a plain library.
Safety at the borderWeakOKOKC checks nothing anywhere. Go and Rust check their own side and trust the declaration.
Making the rules visibleWeakOKStrongC has comments and Go has a documented pointer rule. Rust puts unsafe in the signature.
Build cost of C codeStrongWeakOKGo needs cgo on for every target. Rust needs the cc crate and a compiler for the C part only.

Seventy-seven rows. C was strong in 24, ok in 18 and weak in 33. Go was strong in 46, ok in 19 and weak in 11. Rust was strong in 40, ok in 30 and weak in 7. Two rows for C and one for Go had no mark, because the topic did not apply. Those sums are less interesting than where the marks fall, because each language's weak rows belong to one or two families.

C: the price of seeing everything#

Almost every weak row for C is the same row. Memory that must be freed by the right code at the right time. An error that must be checked by the next line or is lost. A platform difference that the program must know about, because nothing stands between it and the operating system. Those are not three weaknesses. They are one property, that C hides nothing, seen from three sides.

The same property fills C's strong rows. The binary was forty-five kilobytes. The profile read like the source, because the source is what ran. The sanitizers found the use-after-free in chapter 11 in a second, because they watch the real memory operations. And at the border in chapter 14, C was not a language calling another. It was the border. Every other language's door is shaped like a C function, and that will not change in your career.

Choose C when the program must be small, or must run where no runtime fits. Choose it when the program is itself the thing other languages will call, or when the platform is the subject. An operating system, a C library, a device driver, a codec, an interpreter that other languages embed. Choose it knowing that the compiler checks types and nothing else. The chapter 11 tools are not optional in C. They are the part of the compiler that was left out.

Go: the server language#

Go's strong rows cluster around the task this book set. A server that talks to many clients, in one word, go, with a runtime that schedules them across every core. A test runner, a fuzzer, a race detector and a profiler in the box, with no decision to make about any of them. One command to build for any machine, and a binary that needs nothing. The chapter 8 server was the shortest of the four. It was also the one that used every core without a line of code written for the purpose.

Go's weak rows are the cost of the runtime and the cost of simplicity. The binary is 2.5 megabytes before the program starts. Calling C costs a stack switch and the one-file freedom. The language has no way to say that a function can fail except a second return value. Chapter 3 showed what happens when the caller ignores it. The forgotten lock in chapter 8 compiled and ran, and only the race detector, run on purpose, said anything. Go trusts the programmer to check every error and every lock. It gives better tools than C for finding the ones they missed, and no more protection at compile time.

Choose Go when the program is a network service, a command-line tool, or anything a team must read, build and deploy with the least effort. Choose it when the people who will maintain it are many, or new, or both. Go has one way to do most things, and the formatter enforces it. Do not choose it for a library that C or Python will embed, or for a device with a megabyte of flash. Do not choose it when correctness depends on the compiler catching a race.

Rust: the compiler as the reviewer#

Rust is strong in the rows that were weak for C. The hash table freed itself. The lost error could not be lost, because a Result that is dropped is a warning and a ? is a line. The forgotten lock did not compile. The data race did not compile. At the border in chapter 14, the unsafe block was a labelled boundary with a written argument, not a mood. Rust is what C would be if the compiler checked the comments.

Rust's weak rows are the cost of that checking, and one surprise. The compiler is slower. A clean release build of chapter 14 took 19 seconds in Rust, 8 in Go and 1 in C. The chapter 11 tools took the longest to run. The chapter 12 profile was the hardest to read, because the sampler counted fifty sleeping threads and buried the one line that mattered. The mutex in chapter 12 was a different object on macOS and on Linux, and the standard library did not say so. And in chapter 6, writing a hash table was harder than in Go, and harder than the rest of Rust had been. A structure that owns its entries and lends references to them is exactly what the borrow checker was built to question. That one is a real cost. Rust is at its most awkward when writing the data structures it is best at using.

Choose Rust when the program must be fast and must be correct. Choose it when the people who will run it are not the people who wrote it. A database engine, a proxy, a parser for untrusted input, a command-line tool that must never crash. A library that will be embedded in C, in Python or in a browser. Choose it knowing that the first month is spent arguing with the compiler, and that the argument is the point. Do not choose it for a program that will be rewritten next quarter, or for a team that has no time to learn it. A half-learned Rust is slower to write than Go, and a program full of unwrap() crashes when Go would have returned an error.

When the answer is none of them#

This book chose a server so that all three languages would be in their element. Many programs are not servers. A script that runs once, a report, a prototype that exists to be thrown away. A program whose whole cost is the time of the person writing it. For those, a language with a garbage collector, a large library and no build step is the right choice. That it runs many times slower than C does not appear in any row that matters. Python, JavaScript, Ruby and their relatives exist for this, and a systems programmer who refuses them is choosing the wrong tool out of pride.

The honest version of the question is not "which language is best". It is "what does this program need to be true, and which compiler will check it". C checks the types. Go checks the types and watches the running program. Rust checks the types, the lifetimes and the sharing. Each check has a price in time and in freedom, and the right language is the one whose price buys a guarantee the program needs.

What this book did not do#

A list, so that the scorecard is read with it. No chapter ran a Windows binary, so every Windows row rests on documentation and a file command. No chapter measured garbage collection pauses in Go, because kv never allocated enough to show them. The Rust chapters used tokio and did not compare it with another runtime. The C chapters used poll and described kqueue, epoll and completion ports without running them. No chapter covered Go generics, Rust traits beyond what the server needed, or C macros beyond the test runner. Each of those would change a row or add one.

Where to go from here#

kv is small enough to understand and large enough to extend. The next features, in rough order of difficulty, are more data types, replication to a second server, and a cluster that shares keys by hash. Each is a chapter this book did not write. Each is a chance to learn whether the scorecard above survives contact with a harder problem. Pick the language whose weak rows worried you least and build the feature. Then build it again in the language whose weak rows worried you most. The second build is where the learning is.

Appendix A: Rosetta cheat card#

Appendices · Appendix A· 6 min read· 209 lines of code

The constructs kv used most, side by side. Each block shows the same small thing in C, Go and Rust, in the form the chapters settled on. Use the Follow buttons at the top of the page to put your language first.

A value and a type#

C
int n = 42;
const char *name = "kv";
size_t len = strlen(name);
uint64_t big = 1ULL << 40;
bool ok = n > 0;           /* stdbool.h */
Go
n := 42
name := "kv"
length := len(name)
var big uint64 = 1 << 40
ok := n > 0
Rust
let n = 42;
let name = "kv";
let len = name.len();
let big: u64 = 1 << 40;
let ok = n > 0;
let mut count = 0; // mutable

A function that can fail#

C
/* 0 on success, -1 and errno on failure */
int kv_open(const char *path, int *fd_out) {
    int fd = open(path, O_RDONLY);
    if (fd < 0) return -1;
    *fd_out = fd;
    return 0;
}
int fd;
if (kv_open("kv.aof", &fd) < 0) {
    perror("open");
}
Go
func open(path string) (*os.File, error) {
	f, err := os.Open(path)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("open: %w", err)
	}
	return f, nil
}
f, err := open("kv.aof")
if err != nil {
	log.Fatal(err)
}
Rust
fn open(path: &str) -> io::Result<File> {
    let f = File::open(path)?;
    Ok(f)
}
let f = match open("kv.aof") {
    Ok(f) => f,
    Err(e) => {
        eprintln!("open: {e}");
        std::process::exit(1);
    }
};

A struct with a function on it#

C
typedef struct {
    char *data;
    size_t len;
} kv_buf;

void kv_buf_clear(kv_buf *b) {
    b->len = 0;
}
kv_buf b = {0};
kv_buf_clear(&b);
Go
type Buf struct {
	data []byte
}

func (b *Buf) Clear() {
	b.data = b.data[:0]
}

var b Buf
b.Clear()
Rust
struct Buf {
    data: Vec<u8>,
}

impl Buf {
    fn clear(&mut self) {
        self.data.clear();
    }
}
let mut b = Buf { data: Vec::new() };
b.clear();

Bytes and strings#

C
/* a string is bytes with a zero at the end */
char s[] = "PING";
size_t n = strlen(s);        /* 4 */
if (memcmp(s, "PING", 4) == 0) { }
char copy[16];
snprintf(copy, sizeof copy, "%s", s);
Go
s := "PING"            // immutable
b := []byte(s)         // a copy, mutable
if string(b) == "PING" { }
if bytes.Equal(b, []byte("PING")) { }
n := len(b)            // 4, in bytes
Rust
let s = "PING";                 // &str, UTF-8
let b: &[u8] = s.as_bytes();    // no copy
let owned: Vec<u8> = b.to_vec(); // a copy
if b == b"PING" { }
let n = b.len();                // 4

A growable array#

C
size_t cap = 4, len = 0;
int *xs = malloc(cap * sizeof *xs);
if (len == cap) {
    int *p = realloc(xs, 2 * cap * sizeof *xs);
    if (p == NULL) { free(xs); return -1; }
    xs = p;
    cap *= 2;
}
xs[len++] = 7;
free(xs);
Go
xs := make([]int, 0, 4)
xs = append(xs, 7)
for i, x := range xs {
	fmt.Println(i, x)
}
// nothing to free
Rust
let mut xs = Vec::with_capacity(4);
xs.push(7);
for (i, x) in xs.iter().enumerate() {
    println!("{i} {x}");
}
// freed when xs goes out of scope

A map#

C
/* no map in the standard library:
   write one (chapter 6) or use a library */
kv_store_set(&s, key, klen, val, vlen);
kv_entry *e = kv_store_find(&s, key, klen, now);
if (e == NULL) { /* missing */ }
kv_store_del(&s, key, klen);
Go
m := map[string][]byte{}
m["k"] = []byte("v")
if v, ok := m["k"]; ok { use(v) }
delete(m, "k")
Rust
let mut m: HashMap<Vec<u8>, Vec<u8>> = HashMap::new();
m.insert(b"k".to_vec(), b"v".to_vec());
match m.get(b"k".as_slice()) {
    Some(v) => { /* found */ }
    None => { /* missing */ }
}
m.remove(b"k".as_slice());

Heap memory, and who frees it#

C
kv_entry *e = malloc(sizeof *e);
if (e == NULL) return -1;
e->key = malloc(klen);
/* ... */
free(e->key);     /* in the same order */
free(e);          /* you decided, you free */
Go
e := &entry{key: make([]byte, klen)}
// the collector frees e when nothing
// points to it any more
Rust
let e = Box::new(Entry { key: vec![0; klen] });
// freed at the end of the scope that owns it
let moved = e;   // ownership moves, e is gone
drop(moved);     // or free it early

A thread and a shared counter#

C
static int count;
static pthread_mutex_t mu = PTHREAD_MUTEX_INITIALIZER;
void *work(void *arg) {
    pthread_mutex_lock(&mu);
    count++;
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&mu);
    return NULL;
}
pthread_t t;
pthread_create(&t, NULL, work, NULL);
pthread_join(t, NULL);
Go
var mu sync.Mutex
var count int
var wg sync.WaitGroup
wg.Go(func() {
	mu.Lock()
	count++
	mu.Unlock()
})
wg.Wait()
Rust
let count = Arc::new(Mutex::new(0));
let c = Arc::clone(&count);
let t = thread::spawn(move || {
    *c.lock().unwrap() += 1;
    // unlocked here, when the guard drops
});
t.join().unwrap();

Reading a whole file#

C
FILE *f = fopen("kv.aof", "rb");
if (f == NULL) return -1;
char buf[4096];
size_t n;
while ((n = fread(buf, 1, sizeof buf, f)) > 0) {
    /* use buf[0..n] */
}
fclose(f);
Go
data, err := os.ReadFile("kv.aof")
if err != nil {
	return err
}
// data is the whole file
Rust
let data = std::fs::read("kv.aof")?;
// data: Vec<u8>, the whole file

Printing#

C
printf("%s=%zu (%d)\n", key, len, n);
fprintf(stderr, "kv: %s\n", strerror(errno));
Go
fmt.Printf("%s=%d (%d)\n", key, len(key), n)
fmt.Fprintf(os.Stderr, "kv: %v\n", err)
log.Printf("listening on %s", addr)
Rust
println!("{key}={} ({n})", key.len());
eprintln!("kv: {err}");
println!("{:?}", bytes);   // debug form

Build, test, check#

C, with make
make                        # build
make test                   # your runner
make SANITIZE=-fsanitize=address,undefined
clang-format -i src/*.c
make dist                   # zig cc, per target
Go
go build ./cmd/kv
go test ./...
go test -race ./...
go vet ./... && gofmt -l .
GOOS=linux GOARCH=arm64 go build ./cmd/kv
Rust, with cargo
cargo build --release
cargo test
cargo clippy -- -D warnings
cargo fmt
cargo zigbuild --target x86_64-unknown-linux-musl

Appendix B: Glossary#

Appendices · Appendix B· 3 min read

The terms the book uses, each with the section that explains it. The first use of a term in each chapter links here.

TermMeaningSee
ABIApplication binary interface. The convention for how a compiled function receives its arguments and returns its result.The border is a C function
AOFAppend-only file. kv's log of every write, replayed at start to rebuild the store.C: a file descriptor, a write, and an fsync on a timer
AddressSanitizerA compiler mode that watches every memory access and reports a use after free or an access outside a block.C: a runner you write, and a sanitizer that watches
asyncRust code that pauses at an await point instead of blocking a thread, run by a runtime such as tokio.Rust again: tasks on tokio
borrow checkerThe part of the Rust compiler that tracks who may read or change a value and for how long.Rust: a value has one owner
bulk stringThe RESP form of a byte string. A dollar sign, a length, a line break, the bytes, a line break.C: a buffer, a parser and three answers
cgoThe part of the go tool that lets Go code call C and be called from it.Go: cgo
chainingA hash table design where each bucket holds a linked list of the entries that hash to it.C: an array of chains, and every pointer accounted for
clippyThe Rust linter. It reports patterns that compile but are slower, longer or riskier than they need to be.One style, enforced by a tool
crateA Rust package. A library crate, a binary crate, or both, described by Cargo.toml.Rust: a module is a file you must announce
cross-compilingBuilding on one machine a binary that runs on another kind of machine.C: zig as the cross-compiler
data raceTwo threads touching the same memory at the same time, one of them writing, with no lock between them.Go: one word, and one lock
deadlineThe moment a key expires, stored with the key as a reading of the monotonic clock.C: a deadline on the entry, and a sweep with a cursor
dynamic linkingLinking where the binary holds only the name of a library and finds it on the machine at start.What a binary carries
epollThe Linux call that waits for many sockets at once. kqueue on macOS and completion ports on Windows do the same job.C: one thread, and poll to say who is ready
event loopOne thread that asks which sockets are ready and serves each in turn.C: one thread, and poll to say who is ready
extern blockThe Rust declaration of functions that live in another language, marked unsafe since the 2024 edition.Rust: extern and unsafe
file descriptorThe small integer a Unix system gives a program for an open file or socket.C: four calls, and a way to clean up
fsyncThe system call that makes the operating system write a file's bytes to the disk before it returns.What a crash takes
fuzzingTesting with generated inputs that a tool mutates toward the paths it has not yet run.Fuzzing, three ways, one engine
garbage collectorThe part of the Go runtime that finds memory nothing points to and frees it.Go: the same table, with nothing to free
goroutineA function the Go runtime runs on its own small stack, a few kilobytes, scheduled over the real threads.Go: one word, and one lock
headerA C file of declarations, included by every file that uses them.C: headers, static, and a Makefile that knows about both
header guardThe #ifndef at the top of a header that stops it being read twice.C: headers, static, and a Makefile that knows about both
hash tableAn array of buckets, with a hash of the key choosing the bucket. The store under GET and SET.C: an array of chains, and every pointer accounted for
lazy expiryChecking a key's deadline when the key is read, and deleting it then.C: a deadline on the entry, and a sweep with a cursor
libcThe C standard library, which also wraps the system calls. glibc and musl are two of them.What a binary carries
linkerThe tool that joins compiled files and libraries into one binary.What a binary is
miriAn interpreter for Rust that checks unsafe code for undefined behaviour while it runs.Rust: the compiler already ran, and the tools check the rest
moduleIn Go, a set of packages with a version. In Rust, a file or block of code with its own names.Go: a folder is a package, and a capital letter is an export
monotonic clockA clock that only goes forward, unaffected by the user changing the time.C: a deadline on the entry, and a sweep with a cursor
muslA small C library for Linux that is designed to be linked statically.What a binary carries
mutexA lock. One thread holds it at a time, and the others wait.Go: one word, and one lock
ownershipThe Rust rule that every value has one owner, and is freed when the owner goes away.Rust: a value has one owner
packageIn Go, a folder of files that share a name. In Rust, a crate with a Cargo.toml.Go: a folder is a package, and a capital letter is an export
pipeliningSending many commands before reading any reply, so that the network wait is paid once.Taking a number
pollThe portable call that waits until one of a set of sockets is ready.C: one thread, and poll to say who is ready
profileA record of where a running program spent its time, taken by sampling the stack.C: a sampler and a short answer
race detectorThe Go tool, go test -race, that watches memory access and reports a data race it sees.Go: the runner is in the box, and so is the fuzzer
regionA marked part of a listing that the book pulls in by name, so the text and the code cannot drift.The code
RESPThe Redis serialization protocol. Arrays of bulk strings in, and simple strings, errors, integers, bulk strings or nil out.C: a buffer, a parser and three answers
ResultThe Rust type that is either a value or an error, and must be looked at.Rust: a Result at every step
sanitizerA compiler mode that adds checks for memory errors, undefined behaviour or data races.C: a runner you write, and a sanitizer that watches
semantic versioningThree numbers, major, minor and patch, that say what kind of change a release holds.Versions and releases
static linkingLinking where the binary carries its libraries inside and needs nothing from the machine.What a binary carries
sweepThe background pass that deletes expired keys nobody has asked for.C: a deadline on the entry, and a sweep with a cursor
tokioThe Rust async runtime that the book's second Rust server runs on.Rust again: tasks on tokio
undefined behaviourA C or unsafe Rust operation the standard does not define, after which the compiler may do anything.C: a runner you write, and a sanitizer that watches
unsafeThe Rust keyword that marks a block where the five extra powers are allowed, and the author takes responsibility.Rust: extern and unsafe
zigA toolchain that includes a C compiler and the C libraries of many targets, used here to cross-compile.C: zig as the cross-compiler

End of the book
